on data
{y}^{\dagger}_{n+1}
to obtain
\mu_{n+1}.

In particular
\mathcal{T}_{n}^{S}(\mathfrak{\pi}_{n+1}):=(T^{S}_{n})^{\sharp}\mathfrak{\pi}_{n+1}

has property
\mathcal{T}_{n}^{S}(\mathfrak{\pi}_{n+1})=\mathcal{B}_{n}(\mathfrak{\pi}_{n+1}).

Note that the implied map from
\mu_{n}
to
\mu_{n+1}
is a nonlinear Markov process, because of the dependence of
T^{S}_{n}
on
\mathfrak{\pi}_{n+1}
and hence on
\mu_{n}.
Furthermore, we have that
\textit{Law}\,(v_{\ell})=\mu_{\ell}
for all
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
. The important
takeaway is that (
44
) defines a sample-path picture of the evolution
of the filtering distribution: it provides a map in state space with law governed by
the filter. We reemphasize that such a sample-path representation is not uniquely defined.

Consider now a different approach to transport for filtering: we seek a transport map that acts on the state space only to effect Bayes Theorem, i.e. mapping prior
\widehat{\mu}_{n+1}
to posterior
\mu_{n+1}
. To this end we consider the dynamical system

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{n})+\xi_{n},

(46a)

\displaystyle v_{n+1}

\displaystyle=T^{D}(\widehat{v}_{n+1};\widehat{\mu}_{n+1},{y}^{\dagger}_{n+1}),

(46b)

again assumed to hold for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
. The first equation maps
v_{n}\sim\mu_{n}
to
\widehat{v}_{n+1}\sim\widehat{\mu}_{n+1}
, thus giving a sample path realization
of (
23
a). In the second equation, the map
T^{D}_{n}(\cdot):=T^{D}(\cdot;\widehat{\mu}_{n+1},{y}^{\dagger}_{n+1})
is chosen so that, if

\widehat{v}_{n+1}\sim\widehat{\mu}_{n+1}
then

v_{n+1}\sim\mu_{n+1}
, thus giving a sample path realization
of (
23
b).
Thus we have another instance of the sample path perspective, and
(
28
,
30
) in particular.

Equation (
46
) constitutes another mean field stochastic dynamical system: stochastic because of the noise in (
46
a); mean field because the map
T^{D}
in (
46
b) depends on the law of
\widehat{v}_{n+1}
itself,
and hence on
\mu_{n}.
The symbol
D
distinguishes map
T^{D}
from map
T^{S}
: map
T^{D}
is
deterministic
in the sense that it does not require stochastic data
\widehat{y}_{n+1}
, in contrast to
T^{S}.
Therefore, we again have that
\textit{Law}\,(v_{\ell})=\mu_{\ell}
for all
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, where the
probability measure
\mu_{n}
evolves according to

\displaystyle\widehat{\mu}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}\mu_{n},

(47a)

\displaystyle\mu_{n+1}

\displaystyle=(T^{D}_{n})^{\sharp}\widehat{\mu}_{n+1}.

(47b)

This is simply a restatement of (
23
), noting
that
(T^{D}_{n})^{\sharp}
has been chosen so that the pushforward corresponds
to the application of Bayes Theorem to incorporate data

{y}^{\dagger}_{n+1}.

In particular
\mathcal{T}_{n}^{D}(\widehat{\mu}_{n+1}):=(T^{D}_{n})^{\sharp}\widehat{\mu}_{n+1}

has property
\mathcal{T}_{n}^{D}(\widehat{\mu}_{n+1})=\mathcal{L}_{n}(\widehat{\mu}_{n+1}).

The evolution (
47
) is another nonlinear Markov process, now because of the dependence of
T^{D}_{n}
on
\widehat{\mu}_{n+1}.

Again, the underlying sample-path representation (
46
) is not uniquely defined.

The two transport maps
T^{S}
and
T^{D}
introduce an important conceptual
approach to algorithms for filtering, but determining the maps can be
as hard, or harder, than solving the filtering problem itself. Thus,
in the next two subsections, we turn to relaxations of the perfect
transport effected by
T^{S}
and
T^{D}
. We instead seek
mean field maps which match only first and second order moment information; this relaxation allows for approximate transport maps with simple affine (in
senses to be made precise) forms. The perspective of matching first and second order moments naturally suggests working with Gaussians and hence we also relate the approximate transport to the Gaussian projected filter.

2.5.3
Second Order Transport – Motivation

To motivate the more general ideas behind second order transport,
we first study an explicit example. It is well known how to transform samples from a unit centered Gaussian random variable on
\mathbb{R}
into samples from a Gaussian random variable with mean
m\not=0
and variance
\sigma\not=1
by a simple scaling and shifting operation. An appropriate generalization of such a procedure suggests consideration of the map

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{n})+\xi_{n},

(48a)

\displaystyle\widehat{y}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=h(\widehat{v}_{n+1})+\eta_{n+1},

(48b)

\displaystyle v_{n+1}

\displaystyle=m_{n+1}+C_{n+1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\widehat{v}_{n+1}-\mathbb{E}\widehat{v}_{n+1}),

(48c)

where
m_{n+1}
,
\widehat{C}_{n+1}
, and
C_{n+1}
are determined by
(
31
), (
32
) and
(
40
), using (
48
a) and
(
48
b). Here
\{{y}^{\dagger}_{n}\}
arises again from a fixed realization of (
1
).
This is a specific instance of the sample path perspective, and
(
28
,
30
) in particular. In this case the
sample path evolution of
v_{n}
has law which
only approximates the true filtering law.

The map
v_{n}\mapsto v_{n+1}
defined by (
48
)
is a mean field map because of the dependence of
m_{n+1},C_{n+1}
and

\widehat{C}_{n+1}
on
\mathcal{Q}\,\textit{Law}\,(v_{n})
. It may be viewed as an approximation to
(
44
) which is exact when
\mathcal{Q}\,\textit{Law}\,(v_{n})
is Gaussian. To demonstrate
exactness on Gaussians it suffices to show that we obtain the
desired mean and covariance after application of the map. It is clear from (
48
c)
that
\mathbb{E}v_{n+1}=m_{n+1}
and also that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl((v_{n+1}-m_{n+1})\otimes(v_{n+1}-m_{n+1})\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad=C_{n+1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{E}\bigl((v_{n+1}-m_{n+1})\otimes(v_{n+1}-m_{n+1})\bigr)\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}C_{n+1}^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad=C_{n+1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\widehat{C}_{n+1}\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}C_{n+1}^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad=C_{n+1}.

It is important to recognize that, in general,
v_{n+1}
defined by (
48
c) will not be Gaussian distributed since
\widehat{v}_{n+1}
, defined by (
48
a), will not be Gaussian either. However, although (
48
) does not provide a closed iteration on Gaussians, the map from
\widehat{v}_{n+1}
to
v_{n+1}
agrees with the same step in the Gaussian projected filter, at the level of first and second order moments. But, because it is not
a closed iteration on
\mathfrak{G}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}),
it is clearly not the same as the Gaussian projected filter.

Whilst the mean field map from (
48
) is a relatively transparent way to achieve the goal of matching first and second order moments in the transport
step there is an uncountable set of ways of achieving this objective; the next two subsections demonstrate this, identifying all mean field maps effecting approximate transport from within two specific classes of affine transformations. We will then highlight a small subset that have been used in practice, each of which is useful in certain specific contexts.

2.5.4
Second Order Transport – Stochastic Case

The first class of approximate filters determined by mean field maps
have the sample path form

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{n})+\xi_{n},

(49a)

\displaystyle\widehat{y}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=h(\widehat{v}_{n+1})+\eta_{n+1},

(49b)

\displaystyle v_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{T}^{S}(\widehat{v}_{n+1},\widehat{y}_{n+1};\mathfrak{\pi}_{n+1},{y}^{\dagger}_{n+1}),

(49c)

where
\{{y}^{\dagger}_{n}\}
arises from a fixed realization of (
1
). We identify

\widetilde{T}^{S}_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{y}}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}
, where

\widetilde{T}^{S}_{n}(\cdot):=\widetilde{T}^{S}(\cdot\,;\mathfrak{\pi}_{n+1},{y}^{\dagger}_{n+1})
approximates
an exact transport map
T^{S}_{n}(\cdot)=T^{S}(\cdot\,;\mathfrak{\pi}_{n+1},{y}^{\dagger}_{n+1})
, defined
previously, by matching the first and second order moments.

We next introduce
7
7

7

We temporarily drop explicit notational dependence on
n+1
in
\mathfrak{\pi}
and in
{y}^{\dagger}
. This should not cause confusion as the approximate map we derive is concerned simply with finding a push forward which approximates conditioning of
\textit{Law}\,(\widehat{v}_{n+1},\widehat{y}_{n+1})
on
\widehat{y}_{n+1}={y}^{\dagger}
.

\mathfrak{\pi}=\textit{Law}\,(\widehat{v}_{n+1},\widehat{y}_{n+1})

and assume that the exact and approximate transport maps satisfy, respectively,

\displaystyle(T^{S}_{n})^{\sharp}\mathfrak{\pi}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{B}_{n}(\mathfrak{\pi}),

(50a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}\bigl((\widetilde{T}^{S}_{n})^{\sharp}\mathfrak{\pi}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{B}_{n}(\mathcal{G}\mathfrak{\pi}),

(50b)

for all measures
\mathfrak{\pi}
on the product space
\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{y}}.
Of course
T^{S}_{n}
and
\widetilde{T}^{S}_{n}
will depend on
\mathfrak{\pi}
and then pushforward is to be interpreted as in (
42
), (
43
). It is intuitive that (
50
b) enforces map
\widetilde{T}^{S}_{n}
to satisfy (
50
a) when
\mathfrak{\pi}
is Gaussian; we prove this in Lemma
2.12
below.

Perfect transport corresponds to asking that, for all measures
\mathfrak{\pi}
on the space
\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{y}}
, (
50
a) holds; second order transport relaxes this and asks only that
(
50
b) holds. Whilst achieving (
50
a) may be harder than solving the filtering problem directly, we will show that achieving (
50
b) is straightforward and leads to computationally tractable methods. This gain in tractability comes at the price of only achieving (
50
b) in place of (
50
a);
however it is intuitive that this price will not be high for settings
in which the filtering distribution, and the predictive
distribution on state and data, is not too far from Gaussian; we flesh out this idea in Subsection
2.5.6
below.

Working to satisfy (
50
b) allows us to find tractable approximate second order transport maps by seeking
\widetilde{T}^{S}
in the form

\widetilde{T}^{S}(\widehat{v}_{n+1},\widehat{y}_{n+1};\mathfrak{\pi},{y}^{\dagger}):=A\widehat{v}_{n+1}+B\widehat{y}_{n+1}+a.

(51)

We allow the matrices/vectors
A,B,a
to depend on
(\mathfrak{\pi},{y}^{\dagger})
; however, they are assumed to be independent of
(\widehat{v}_{n+1},\widehat{y}_{n+1})
. Making this assumption ensures that the transport map is affine with respect to the realization of
(\widehat{v}_{n+1},\widehat{y}_{n+1})
(but not their law). This in turn leads to tractable computations to determine
A,B,a
on the basis of matching second order moments of perfect transport. In addition to computational tractability, the affine form of the transport map
\widetilde{T}^{S}
is motivated by the following which shows that the approximate transport is perfect when applied to Gaussian source:

Lemma 2.12
.

Consider approximate transport map
\widetilde{T}^{S}_{n}=\widetilde{T}^{S}(\widehat{v}_{n+1},\widehat{y}_{n+1};\mathfrak{\pi},{y}^{\dagger})
with the form (
51
),
assumed to satisfy (
50
b). Then
\widetilde{T}^{S}
depends on
\mathfrak{\pi}
only through
\mathcal{G}\mathfrak{\pi}
. Furthermore,

(\widetilde{T}^{S}_{n})^{\sharp}(\mathcal{G}\mathfrak{\pi})=\mathcal{B}_{n}(\mathcal{G}\mathfrak{\pi});

thus, if
\mathfrak{\pi}
is Gaussian, equation (
50
b)
implies (
50
a).

\Diamond

Proof 2.13
.

We first note that (
50
b) is equivalent to insisting that

\mathcal{G}\bigl((\widetilde{T}^{S}_{n})^{\sharp}\mathcal{G}\mathfrak{\pi}\bigr)=\mathcal{B}_{n}(\mathcal{G}\mathfrak{\pi})

(52)

for all measures
\mathfrak{\pi};
this follows because, noting the
definition (
42
) and consequence (
43
),
the first and second moments of
(\widetilde{T}^{S}_{n})^{\sharp}\mathcal{G}\mathfrak{\pi}
and
(\widetilde{T}^{S}_{n})^{\sharp}\mathfrak{\pi}

agree, because of the affine form (
51
)
assumed for
\widetilde{T}^{S}_{n}
. Recall that
(\widetilde{T}^{S}_{n})
depends on
(\pi,{y}^{\dagger})=\bigl(\textit{Law}\,(\widehat{v}_{n+1},\widehat{y}_{n+1}),{y}^{\dagger}_{n+1}\bigr)
.
From the identity (
52
),
it is clear that
\widetilde{T}^{S}_{n}
only depends on
\mathfrak{\pi}

through
\mathcal{G}\mathfrak{\pi}
because changing
\mathfrak{\pi}\to\mathcal{G}\mathfrak{\pi}
leaves the identity invariant,
as
\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}.

Now note that, because Gaussians are preserved under affine transformations,

\bigl(\widetilde{T}^{S}(\widehat{v}_{n+1},\widehat{y}_{n+1};\mathfrak{\pi},{y}^{\dagger})\bigr)^{\sharp}(\mathcal{G}\mathfrak{\pi})=\mathcal{G}\Bigl(\bigl(\widetilde{T}^{S}(\widehat{v}_{n+1},\widehat{y}_{n+1};\mathfrak{\pi},{y}^{\dagger})\bigr)^{\sharp}\mathfrak{\pi}\Bigr),

or, in compact notational form,

(\widetilde{T}^{S}_{n})^{\sharp}(\mathcal{G}\mathfrak{\pi})=\mathcal{G}\bigl((\widetilde{T}^{S}_{n})^{\sharp}\mathfrak{\pi}\bigr).

(53)

The desired display in the lemma is then immediate from (
50
b).

An affine transport map of the form (
51
), when combined with particle approximations, leads to practical implementable algorithms and achieves (
50
b) by ensuring that
(\widetilde{T}^{S}_{n})^{\sharp}\mathfrak{\pi}
has first and second moments which agree with those of the Gaussian projected filter; these are given by equations (
31
), (
32
) and (
40
) when
\mathfrak{\pi}
is the law of
(\widehat{v}_{n+1},\widehat{y}_{n+1})
.

In Appendix C, Subsection
9.1
, we identify the (uncountable) set of all possible
A,B,a
which achieve the desired matching of first and
second order moments. Here we focus on the two specific choices given in
Example
9.6
from that Appendix. The first that we highlight corresponds to the choice

\widetilde{T}^{S}(\widehat{v}_{n+1},\widehat{y}_{n+1};\widetilde{\mathfrak{\pi}}_{n+1},{y}^{\dagger}_{n+1}):=\widehat{v}_{n+1}+K_{n}({y}^{\dagger}_{n+1}-\widehat{y}_{n+1}),

with
K_{n}=K(\mathfrak{\pi}_{n+1})
given by (
33
).
Thus we obtain the following mean field dynamical system,
which corresponds to (
14
) in the setting where the Kalman gain
K_{n}

is defined by (
33
):

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{n})+\xi_{n},

(54a)

\displaystyle\widehat{y}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=h(\widehat{v}_{n+1})+\eta_{n+1},

(54b)

\displaystyle v_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{v}_{n+1}+\widehat{C}^{vy}_{n+1}(\widehat{C}^{yy}_{n+1})^{-1}({y}^{\dagger}_{n+1}-\widehat{y}_{n+1}),

(54c)

where
\{{y}^{\dagger}_{n}\}
arises from a fixed realization of (
1
)
and equations (
31
), (
32
) define
the Kalman gain
K_{n}=\widehat{C}^{vy}_{n+1}(\widehat{C}^{yy}_{n+1})^{-1}
. We refer to this as
Kalman transport
, noting that it serves as a derivation of the Kalman gain, beyond the linear Gaussian setting.
This is a specific instance of the sample path perspective, and
(
28
,
29
) in particular. Again, this
is a case in which the
sample path evolution for
v_{n}
has law which
only approximates the true filtering law.

The second transport map from Example
9.6
corresponds to the choice

\widetilde{T}^{S}(\widehat{v}_{n+1},\widehat{y}_{n+1};\mathfrak{\pi}_{n+1},{y}^{\dagger}_{n+1}):=m_{n+1}+C_{n+1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\widehat{v}_{n+1}-\mathbb{E}\widehat{v}_{n+1}),

leading to the mean field map (
48
), recalling
that
m_{n+1}
,
\widehat{C}_{n+1}
, and
C_{n+1}
are determined by
(
31
), (
32
) and
(
40
), using (
48
a) and
(
48
b).

Remark 2.14
.

One important difference between the mean field
models (
54
) and (
48
)
is that the former involves inversion of matrices
in data space, and the latter in state space. The relative dimensions of the two spaces plays a role in determining which mean field model is more appropriate as the basis of algorithms. A second noteable difference is that the mean field model
(
48
) does not require generation
of the stochastic data
\widehat{y}_{n+1}
. This is because
we may employ the identity
\mathbb{E}\widehat{y}_{n+1}=\mathbb{E}h(\widehat{v}_{n+1})
and
use (
34
), (
35
) to compute

m_{n+1},C_{n+1}
and
\widehat{C}_{n+1}
.
Motivated by this observation, the next subsection studies a wide class
of approximate transport maps with the property that they do not require
generation of stochastic data.

\blacksquare

2.5.5
Second Order Transport – Deterministic Case

We now turn our attention to approximate filters defined by deterministic mean field maps. We seek to approximate the exact transport (
46
) by mean field maps with the sample path form

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{n})+\xi_{n},

(55a)

\displaystyle v_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{T}^{D}(\widehat{v}_{n+1};\widehat{\mu}_{n+1},{y}^{\dagger}_{n+1}),

(55b)

where
\{{y}^{\dagger}_{n}\}
arises from a fixed realization of (
1
).
As in the previous subsection we drop
explicit
n
-dependence on the measure
\widehat{\mu}_{n+1}
and on the data
{y}^{\dagger}_{n+1}

when no confusion arises from doing so. To this end
we define, for
\widehat{v}_{n+1}
given by (
28
b),

\widehat{\mu}=\textit{Law}\,(\widehat{v}_{n+1})
and
{y}^{\dagger}={y}^{\dagger}_{n+1}.

In the following

T^{D}_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}
is defined by
T^{D}_{n}(\cdot)=T^{D}(\cdot;\widehat{\mu},{y}^{\dagger})

and
\widetilde{T}^{D}_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}
is defined by
\widetilde{T}^{D}_{n}(\cdot)=\widetilde{T}^{D}(\cdot;\widehat{\mu},{y}^{\dagger})
; this is
a useful notational convention for the reasons explained in the
stochastic transport setting.

Analogously to the identities (
50
) in the previous subsection, we seek an approximation
\widetilde{T}^{D}

which, in comparison with the true transport map
T^{D}
, satisfies

\displaystyle(T^{D}_{n})^{\sharp}\widehat{\mu}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{B}_{n}(\mathcal{Q}\widehat{\mu}),

(56a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}\bigl((\widetilde{T}^{D}_{n})^{\sharp}\widehat{\mu}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{B}_{n}(\mathcal{G}\mathcal{Q}\widehat{\mu}),

(56b)

for all measures
\widehat{\mu}
on the state space
\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}.

As in the previous subsection, where we studied approximate stochastic
transport, we again seek maps with a specific affine form. Concretely, the maps are assumed to be affine in the pair
\bigl(\widehat{v}_{n+1},\widehat{h}_{n+1}\bigr)
, with
\widehat{h}_{n+1}=h(\widehat{v}_{n+1})
, leading to the assumed form

\widetilde{T}^{D}_{n}(\cdot)=\widetilde{T}^{D}(\cdot;\mu,{y}^{\dagger})
with

\widetilde{T}^{D}(\widehat{v}_{n+1},\widehat{h}_{n+1};\mu,{y}^{\dagger}):=R\widehat{v}_{n+1}+S\widehat{h}_{n+1}+r,

(57)

for
(\widehat{\mu},{y}^{\dagger})-
dependent matrices/vectors
R,S,r
of appropriate dimensions. Note, however, that
R,S,r
are assumed to be independent of the realization

(\widehat{v}_{n+1},\widehat{h}_{n+1}
), depending only on its law, so that the transport map is affine in
(\widehat{v}_{n+1},\widehat{h}_{n+1})
. With this restriction, which will lead to practical implementable algorithms,
we simply ask that (
56
b) holds:
the first and second moments of the output map agree with those of the
Gaussian projected filter, given by equations
(
31
), (
34
) and (
41
).

As in the previous subsection, there are uncountably many choices of
R,S,r
which we identify in Appendix C, Subsection
9.2
; Example
9.15
highlights two important cases.
The first coincides with (
48
) since
S=0
,
but the second leads to a new mean field map. To formulate this
new map we first define

\widetilde{K}_{n}=\widetilde{K}(\widehat{\mu})
by

\widetilde{K}_{n}=\widehat{C}^{vh}_{n+1}\Bigl((\widehat{C}^{hh}_{n+1}+\Gamma)+\Gamma^{1/2}(\widehat{C}^{hh}_{n+1}+\Gamma)^{1/2}\Bigr)^{-1}.

(58)

We then make the choice

\widetilde{T}^{D}(\widehat{v}_{n+1},\widehat{h}_{n+1};\widehat{\mu},{y}^{\dagger}):=\widehat{v}_{n+1}-\widetilde{K}_{n}(\widehat{h}_{n+1}-\mathbb{E}\widehat{h}_{n+1})+K_{n}({y}^{\dagger}-\mathbb{E}\widehat{h}_{n+1}),

with
K_{n}
given by (
35
) and repeated here for convenience:

K_{n}=\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{vh}\left(\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{hh}+\Gamma\right)^{-1}.

The second mean field map identified in Example
9.15
is

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{n})+\xi_{n},

(59a)

\displaystyle\widehat{h}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=h(\widehat{v}_{n+1}),

(59b)

\displaystyle v_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{v}_{n+1}-\widetilde{K}_{n}(\widehat{h}_{n+1}-\mathbb{E}\widehat{h}_{n+1})+K_{n}({y}^{\dagger}_{n+1}-\mathbb{E}\widehat{h}_{n+1}),

(59c)

where
K_{n}
and
\widetilde{K}_{n}
are computed under
\textit{Law}\,(\widehat{v}_{n+1})
.
This is another instance of the sample path perspective, and
(
28
,
30
) in particular. Again this
sample path evolution for
v_{n}
has law which
only approximates the true filtering law.

Remark 2.15
.

If the ensemble spread is such that
the size of
\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{hh}
is much smaller
than the size of the observational covariance

\Gamma
then we may invoke the approximation

\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{hh}+\Gamma\approx\Gamma
.
With this approximation it follows that
\widetilde{K}_{n}\approx\frac{1}{2}K_{n}
in
(
58
). Some
deterministic ensemble Kalman filters are derived from
mean field dynamics which exploit this approximation by setting
\widetilde{K}_{n}=\frac{1}{2}K_{n}
in (
59
).
We then replace (
59
c) by the compact update step

\displaystyle v_{n+1}=\widehat{v}_{n+1}+K_{n}\left({y}^{\dagger}_{n+1}-\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\mathbb{E}\widehat{h}_{n+1}+\widehat{h}_{n+1})\bigr)\right).

(60)

Such a formulation corresponds to the control-theoretic
perspective of (
14
), with

K_{n}
given by (
35
) and the innovation (
15
) replaced by

\mathfrak{I}_{n}={y}^{\dagger}_{n+1}-\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\mathbb{E}\widehat{h}_{n+1}+\widehat{h}_{n+1}\bigr).

(61)

Filters based on this mean field dynamics thus invoke an additional approximation
of perfect transport, over and above that stemming
from matching only first and second moments: they assume
further that the observational noise dominates ensemble variation.
However we will see that, in the continuous time limit
described in Section
3
,
this form of the innovation arises naturally and does not
constitute an additional approximation.
\hfill\blacksquare

2.5.6
Second Order Transport – Summary

It is helpful at this point to take stock of two approximations
to filtering that we have introduced, Gaussian projected filtering and
approximate transport, and discuss their inter-relations. For simplicity
we do this in the context of mean field stochastic maps, but similar considerations extend to mean field deterministic maps. In this subsection we also include Example
2.17
demonstrating the existence of mean field maps for the
Kalman filter which, recall, applies only in the linear Gaussian setting.

Recall that

\displaystyle\mu_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{B}_{n}(\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}\mu_{n}),\quad\mu_{0}=\mathsf{N}(m_{0},C_{0}),

(62a)

\displaystyle\mu^{G}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{B}_{n}(\mathcal{G}\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}\mu^{G}_{n}),\quad\mu^{G}_{0}=\mathsf{N}(m_{0},C_{0}),

(62b)

With the goal of discussing the inter-relations between
Gaussian projected filtering and approximate transport methods,
we let
\mu^{MF}
denote the measure associated with using the mean field
map
\widetilde{T}^{S}_{n}
to approximate the conditioning step in (
21
).
Using (
50
b) to rewrite the Gaussian projected filter, and using
the construction of the stochastic mean field model (
49
),
we obtain

\displaystyle\mu^{G}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{G}\bigl((\widetilde{T}^{S}_{n})^{\sharp}(\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}\mu^{G}_{n})\bigr),\quad\mu^{G}_{0}=\mathsf{N}(m_{0},C_{0}),

(63a)

\displaystyle\mu^{MF}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=(\widetilde{T}^{S}_{n})^{\sharp}(\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}\mu^{MF}_{n}),\quad\mu^{MF}_{0}=\mathsf{N}(m_{0},C_{0}).

(63b)

Remark 2.16
.

Equations (
63
)
show that
\{\mu^{MF}_{n}\}
is close to
\{\mu^{G}_{n}\}
, if the Gaussian projection in
(
63
a) is close to the identity where it acts on the output
of onestep.
Equations (
62
) show that
\{\mu^{G}_{n}\}
is close
to
\{\mu_{n}\}
if the Gaussian projection in
(
62
b) is close to the identity where it acts on the joint
space of state and observation. Together these two facts suggest that

\{\mu^{MF}_{n}\}
,
\{\mu^{G}_{n}\}
and
\{\mu_{n}\}
are all close
to one another if the two Gaussian projections can be viewed as being close
to the identity map, where they appear in (
62
) and in
(
63
). This provides a potential path for analysis of the mean
field model, away from the linear setting where it is exact. Note also that (
63
a) shows that the Gaussian
projected filter evolves within the manifold of Gaussian probability measures; the mean field model (
63
b) does not.

Example 2.17
.

Assume that
v_{0}\sim\mathsf{N}(m_{0},C_{0}),
that
\Gamma\succ 0
and
consider the Kalman filter setting of Example
2.6
; in particular (
6
) prevails rendering
\Psi
and
h
linear. The mean field stochastic
dynamical system (
54
) then takes the form

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=Mv_{n}+\xi_{n},

(64a)

\displaystyle\widehat{y}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=H\widehat{v}_{n+1}+\eta_{n+1},

(64b)

\displaystyle v_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{v}_{n+1}+\widehat{C}_{n+1}H^{\top}(H\widehat{C}_{n+1}H^{\top}+\Gamma)^{-1}({y}^{\dagger}_{n+1}-\widehat{y}_{n+1}),

(64c)

where
\{{y}^{\dagger}_{n}\}
arises from a fixed realization of (
24
) and
where
C_{n}
is the covariance of
v_{n}
and
\widehat{C}_{n+1}=MC_{n}M^{\top}+\Sigma
is the covariance of
\widehat{v}_{n+1}.

The resulting dynamics give a sample path representation of the Kalman filter in that
v_{n}\sim\mathsf{N}(m_{n},C_{n})
where
m_{n},C_{n}
are as given in Example
2.6
.
This follows because the map defined by (
64
) is well-defined,
since
\Gamma\succ 0.
Lemma
2.12
shows that the approximate
transport is exact in this Gaussian setting.

Similar ideas can be applied to (
48
)
and (
59
) to determine other mean field models with law equal to that of the Kalman filter. Furthermore we observe that the formulation based on (
48
) can be symmetrized to obtain, in the linear Gaussian setting
of (
6
),

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=Mv_{n}+\xi_{n},\>n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},

(65a)

\displaystyle v_{n+1}

\displaystyle=m_{n+1}+A_{n}(\widehat{v}_{n+1}-\widehat{m}_{n+1}),

(65b)

\displaystyle A_{n}

\displaystyle=(C_{n+1})^{1/2}\left[(C_{n+1})^{1/2}\widehat{C}_{n+1}(C_{n+1})^{1/2}\right]^{-1/2}(C_{n+1})^{1/2},

(65c)

with

(\widehat{m}_{n+1},\widehat{C}_{n+1},\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{vh},\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{hh})

defined by (
31
), (
34
)
and
(m_{n+1},C_{n+1})
defined by (
40
).
We note that the second component of the map may be written in gradient
form and corresponds to an optimal transport from
\widehat{\mu}_{n+1}
into
\mu_{n+1}
in the sense of the Euclidean Wasserstein distance of optimal transportation (see Subsection
2.7
for details); indeed this is true of for an entire family of
weighted Wasserstein distances – see Example
9.9
.
To recognize the gradient structure define

\Phi_{n}(v):=\langle m_{n+1},v\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\langle A_{n}(v-\widehat{m}_{n+1}),v-\widehat{m}_{n+1}\rangle

and note that then

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=Mv_{n}+\xi_{n},\>n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},

(66a)

\displaystyle v_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\nabla\Phi_{n}(\widehat{v}_{n+1}).

(66b)

\blacksquare

2.6
Ensemble Kalman Methods

The mean field formulations from Subsection
2.5
provide clear insights into many of the design choices and mechanisms that underlie ensemble Kalman methods.
In this subsection we take the mean field models
and use particle approximations to derive implementable numerical algorithms. When approximated by interacting particle systems, the mean field formulations of ensemble Kalman methods
lead to actionable algorithms.

We start, in Subsection
2.6.1
, with the setting in which transport
is perfect; these algorithms are not, in general, implementable since determining perfect transport is itself a difficult computational task and the subject of ongoing research; see the bibliography Subsection
2.7
. Thus we turn to particle approximations of the transports designed to match first and second order statistics. This leads to the (stochastic)
ensemble Kalman filter
in Subsection
2.6.2
, and to (deterministic)
ensemble square root filters
in
Subsection
2.6.3
. The methods derived in this subsection involve approximating the Kalman gain by computing covariances under the empirical measure defined by the ensemble of particles. To avoid overloading notation, in the rest of this subsection
C_{n+1}
,
\widehat{C}_{n+1}
,
\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{vy}
and
\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{yy}
will denote covariances computed with expectation under the empirical measure. With this notation in place, in the rest of this specific subsection,
K_{n}
will directly refer to the particle approximation of the Kalman gain, computed using the covariances with respect to the empirical measure, without further specification needed. Throughout this section
\{{y}^{\dagger}_{n}\}
arises from a fixed realization of (
1
).

2.6.1
Perfect Particle Filters

The mean field equations (
44
) could, in principle, be
approximated through a particle approximation of the mean field
leading to the following conceptual (because map
T^{S}_{n}
is not known explicitly) algorithm: let
\mathsf{J}=\{1,\cdots,J\}
and consider,
for
(n,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}\times\mathsf{J}
, the
interacting particle dynamical system

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{n+1}^{(j)}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{n}^{(j})+\xi_{n}^{(j)},

(67a)

\displaystyle\widehat{y}_{n+1}^{(j)}

\displaystyle=h(\widehat{v}_{n+1}^{(j})+\eta_{n+1}^{(j)},

(67b)

\displaystyle v_{n+1}^{(j)}

\displaystyle=T^{S}(\widehat{v}_{n+1}^{(j)},\widehat{y}_{n+1}^{(j)};\mathfrak{\pi}_{n+1}^{\mathsf{J}},{y}^{\dagger}_{n+1}),

(67c)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{\pi}_{n+1}^{\mathsf{J}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\delta_{(\widehat{v}_{n+1}^{(j)},\widehat{y}_{n+1}^{(j)})}.

(67d)

This evolves the particles
\{{v}^{(j)}_{n}\}_{j\in\mathsf{J}}
into

\{{v}^{(j)}_{n+1}\}_{j\in\mathsf{J}}
. Here the
\{{\xi}^{(j)}_{n}\}
are, for each
j
,
random variables given by the known distribution of
\xi_{n}

specified in (
2
) and, furthermore, are drawn independently
with respect to each
(n,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}\times\mathsf{J}
.
Similar considerations apply to the
\{{\eta}^{(j)}_{n}\}
which, additionally,
are independent of the
\{{\xi}^{(j)}_{n}\}
.
It is intuitive that the large
J
limit of this
system recovers the mean field dynamics (
44
) and, in particular,

\mu_{n}^{\mathsf{J}}=\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\delta_{v_{n}^{(j)}}\approx\mu_{n}.

(68)

Applying a similar idea to
(
46
) leads to the following conceptual (because map
T^{D}_{n}
is not known explicitly) algorithm.
Consider, for
(n,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}\times\mathsf{J}
,
the interacting particle dynamical system

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{n+1}^{(j)}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{n}^{(j})+\xi_{n}^{(j)},

(69a)

\displaystyle v_{n+1}^{(j)}

\displaystyle=T^{D}(\widehat{v}_{n+1}^{(j)};\widehat{\mu}_{n+1}^{\mathsf{J}},{y}^{\dagger}_{n+1}),

(69b)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mu}_{n+1}^{\mathsf{J}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\delta_{\widehat{v}_{n+1}^{(j)}}.

(69c)

This evolves the particles
\{{v}^{(j)}_{n}\}_{j\in\mathsf{J}}
into

\{{v}^{(j)}_{n+1}\}_{j\in\mathsf{J}}
.
The same assumptions are made about the
\{{\xi}^{(j)}_{n}\}

as for the preceding interacting particle dynamical system.
It is again intuitive that the large
J
limit of this
system recovers the mean field dynamics (
46
), and the evolution
(
47
). In particular it is intuitive that
(
68
) holds for this particle approximation too.
We reiterate that in practice these algorithms are, in general, not easy
to use. More specifically, finding particle based approximations
T^{S,J}
and
T^{D,J}
to the desired transport maps such that

\lim_{J\to\infty}T^{S,J}=T^{S},\qquad\lim_{J\to\infty}T^{D,J}=T^{D}

in an appropriate sense is a computationally challenging task and the subject of ongoing research. This leads to the next two subsections in
which we replace
T^{S}
and
T^{D}
, in the interacting particles systems (
67
) and (
69
), by the previously introduced affine approximate transports
\widetilde{T}^{S}
and
\widetilde{T}^{D},
respectively.

2.6.2
Stochastic Ensemble Kalman Filters

Particle approximation of the mean field dynamical system (
54
),
effecting Kalman transport, bring us to the stochastic EnKF
(
ensemble Kalman filter
). This method may be
derived by writing down a particle approximation of
the mean field stochastic dynamics defined by
(
54
).
We evolve the particles
\{{v}^{(j)}_{n}\}_{j\in\mathsf{J}}
into

\{{v}^{(j)}_{n+1}\}_{j\in\mathsf{J}}
according to the following
stochastic interacting particle system, holding
for
(n,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}\times\mathsf{J}
:

\displaystyle\widehat{v}^{(j)}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi({v}^{(j)}_{n})+{\xi}^{(j)}_{n},\>n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},

(70a)

\displaystyle\widehat{y}^{(j)}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=h(\widehat{v}^{(j)}_{n+1})+{\eta}^{(j)}_{n+1},\>n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},

(70b)

\displaystyle{v}^{(j)}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{v}^{(j)}_{n+1}+K_{n}\bigl({y}^{\dagger}_{n+1}-\widehat{y}^{(j)}_{n+1}\bigr),

(70c)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{\pi}_{n+1}^{\mathsf{J}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\delta_{(\widehat{v}_{n+1}^{(j)},\widehat{y}_{n+1}^{(j)})}.

(70d)

Here the Kalman gain from (
33
) is approximated using the empirical measure
\mathfrak{\pi}_{n+1}^{\mathsf{J}}
, but is still denoted by
K_{n}
to avoid proliferation of notation;
details follow below. The same assumptions regarding

\{{\xi}^{(j)}_{n}\}
and
\{{\eta}^{(j)}_{n+1}\}

are made as for (
67
). We let
\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}_{n}
denote expectation under
\mathfrak{\pi}_{n}^{\mathsf{J}}.

For the basic implementation of EnKF (
70
) the desired covariance matrices, and
Kalman gain (
33
), are then approximated by expectation under

\mathfrak{\pi}_{n+1}^{\mathsf{J}}
, so that

8
8

8

The empirical covariance computations are often
modified to accommodate the widely adopted convention
of scaling by
1/(J-1)
, instead of
1/J
, in view of the matrix being computed from
J-1

independent increments about the mean.

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{vy}

\displaystyle=\,\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}_{n+1}\Bigl(\bigl(\widehat{v}_{n+1}-\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}_{n+1}\widehat{v}_{n+1}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(\widehat{y}_{n+1}-\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}_{n+1}\widehat{y}_{n+1}\bigr)\Bigr),

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{yy}

\displaystyle=\,\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}_{n+1}\Bigl(\bigl(\widehat{y}_{n+1}-\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}_{n+1}\widehat{y}_{n+1}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(\widehat{y}_{n+1}-\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}_{n+1}\widehat{y}_{n+1}\bigr)\Bigr),

\displaystyle K_{n}

\displaystyle=\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{vy}\bigl(\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{yy}\bigr)^{-1}.

Note that a pseudo-inverse may be required to define
K_{n}
. An alternative, avoiding pseudo-inverse, is to use a particle approximation in formula
(
35
b), leading to the use of

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{vh}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{n+1}^{\mathsf{J}}\Bigl(\bigl(\widehat{v}_{n+1}-\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}_{n+1}\widehat{v}_{n+1}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(h(\widehat{v}_{n+1})-\mathbb{E}_{n+1}^{\mathsf{J}}h(\widehat{v}_{n+1})\bigr)\Bigr),

(71a)

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{hh}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{n+1}^{\mathsf{J}}\Bigl(\bigl(h(\widehat{v}_{n+1})-\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}_{n+1}h(\widehat{v}_{n+1})\bigr)\otimes\bigl(h(\widehat{v}_{n+1})-\mathbb{E}_{n+1}^{\mathsf{J}}h(\widehat{v}_{n+1})\bigr)\Bigr),

(71b)

\displaystyle K_{n}

\displaystyle=\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{vh}\bigl(\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{hh}+\Gamma\bigr)^{-1}.

(71c)

to compute the gain
K_{n}.

The advantage of this latter formulation is that it ensures positivity, and hence invertibility, of the covariance in data space, if
\Gamma
is assumed positive-definite. It is hence typically preferred.

Pseudo-code for the stochastic EnKF may be found as
Algorithm
2
in Appendix
7
.

Example 2.18
.

We return to the set-up of Example
2.3
, and now
demonstrate performance of the stochastic EnKF on the same Lorenz ’96 model. Indeed, we again study the Lorenz ’96 (singlescale) model for unknown

v\in C(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathbb{R}^{L})
satisfying the equations (
9
) with
L=9,h_{v}=-0.8
and
F=10

and function
m
as shown in Figure
2.1
. We consider observations
\{{y}^{\dagger}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}
arising from the model

\displaystyle{v}^{\dagger}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi({v}^{\dagger}_{n})+{\xi}^{\dagger}_{n},

\displaystyle{y}^{\dagger}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=h({v}^{\dagger}_{n+1})+{\eta}^{\dagger}_{n+1},

where
\Psi
is the solution operator for (
9
) over the observation time interval
\tau
, and
\{{\xi}^{\dagger}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}
,
\{{\eta}^{\dagger}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are mutually independent Gaussian sequences defined by

{\xi}^{\dagger}_{n}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I)\,\,\text{i.i.d.}\,,\quad{\eta}^{\dagger}_{n}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\gamma^{2}I)\,\,\text{i.i.d.}\,,

with
\sigma=0.1
and
\gamma=0.1
. We again assume that the observation function is linear:
h(v)=Hv
for matrix
H:\mathbb{R}^{9}\to\mathbb{R}^{6}
defined by (
10
).

Figures
2.5(a)
and
2.5(b)
demonstrate the performance of stochastic EnKF in this experimental setting with
\tau=10^{-3}
and using
J=10^{2}
and
J=5\cdot 10^{2}
, respectively, against the performance of 3DVAR with no noise; note that the EnKF uses a time-varying estimate of the gain
K_{n}
, whilst 3DVAR uses the fixed
K
given in Example
2.3
. These experiments illustrate that using sufficiently large ensembles, the ensemble Kalman filter outperforms 3DVAR on such a nonlinear filtering problem where the true state and observational noise levels are high.
Here
outperforms
refers to mean square error in recovery
of the state. To quantitatively demonstrate this improvement, we compute: (a) the mean squared error between the estimates yielded by 3DVAR and the true states; and (b) the mean squared error between the ensemble mean of stochastic EnKF and the true states. In particular we report time-averaged mean squared errors obtained from both 3DVAR and stochastic EnKF given by use of formula (
16
) from Example
2.5
using
t^{*}=3
and
T=10
. An ensemble size of
J=10^{2}
yields
e_{\text{EnKF}}=1.05\cdot 10^{0}
, while for
J=5\cdot 10^{2}
we obtain
e_{\text{EnKF}}=5.24\cdot 10^{-1}
. For comparison, the error obtained using 3DVAR is
e_{\text{3DVAR}}=1.85\cdot 10^{0}
.

\blacksquare

(a)

EnKF with ensemble size
J=10^{2}
.

(b)

EnKF with ensemble size
J=5\cdot 10^{2}
.

Figure 2.5
:
In this experiment we set the noise levels
\sigma=10^{-1},\gamma=10^{-1}
. We display the estimates of
v_{3}
in time produced by EnKF (using ensemble average) and 3DVAR against the true dynamics using observation time interval
\tau=10^{-3}
. Again “dns” refers to direct numerical simulation. The results show that the EnKF provides a more accurate estimate of the trajectory, albeit at higher cost in terms of number of model evaluations.

2.6.3
Ensemble Square Root Filters

The variants on the EnKF described in this subsection
are known as ensemble square root filters; they are based on
mean field maps (
48
) and (
59
),
approximated by interacting particle systems.

Remark 2.19
.

Square root filters are sometimes referred to as deterministic ensemble Kalman filters, to distinguish them from the ensemble Kalman filters described in the preceding subsection (see discussion of this,
and bibliographic information, in Subsection
1.1
.)
However we have already used the terminology
“stochastic” and “deterministic” to distinguish between
different variants on the mean field models that we
describe in Subsections
2.5.4
and
2.5.5

respectively. With the exception of the discussion in Subsection
1.1
, we simply refer to square root filters
for the methods introduced in this subsection. We note however that
they are deterministic in the sense that they do not require generation of random variables.

We introduce two families of square root filters: of adjustment and transform type.
We emphasize again that the choice of which method to use in practice
is determined by implementation details such as the number of particles
J
, the dimension of state space
d_{v}
, and the dimension of the observation space
d_{y}
. These implementation details; although important, are not the focus of this paper.

\blacksquare

Ensemble Adjustment Kalman Filters

We introduce two different particle-based approximations
of mean field models. Both methods are examples of a general class of algorithms known as
ensemble adjustment Kalman filters
: EAKF.
The starting point for the first of these EAKF methods is the mean field map
(
48
a), (
48
c),
repeated
here for convenience:

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{n})+\xi_{n},

(72a)

\displaystyle v_{n+1}

\displaystyle=m_{n+1}+C_{n+1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\widehat{v}_{n+1}-\mathbb{E}\widehat{v}_{n+1}),

(72b)

where
m_{n+1}
,
\widehat{C}_{n+1}
, and
C_{n+1}
are determined by
(
31
), (
34
), (
35
) and (
40
).
9
9

9

Although the identity
(
40
) is derived in a subsection concerning the
Gaussian projected filter, it is contained in Lemma
2.9
which simply concerns conditioning of Gaussians.

As in the previous subsection the methods evolve
particle ensemble
\{{v}^{(j)}_{n}\}_{j\in\mathsf{J}}
into

\{{v}^{(j)}_{n+1}\}_{j\in\mathsf{J}}
, via the predictive ensemble

\{\widehat{v}^{(j)}_{n+1}\}_{j\in\mathsf{J}}.
However they do not employ simulated data

\{\widehat{y}^{(j)}_{n+1}\}_{j\in\mathsf{J}}
, rather they make use of

\{\widehat{h}^{(j)}_{n+1}\}_{j\in\mathsf{J}}
, where
\widehat{h}^{(j)}_{n}=h(\widehat{v}^{(j)}_{n}).

To define the methods it helps to introduce new
notation. Slightly modifying the notation in the preceding subsection,
we now let
\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}_{n}
denote expectation with respect to the empirical measure

\widehat{\mu}_{n}^{\mathsf{J}}=\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\delta_{\widehat{v}^{(j)}_{n}}.

We let
\widehat{v}_{n+1}
denote the random variable with this distribution and,
as before,
\widehat{h}_{n+1}=h(\widehat{v}_{n+1}).

Next we define matrix
\widehat{V}_{n}
comprising
scaled ensemble deviations in state space:

\widehat{V}_{n}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{J}}\left(\widehat{v}_{n}^{(1)}-\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}_{n}\widehat{v}_{n},\widehat{v}_{n}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}_{n}\widehat{v}_{n},\cdots,\widehat{v}_{n}^{(J)}-\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}_{n}\widehat{v}_{n}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}\times J},

we then define the analogous matrix
\widehat{H}_{n}
in observation space

\widehat{H}_{n}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{J}}\left(\widehat{h}_{n}^{(1)}-\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}_{n}\widehat{h}_{n},\widehat{h}_{n}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}_{n}\widehat{h}_{n},\cdots,\widehat{h}_{n}^{(J)}-\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}_{n}\widehat{h}_{n}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{y}\times J}.

With these notations in hand,
we have, with expectations computed under
\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}_{n}
,

\displaystyle\widehat{C}^{vh}_{n}

\displaystyle=\widehat{V}_{n}\widehat{H}_{n}^{\top},

(73a)

\displaystyle\widehat{C}^{hh}_{n}

\displaystyle=\widehat{H}_{n}\widehat{H}_{n}^{\top},

(73b)

and the ensemble-based approximation of the Kalman gain matrix is

10
10

10

Here too, the empirical covariance computations are often
modified to accommodate the widely adopted convention
of scaling by
1/(J-1)
, instead of
1/J
.

K_{n}=\widehat{V}_{n+1}\widehat{H}_{n+1}^{\top}(\widehat{H}_{n+1}\widehat{H}_{n+1}^{\top}+\Gamma)^{-1}.

(74)

This is a linear algebraic reformulation of the Kalman gain approximation resulting from
(
71
).
By making a particle approximation of (
72
),
using (
31
), (
34
), (
35
) and (
40
), we obtain

\displaystyle\widehat{v}^{(j)}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi({v}^{(j)}_{n})+{\xi}^{(j)}_{n},\>n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},

(75a)

\displaystyle\widehat{m}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}_{n+1}\widehat{v}_{n+1},

(75b)

\displaystyle m_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{m}_{n+1}+K_{n}({y}^{\dagger}_{n+1}-\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}_{n+1}\widehat{h}_{n+1}),

(75c)

\displaystyle v_{n+1}^{(j)}

\displaystyle=m_{n+1}+C_{n+1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\widehat{v}_{n+1}^{(j)}-\widehat{m}_{n+1}\right),

(75d)

where
K_{n}
is given by (
74
); furthermore,

\widehat{C}_{n+1}
and
C_{n+1}
are computed empirically using

\widehat{C}_{n+1}=\widehat{V}_{n+1}\widehat{V}_{n+1}^{\top},\quad C_{n+1}=\widehat{V}_{n+1}\left(I+\widehat{H}_{n+1}^{\top}\Gamma^{-1}\widehat{H}_{n+1}\right)^{-1}\widehat{V}_{n+1}^{\top}.

(76)

The first of these two formulae follows similarly to (
73
);
the second of these two formulae is derived as follows:

11
11

11

Using, in the last line, the identity
I-W^{\top}(WW^{\top}+I)^{-1}W=(I+W^{\top}W)^{-1}
which holds for all (not necessarily square) matrices
W
.

\displaystyle C_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{C}_{n+1}-\widehat{C}^{vh}_{n+1}(\widehat{C}^{hh}_{n+1}+\Gamma)^{-1}(\widehat{C}^{vh}_{n+1})^{\top}

(77a)

\displaystyle=\widehat{V}_{n+1}\widehat{V}_{n+1}^{\top}-\widehat{V}_{n+1}\widehat{H}_{n+1}^{\top}(\widehat{H}_{n+1}\widehat{H}_{n+1}^{\top}+\Gamma)^{-1}\widehat{H}_{n+1}\widehat{V}_{n+1}^{\top}

(77b)

\displaystyle=\widehat{V}_{n+1}\left(I-\widehat{H}_{n+1}^{\top}(\widehat{H}_{n+1}\widehat{H}_{n+1}^{\top}+\Gamma)^{-1}\widehat{H}_{n+1}\right)\widehat{V}_{n+1}^{\top}

(77c)

\displaystyle=\widehat{V}_{n+1}\left(I+\widehat{H}_{n+1}^{\top}\Gamma^{-1}\widehat{H}_{n+1}\right)^{-1}\widehat{V}_{n+1}^{\top}.

(77d)

The EAKF (
75
) takes as starting point (
48
). If instead we apply a particle approximation to the mean field dynamical
system (
59
) we obtain a second version of the EAKF:

\displaystyle\widehat{v}^{(j)}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi({v}^{(j)}_{n})+{\xi}^{(j)}_{n},\>n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},

(78a)

\displaystyle\widehat{h}_{n+1}^{(j)}

\displaystyle=h(\widehat{v}_{n+1}^{(j)}),

(78b)

\displaystyle\widehat{m}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}_{n+1}\widehat{v}_{n+1},

(78c)

\displaystyle m_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{m}_{n+1}+K_{n}({y}^{\dagger}_{n+1}-\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}_{n+1}\widehat{h}_{n+1}),

(78d)

\displaystyle v_{n+1}^{(j)}

\displaystyle=m_{n+1}+(\widehat{v}_{n+1}^{(j)}-\widehat{m}_{n+1})-\widetilde{K}_{n}(\widehat{h}_{n+1}^{(j)}-\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}_{n+1}\widehat{h}_{n+1}).

(78e)

By making an empirical approximation of the formula (
58
)
the matrix
\widetilde{K}_{n}
is defined using the identification

\widetilde{K}_{n}=\widehat{V}_{n+1}\widehat{H}_{n+1}^{\top}\left[(\widehat{H}_{n+1}\widehat{H}_{n+1}^{\top}+\Gamma)+(\widehat{H}_{n+1}\widehat{H}_{n+1}^{\top}+\Gamma)^{1/2}\Gamma^{1/2}\right]^{-1}.

Remark 2.20
.

The key difference between (
75
) and (
78
) is that the former involves inversion in state space, and the latter in data space. The relative size of the two dimensions dictates which is preferable.
\blacksquare

Ensemble Transform Kalman Filters

The two EAKFs just defined both involve application, and inversion,
of matrices which are applied on the left and act on state space.
A different class of
algorithms, known as
ensemble transform Kalman filters
(ETKF), involve matrix multiplication
from the right and consequently inversions take place in the ensemble space of dimension
J
. In many applications this is far smaller than the dimension of the state or data spaces, and then use of this version of the methodology is preferred. The aim is to determine matrix
Z_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{J\times J}
, and to derive Kalman gain
K_{n}
from
Z_{n}
, so that the following interacting particle system produces an ensemble of particles
\{{v}^{(j)}_{n+1}\}_{j\in\mathsf{J}}
with empirical covariance

C_{n+1}
defined by the second item in display (
76
):

\displaystyle\widehat{v}^{(j)}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi({v}^{(j)}_{n})+{\xi}^{(j)}_{n},\>n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},

(79a)

\displaystyle\widehat{m}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}_{n+1}\widehat{v}_{n+1},

(79b)

\displaystyle m_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{m}_{n+1}+K_{n}({y}^{\dagger}_{n+1}-\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}_{n+1}\widehat{h}_{n+1}),

(79c)

\displaystyle v_{n+1}^{(j)}

\displaystyle=m_{n+1}+\sum_{i=1}^{J}\left(\widehat{v}_{n+1}^{(i)}-\widehat{m}_{n+1}\right)(Z_{n})_{ij}.

(79d)

To this end we define the matrix of ensemble deviations

V_{n}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{J}}\left(v_{n}^{(1)}-\mathbb{E}_{*,n}^{\mathsf{J}}v_{n},v_{n}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}_{*,n}^{\mathsf{J}}v_{n},\cdots,v_{n}^{(J)}-\mathbb{E}_{*,n}^{\mathsf{J}}v_{n}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}\times J},

where, here, expectation
\mathbb{E}_{*,n}^{\mathsf{J}}
is with respect to the empirical measure (
68
)
and
v_{n}
is a random variable with this distribution. It then follows from (
79
d) that

V_{n+1}=\widehat{V}_{n+1}Z_{n}.

(80)

If we define

Z_{n}=\left(I+\widehat{H}_{n+1}^{\top}\Gamma^{-1}\widehat{H}_{n+1}\right)^{-1/2}\in\mathbb{R}^{J\times J}.

(81)

then, as desired,
V_{n+1}V_{n+1}^{\top}=C_{n+1}
as defined by (
76
), by virtue of (
77
).
The calculations in (
77
) can also be utilized to verify that
the empirical Kalman gain matrix defined by (
74
) satisfies

K_{n}=\widehat{V}_{n+1}Z_{n}^{2}\widehat{H}_{n+1}^{\top}\Gamma^{-1}.

Using this formula for
K_{n}
in (
75
) leads to an algorithm which matches first and second order statistics of the Gaussian projected filter, at
n+1
, requiring only matrix inversions in space of dimension defined by the number of particles
J
.

We finally note that (
79
c) and
(
79
d) can be combined into a single transformation step of the form

v_{n+1}^{(j)}=\sum_{i=1}^{J}\widehat{v}_{n+1}^{(i)}(S_{n})_{ij}\,,

(82)

where
S_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{J\times J}
replaces the matrix
Z_{n}

in (
79
d) such that

m_{n+1}=\sum_{j=1}^{J}v_{n+1}^{(j)}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{J}\widehat{v}_{n+1}^{(i)}(S_{n})_{ij}

holds in addition to (
80
) with
Z_{n}
replaced by
S_{n}
.

Remark 2.21
.

Formulation (
82
) has a number of attractive features. First, it clearly reveals that the analysis
\{{v}^{(j)}_{n+1}\}_{j\in\mathsf{J}}
lies in the span of the space spanned by the predictions
\{\widehat{v}^{(j)}_{n+1}\}_{j\in\mathsf{J}}
, which is relevant whenever
J<d_{v}
. Second, all particle implementations of the ensemble Kalman filter and many of its extensions can be put into the framework (
82
) with the (possibly random)
matrix
S_{n}
chosen appropriately. Third, it encodes a coupling between the prediction
\{\widehat{v}^{(j)}_{n+1}\}_{j\in\mathsf{J}}
and the analysis

\{{v}^{(j)}_{n+1}\}_{j\in\mathsf{J}}
at the level of their associated empirical measures
\widehat{\mu}_{n}^{\mathsf{J}}
and
\mu_{n}^{\mathsf{J}}
, respectively.
See the following bibliographic Section
2.7
for more details.

\blacksquare

2.7
Bibliographical Notes

Ideas from feedback control underlie the material in
Subsection
2.2
, addressing Objective 1.
Control theory is an enormous subject in its
own right and we cannot do justice to it in this paper. For study of linear control theory, as illustrated in Example
2.2
, see
Åström and Murray 2021
,
Sontag 2013
for engineering and mathematical treatments respectively.
For the control theoretic approach to the state estimation problem see
Luenberger 1964
and
Luenberger 1971
.

Our study of control-theoretic methods has focused on
3DVAR. Recent analysis of the 3DVAR method rests heavily on ideas
arising from determining modes for dissipative evolution equations, an idea with roots in the paper
Foias and Prodi 1967
and
unified in the book
Temam 2012
.
The use of these ideas in data
assimilation was introduced in
Olson and Titi 2003

and developed further in
Hayden et al. 2011
and
Foias et al. 2016
; the papers
Law and Stuart 2012
,
Law et al. 2014
,
Law et al. 2016b
and
Sanz-Alonso and Stuart 2015

essentially establish the stability of these deterministic results to small noise perturbations; see also
Moodey et al. 2013
for related analysis. In the context of using observations to control the instability of chaotic systems, all of this work may be seen as building on the study of synchronization
(
Pecora and Carroll 1990
)
, reviewed in
Ashwin 2003
.

In Subsection
2.3
we introduce the probabilistic approach to filtering, addressing Objective 2. In low dimensional
systems particle filters provide a flexible and efficient tool
for attacking probabilistic filtering; see
Doucet et al. 2001
. However in this paper our focus
is on high dimensional problems and Kalman-based methods
specifically. The books

Reich and Cotter 2015
,

Asch et al. 2016
,

Law et al. 2015
,

Harlim and Majda 2010
,

Abarbanel 2013
, and

Evensen et al. 2022
provide overviews of a variety of
filtering methods, and ensemble Kalman methods
from Subsection
2.6
in particular.

The Kalman filter
(
Kalman 1960
)
from Example
2.6
led to arguably the first systematic analysis of an algorithm for incorporation of discrete time data into estimation of a discrete time stochastic dynamical system; it applies
only to linear Gaussian systems.
The monograph
Jazwinski 2007
provides an introduction to nonlinear filtering both in discrete and continuous time; in particular it discusses the extended Kalman filter, found by applying the Kalman filter to a linearization of the state and data dynamics. However this method does not work well in high dimensions

Ghil et al. 1981
, motivating the use of mean field
maps, as introduced in Subsection
2.5
, and the
ensemble-based methods from Subsection
2.6
which
approximate them. The approximation of
mean field maps by interacting particle systems is overviewed in
Sznitman 1991
.

We refer the reader to the excellent monographs by
Asch et al. 2016
and
Evensen et al. 2022
for texts with emphasis on important implementation details, not covered in this paper, relating to these ensemble Kalman methods; these include techniques such as
inflation and localization that are central to the success of
these methods in high dimensions. We also refer to the paper by
Vetra-Carvalho et al. 2018
for a comprehensive review of the algorithmic details of ensemble Kalman methods. Here we point to two particular implementation details that are
of particular practical importance. The first concerns the use of ensemble square-root filters from Subsection
2.6.3
. The matrix
Z_{n}
in (
80
) is not uniquely defined by the requirement
V_{n+1}V_{n+1}^{\top}=C_{n+1}
. Formula (
81
) constitutes one possible choice, which leads to a symmetric
Z_{n}
. See
Livings et al. 2008
for more details.
Second, finite particle implementations of the stochastic EnKF
from Subsection
2.6.2

entail that the random realizations
\widehat{y}^{(j)}_{n+1}
appear both in
the Kalman gain
K_{n}
as well as in the innovation term in (
70
c). As first observed by
Houtekamer and Mitchell 2005
, this leads to a systematic underestimation of the ensemble spread, which vanishes in the
J\to\infty
limit; but can affect the performance of the EnKF for small particle sizes.
In Subsection
5.6
we will
highlight the same effect when discussing finite particle
implementations
(
Nüsken and Reich 2019
,
Garbuno-Inigo et al. 2020b
)

of the ensemble Kalman sampler for Bayesian inversion,
based on the mean field model proposed in
Garbuno-Inigo et al. 2020a
.

Particle-based extensions of the classical Kalman filter to nonlinear filtering problems include the unscented Kalman filter and the ensemble Kalman filter. While this paper focuses primarily on ensemble Kalman filter techniques, the unscented Kalman filter is an approach based on application of quadrature to the Gaussian projected filter from
Subsection
2.4
; see
Julier et al. 2000
as well as
Särkkä and Svensson 2023
. A discussion and evaluation in the context of ensemble square root filters
may be found in
Wang et al. 2004
.

Much of the development of ensemble Kalman methods reflects
the historical roots of the subject in the geophysical sciences, the atmosphere-ocean sciences in particular, including Lagrangian data assimilation, and in
the modeling of subsurface flow

(
Burgers et al. 1998
,
Houtekamer and Mitchell 1998
,
Anderson 2001
,
Bishop et al. 2001
,
Whitaker and Hamill 2002
,
Tippett et al. 2003
,
Hunt et al. 2007
,
Li and Reynolds 2009
,
Sakov et al. 2012
,
Bocquet and Sakov 2014
,
Evensen 2019
,
Bocquet and Sakov 2012
,
Bocquet et al. 2017
,
Gurumoorthy et al. 2017
,
Sampson et al. 2021
,
Kuznetsov et al. 2003
,
Salman et al. 2006
)
. We also mention the randomized maximum likelihood (RML) approach to Bayesian inference which is closely related to the analysis step of a stochastic EnKF and has also been developed primarily through application in the geophysical sciences
(
Kitanidis 1995
,
Oliver et al. 1997
,
Oliver et al. 2008
)
.

There is also a body of literature concerning the analysis and development of ensemble methods with an emphasis on applications in
complex and turbulent flows:

Grooms et al. 2014
,

Grooms et al. 2015
,

Robinson et al. 2018
,

Lee et al. 2017
,

Gottwald and Majda 2013
,

Kelly et al. 2015
,

Tong et al. 2016a
,

Tong et al. 2016b
,

Kelly et al. 2015
,

Harlim et al. 2014
,

Majda and Tong 2018
,

Fertig et al. 2007
,

Harlim and Hunt 2007b
,

Harlim and Hunt 2007a
.
The conceptual fluid dynamics models of Lorenz
(
Lorenz 1996
)
(often referred to, collectively, as Lorenz ’96 models) have been particularly influential in germinating this body of work and we use them exclusively in our illustrative Examples
2.3
,
2.18
,
2.5
,
4.25
and
8.1
. Furthermore we will make use of the relationship between the multiscale and singlescale version of the model as developed in
Fatkullin and Vanden-Eijnden 2004
.

Recently ensemble Kalman methods have been developed for potential use in machine learning

(
Haber et al. 2018
,
Kovachki and Stuart 2019
,
Guth et al. 2022
,
Grooms 2021
,
Gottwald and Reich 2021
,
Yang and Grooms 2021
,
Pidstrigach and Reich 2023
)
;
see also
Bocquet et al. 2017
,
Chen et al. 2022b

for research at the intersection of machine learning with ensemble Kalman methodology.

In this survey we have started from mean field equations, in
Subsection
2.5
, and then discretized the mean field limit using
J
particles in subsequent subsections. It is of interest to demonstrate that the discrete formulations which arise actually converge to the mean field equations in the
J\to\infty
limit. This has indeed been established for the ensemble Kalman filter when applied in the
linear Gaussian setting in which the mean field limit exactly recovers the filtering distribution
(
Le Gland et al. 2011
,
Mandel et al. 2011
,
Kwiatkowski and Mandel 2015
)
and indeed some
results also apply in the nonlinear setting.
In the continuous time-setting long-time error estimates, exploiting ergodicity of the Kalman-Bucy filter itself and propagation of chaos ideas to extend to the
ensemble Kalman approximations, are developed in
Del Moral and Tugaut 2018
; see
Subsection
3.7
for further details concerning continuous time. The paper
Law et al. 2016a
studies related work concerning the mean field limit of ensemble Kalman
methods in the context of non-Gaussian problems. The papers
Ding et al. 2021
,

Ding and Li 2021a
,
Ding and Li 2021b
study particle approximation of mean field
limits beyond the Gaussian setting, primarily in the context of the solution of inverse problems; see the discussion in Subsection
5.6
. The papers
Hoel et al. 2016
,
Chernov et al. 2021
study the use of multilevel approximation of the mean field limit, coupling ensemble approximations at different levels of space or time discretization.

In Subsection
2.5.3
we introduce the idea of
second-order transport: approximations of the perfect transport maps
that effect filtering.
The non-uniqueness of second order transport maps is studied
in continuous time, for linear Gaussian stochastic differential
equations, in
Taghvaei and Mehta 2020
; this work is closely
related to our analysis in the first two subsections of Section
9
. Non-Gaussian extensions are discussed in
Taghvaei and Hosseini 2022
.
We also highlight that it is possible to construct second
order transport maps
\widetilde{T}
, which satisfy conditions different from
(
50
b) and (
56
b), respectively. For example,
one could request that

\mathcal{G}\bigl((\widetilde{T}^{S}_{n})^{\sharp}\mathfrak{\pi}\bigr)=\mathcal{G}\mathcal{B}_{n}(\mathfrak{\pi}).

This approximation has been utilized by
Lei and Bickel 2011
. The analogous
deterministic approach has been put forward by
Tödter and Ahrens 2015

and has been explored further, for example, in
Acevedo et al. 2017
. Alternatively, one can also replace the definition (
36
) of the Gaussian projection operator
G
. To this end, recall that the Kullback–Leibler divergence
between probability measures
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is defined as

d_{\rm{KL}}(\pi_{1}||\pi_{2})=\int\pi_{1}(du)\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\pi_{1}}{\mathrm{d}\pi_{2}}(u);

in particular, it is not symmetric in its two arguments. Gaussian variational inference

(
Bishop 2011
)
is for example based of the definition

\mathcal{G}\mu={\rm argmin}_{\pi\in\mathfrak{G}}d_{\rm{KL}}(\pi||\mu)

(83)

in place of (
36
); note, however, that the minimizer of
(
83
) may not be unique, whilst the minimizer of (
36
)
is always unique.

While we follow the moment matching perspective on the derivation of ensemble Kalman filter methods in this survey, we mention in passing that there is an alternative perspective based on linear minimum variance estimators. See
Van Leeuwen 2020
in the context of the stochastic ensemble Kalman filter and the Appendix
9.3
as well as
Lei and Bickel 2011
for nonlinear extensions. The Bayes linear methodology is also an alternative
approach of potential interest
Goldstein and Wooff 2007
.

Even in the mean field limit
J\to\infty
, the ensemble Kalman filter provides approximations only to approximate transport based filters. They are only exact in the linear Gaussian setting
(
Le Gland et al. 2011
)
; recent works develop new tools of analysis
to extend this to nonlinear filtering problems that are close to
Gaussian
(
Carrillo et al. 2022b
,
Calvello et al. 2024
)
. As mentioned in Subsection
1.1
, sequential Monte Carlo methods can be designed to be consistent with the underlying nonlinear filtering problem as defined, for example, by perfect transport based filters. Foundational analysis of these particle methods is undertaken in
Crisan et al. 1999
and
Del Moral 2004
; but we reiterate
that, in contrast to ensemble Kalman based methods,
they do not scale well to high dimensions.
We also point to
Del Moral et al. 2006
for an application of the sequential Monte Carlo method to Bayesian inference problems; the approach therein is closely related to iterative implementations of the EnKF that were
subsequently developed in the papers
Li and Reynolds 2009
,
Gu and Oliver 2007
,
Sakov et al. 2012
.

Despite only providing approximations to the exact filtering distribution, i.e. from the perspective of Objective 2, accuracy and stability results for the ensemble Kalman filter, viewed as a state estimator and hence from the
perspective of Objective 1, have been derived. See, for example,

González-Tokman and Hunt 2013
,
Kelly et al. 2014
,

Tong et al. 2016b
,
Tong et al. 2016a

and
Del Moral and Horton 2023
. Mechanisms for finite time filter divergence have also been identified
(
Gottwald and Majda 2013
,
Kelly et al. 2015
)
.

Extending the ensemble Kalman filter to strongly nonlinear and high dimensional state estimation problems constitute an area of active ongoing research. The current state of the art has been summarized in
Van Leeuwen et al. 2019
in the context of high dimensional geophysical applications. Extensions of the transport framework (
46
), which build on approximating the perfect transport maps
T^{D}
in (
46
b) in an asymptotically consistent manner, include the work by
Reich 2013
,
Cheng and Reich 2015
,
Spantini et al. 2022
, and
Zech and Marzouk 2022
. In an alternative line of research there have been several proposals to construct hybrid methods, which aim to adaptively bridge between the ensemble Kalman and particle filters; including the work by
Stordal et al. 2011
,
Frei and Künsch 2013
,
Chustagulprom et al. 2016
, and
Nerger 2022
.

In this context, the transformation formula (
82
) proves to be rather useful since most existing particle based methods can be covered by appropriate choices of
S_{n}
, where
S_{n}
is typically the realization of a random matrix. See
Reich and Cotter 2015
for more details. For example, a resampling step in a sequential Monte Carlo method gives rise to a matrix
S_{n}
with a single non-zero entry equal to one in each of its columns. More generally it holds that, for all
j\in\mathsf{J}
,

\sum_{i\in\mathsf{J}}(S_{n})_{ij}=1.

In particular, the matrix
S_{n}
can be chosen to correspond to an optimal coupling between two discrete random variables
(
Reich 2013
)
, which builds a link between filtering and optimal transport also explored in
Corenflos et al. 2021
. The subject of optimal transport is given a comprehensive treatment in
Villani 2008
; see also
Villani 2021
. Computational aspects of the subject including entropy-regularized optimal transport are discussed in
Cuturi 2013
and
Peyré and Cuturi 2019
. Entropy-regularization is linked to the Schrödinger bridge problem, and connections with data assimilation are developed in
Reich 2019
. See also Subsection
1.1
for discussion of transport-based methodologies within the context of ensemble Kalman methods. See Example
9.9
and
Reich and Cotter 2015
for the connection between the map (
66
) and optimal transport. For a derivation of the standard formulae for mean and covariance of conditioned Gaussians used in the proof of Lemma
2.9
see
Eaton 2007
.

Finally we observe that we do not discuss, in this paper, the
smoothing
approach to state estimation from data in model (
1
). This approach aims at finding
the entire sequence
\{v_{\ell}\}_{\ell=0}^{n}
from the data
{Y}^{\dagger}_{n}
. Thus state estimates depend on data in their future. To read about smoothing see
Evensen et al. 2022
,
Sanz-Alonso et al. 2023
. We note here that there is a smoothing counterpart
of the 3DVAR algorithm (see Remark
2.1
) known as 4DVAR because, for physical systems, it uses data distributed
in the three space and one time dimensions.

3
State Estimation: Continuous Time

This section is devoted to deriving, and studying properties of, continuous
time analogs of concepts introduced in the previous Section

2
. We start in Subsection
3.1

defining the set-up.
Thereafter the subsections mirror those from the preceding
Section
2
, describing the relevant continuous time analogs;
in particular we conclude with Subsection
3.7

containing bibliographic notes.

All problems arising in practice are implemented as
algorithms in discrete time, so it is important to establish
motivation for the continuous time formulations.
There are two primary reasons for introducing them.
The first is that continuous time limits of the
discrete algorithms provide a way to understand and interpret
the behavior of the discrete algorithms; results about
accuracy, stability and uncertainty quantification, which shed
light on the relative merits of different algorithmic approaches,
are often cleanest in the continuous time setting. The
second is that many problems arising in practice involve
physical processes which evolve in continuous time; the
data informing these models is typically discrete in time, but when
the observations take place at very high frequency, it is insightful to consider the idealization of continuous time data. Both of these motivations underlie the developments in this section.

3.1
Set-Up

We start by deriving the continuous-time analog of the discrete-time set-up (
1
) for state-observation coevolution: for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
we have

\displaystyle v_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{n})+\xi_{n},

\displaystyle y_{n+1}

\displaystyle=h(v_{n+1})+\eta_{n+1};

recall that we assume that
v_{0},\{\xi_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}

and
\{\eta_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are mutually independent
Gaussians defined by

v_{0}\sim\mathsf{N}(m_{0},C_{0}),\quad\xi_{n}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\Sigma)\,\,\text{i.i.d.},\quad\eta_{n}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\Gamma)\,\,\text{i.i.d.}\,\,.

We introduce a small increment in time, denoted by
\Delta t
.
From the map
\Psi(\cdot)
defining the systematic component of the state dynamics, we now define an infinitesimal analog
f(\cdot)
; we also introduce the rescaled observation operators
\mathsf{h}(\cdot)
from the original nonlinear observation operator
h(\cdot)
; and we introduce state/observational covariances
(\mathsf{\Gamma},\mathsf{\Sigma})

by rescaling
(\Gamma,\Sigma)
:

\displaystyle\Psi(v)

\displaystyle=v+\Delta tf(v),\quad h(v)=\Delta t\mathsf{h}(v),

(84a)

\displaystyle\Sigma

\displaystyle=\Delta t\mathsf{\Sigma},\quad\Gamma=\Delta t\mathsf{\Gamma}.

(84b)

By virtue of our assumptions on
\Psi
and
h
, functions

f
and
\mathsf{h}
are assumed to be known measurable functions (with respect to the Borel algebra), bounded on compact sets.
In the linear setting
\Psi(\cdot)=M\cdot
,
h(\cdot)=H\cdot
we will also introduce
an infinitesimal vector field
f(\cdot)=F\cdot
for matrix
F
, and
rescaled linear observation operator
\mathsf{H}

12
12

12

Note the difference, conceptual and notational, between the discrete time objects

(h(\cdot),H,\Gamma,\Sigma)
and the related continuous time objects
(\mathsf{h}(\cdot),\mathsf{H},\mathsf{\Gamma},\mathsf{\Sigma})
.

\displaystyle M=\text{Id}+\Delta tF,\quad H=\Delta t\mathsf{H}.

(85)

The observation
\{y_{n}\}
is
best thought of, in the scalings we introduce, as capturing increments of a process

\{z_{n}\}
. To capture this, and extend it to the specific realization of the data
appearing in the algorithms, and the artificial data used in some algorithms,
we introduce the variables
z_{n}
,
{z}^{\dagger}_{n}
,
\widehat{z}_{n}
by assuming that

\displaystyle y_{n+1}

\displaystyle:=z_{n+1}-z_{n}=\Delta z_{n+1},

(86a)

\displaystyle{y}^{\dagger}_{n+1}

\displaystyle:={z}^{\dagger}_{n+1}-{z}^{\dagger}_{n}=\Delta{z}^{\dagger}_{n+1},

(86b)

\displaystyle\widehat{y}_{n+1}

\displaystyle:=\widehat{z}_{n+1}-\widehat{z}_{n}=\Delta\widehat{z}_{n+1}.

(86c)

Note that
z_{n}
,
{z}^{\dagger}_{n}
,
\widehat{z}_{n}
have dimension
d_{z}=d_{y}
.
We assume that
z_{0}={z}^{\dagger}_{0}=\widehat{z}_{0}=0
. Then
z_{n}
,
{z}^{\dagger}_{n}
,
\widehat{z}_{n}

are uniquely defined from
y_{n}
,
{y}^{\dagger}_{n}
,
\widehat{y}_{n}
, respectively.

In the following we define
t_{n}=n\Delta t.
With the scalings above in hand, we may view the state
v_{n}
and observation
y_{n}
as
relating to approximations of continuous time processes
v(\cdot)
and
z(\cdot)
:

v_{n}\approx v(t_{n})
,
z_{n}\approx z(t_{n})
.
We also introduce continuous time process
\widehat{v}(\cdot)
, which will be used
in the prediction steps of algorithms, and

\bigl({z}^{\dagger}(\cdot),\widehat{z}(\cdot)\bigr)
, which denotes the continuous time observed
data which we are conditioning on and predicted data, respectively. We assume that
z(0)={z}^{\dagger}(0)=\widehat{z}(0)=0.

Under the rescalings above, and in the limit
\Delta t\to 0
,
the data assimilation problem may be reformulated in
terms of SDEs. Furthermore, the related mappings on measures, and discrete-time algorithms that stem from them, may be reformulated in terms of SPDEs and SDEs respectively; we now go on to identify these continuous time stochastic processes.

Applying the rescalings in (
84
) and the reparametrization of
y_{n+1}
in (
86
a), we obtain the system

\displaystyle v_{n+1}

\displaystyle=v_{n}+\Delta tf(v_{n})+\xi_{n},

(87a)

\displaystyle z_{n+1}

\displaystyle=z_{n}+\Delta t\mathsf{h}(v_{n+1})+\eta_{n+1},

(87b)

for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, where we assume
v_{0},\{\xi_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}

and
\{\eta_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are mutually independent
Gaussians defined by

v_{0}\sim\mathsf{N}(m_{0},C_{0}),\quad\xi_{n}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\Delta t\mathsf{\Sigma})\,\,\text{i.i.d.},\quad\eta_{n}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\Delta t\mathsf{\Gamma})\,\,\text{i.i.d.}\,\,.

Note that (
87
) is a variant on the Euler-Maruyama discretization of a vector-valued SDE. Indeed, by taking the
\Delta t\rightarrow 0
limit it is clear that the natural continuous time analog of equations (
1
) is the SDE

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=f(v)\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\mathsf{\Sigma}}\mathrm{d}W,\quad v_{0}\sim\mathsf{N}(m_{0},C_{0}),

(88a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=\mathsf{h}(v)\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\mathrm{d}B,\quad z(0)=0,

(88b)

taken to hold for all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}.

The vector fields
f(\cdot)
and
\mathsf{h}(\cdot)
describe the systematic,
deterministic components of the dynamics and observation processes
and are assumed known. The systematic
components of the model
are subjected to white noise defined through the
independent unit Brownian motions
W
and
B
, in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}

and
\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}
respectively, and correlated across the
state and data spaces via the covariances
\mathsf{\Sigma},\mathsf{\Gamma}.
The initial condition for
v

is Gaussian and independent of
W
and
B
. Analogous to the discrete time setting, we assume that

C_{0}\succeq 0,\quad\mathsf{\Sigma}\succeq 0,\quad\mathsf{\Gamma}\succ 0.

(89)

Note that, for each fixed
t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, the state
v(t)\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}
and the
observations
z(t)\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}.

Throughout we use
\dagger
again
to denote a specific realization of a process,
as in the discrete time setting. We assume that we
have available to us a sample path
\{{z}^{\dagger}(t)\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}}

of the observation coordinates of a realization
of the SDE (
88
). From this sample path we wish to recover the true realization of the state
\{{v}^{\dagger}(t)\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}}

which gave rise to it. These observation and state sample paths
are generated by
{v}^{\dagger}_{0},\{{W}^{\dagger}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}}
and

\{{B}^{\dagger}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}}
, specific realizations of the
initial condition and the Brownian motions
driving the state and observation components of (
88
).
We also introduce
Z^{\dagger}(t)=\{{z}^{\dagger}(s)\}_{0\leq s\leq t}.

Analogously to the discrete time setting, it is natural to establish two distinct objectives, both related to recovery of the state from the observation:

•

Objective 1: design an algorithm producing output

v(t)
from
Z^{\dagger}(t)
so that

\{v(t)\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}}
estimates

\{{v}^{\dagger}(t)\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}}
, the true signal generated
by (
88
a);

•

Objective 2: design an algorithm which estimates
the distribution of random variable
v(t)|Z^{\dagger}(t)
.

As in discrete time we are interested in Markovian
formulations which update the estimate
v(t)
, or the
distribution
v(t)|Z^{\dagger}(t)
, sequentially as the data is
acquired. All of the algorithms we describe depend only on
the increments of the process
{z}^{\dagger}(t)
, hence the fixing of
{z}^{\dagger}(0)=0
is immaterial. In the next two subsections we describe control theoretic and probabilistic approaches to this problem which, respectively, provide the basis for algorithms addressing Objectives 1 and 2. Following the road-map
from the previous section in the discrete time setting,
we then proceed to study exact transport leading to mean field equations
related to the Objective 2; we then study second order approximations
of exact transport, and finally reach ensemble Kalman methods through particle approximations.

3.2
Control Theory Perspective

As for the time-discrete problem, we again start with the control theoretical approach based on the small uncertainty assumption. Specifically we assume that the three covariances appearing in (
89
)
are small so that the states and observations can be well approximated as deterministic. Thus
we initially set
\mathsf{\Sigma}
and
\mathsf{\Gamma}
to zero. In this setting
we derive a continuous time analog of the 3DVAR methodology.

Applying the rescalings (
84
) to (
3
) we obtain, in the deterministic setting,

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=v_{n}+\Delta tf(v_{n}),

(90a)

\displaystyle\widehat{z}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{z}_{n}+\Delta t\mathsf{h}(\widehat{v}_{n+1}),

(90b)

\displaystyle v_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{v}_{n+1}+K(\Delta{z}^{\dagger}_{n+1}-\Delta\widehat{z}_{n+1})

(90c)

with the observed increments from (
86
b) so that

\displaystyle\Delta{z}^{\dagger}_{n+1}

\displaystyle:={z}^{\dagger}(t_{n+1})-{z}^{\dagger}(t_{n}),

derived from a specific fixed realization of (
88
) and
t_{n}=n\Delta t
.
For fixed observed increments
\Delta{z}^{\dagger}_{n+1}
, equations (
90
) define a deterministic
map
v_{n}\mapsto v_{n+1}
. Taking the continuous time limit,
and eliminating
\widehat{z}
, we obtain the following estimator
v
for
{v}^{\dagger}
given
Z^{\dagger}:

{\mathrm{d}v}=f(v)\mathrm{d}t+K\bigl({\mathrm{d}{z}^{\dagger}}-\mathsf{h}(v)\mathrm{d}t\bigr),

(91)

where
{z}^{\dagger}
(the data) is obtained
from a specific fixed realization of (
88
):

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}{v}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=f({v}^{\dagger})\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\mathsf{\Sigma}}\mathrm{d}W^{\dagger},\quad{v}^{\dagger}(0)\sim\mathsf{N}(m_{0},C_{0}),

(92a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}{z}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{h}({v}^{\dagger})\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\mathrm{d}B^{\dagger},\quad{z}^{\dagger}(0)=0.

(92b)

Equation (
91
) defines a continuous time analog of the 3DVAR algorithm (
4
)
and
gain matrix

K
should be viewed as a parameter to be chosen. The equation
has the form of a controlled
ordinary differential equation (ODE); typically it is initialized
with
v(0)\sim\mathsf{N}(m_{0},C_{0})
.

We now include the effect of uncertainty, allowing for non-zero covariances in (
89
).
Accounting for noise in the expressions for
\widehat{v}_{n+1}
and
\widehat{z}_{n+1}
in (
90
) we obtain the following
rescaling of (
14
):

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=v_{n}+\Delta tf(v_{n})+\xi_{n},

\displaystyle\widehat{z}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{z}_{n}+\Delta t\mathsf{h}(\widehat{v}_{n+1})+\eta_{n+1},

\displaystyle v_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{v}_{n+1}+K_{n}(\Delta{z}^{\dagger}_{n+1}-\Delta\widehat{z}_{n+1}),

for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, where we assume
v_{0},\{\xi_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}

and
\{\eta_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are mutually independent
Gaussians defined by (
3.1
).
We may now formally take the
\Delta t\to 0
limit and obtain
the following continuous time analog of
(
14
), namely the controlled SDE formulation:

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=f(v)\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\mathsf{\Sigma}}\mathrm{d}W+K(\mathrm{d}{z}^{\dagger}-\mathrm{d}\widehat{z}),

(93a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\widehat{z}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{h}(v)\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\mathrm{d}B.

(93b)

where
{z}^{\dagger}
is given by (
92
). The unit Brownian motions
W,W^{\dagger},B
and
B^{\dagger}
, in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}
,
\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}
,
\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}
and
\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}
respectively, are all independent of one another.
As in the discrete time analog, encapsulated in (
14
),
the choice of a (now
time-dependent
) gain matrix
K
remains to be determined and is crucial for the success of such a methodology;
and as in discrete time, a time-evolving gain matrix is often
desirable. To determine
K
we adopt a mean field perspective, as we did in discrete time. To this end we now discuss the evolution of probability measures describing the conditional distribution of
v(t)|Z^{\dagger}(t)
.

3.3
Probabilistic Perspective

We start, in Subsection
3.3.1
, by discussing the unconditioned dynamics
and introducing the Fokker-Planck equation associated with the state space evolution.
In Subsection
3.3.2
we take the formulation of
the filtering iteration in discrete time, from Subsection
2.3
,
and take a continuous time limit to derive the Kushner-Stratonovich
equation; we study the linear Gaussian setting, and the Kalman-Bucy
filter, as a special case. In Subsection
3.3.3
we
introduce the
sample path perspective
, central to the algorithmic
perspective developed in this paper. Subsection
3.3.4
defines
notation that will be useful throughout the remainder of
this section on continuous time data assimilation.

The derivation of continuous time limits in the previous two subsections is relatively
straightforward. However, there is an important practical and theoretical issue which we need to address. As mentioned
at the start of Section
3
continuous time observations
{z}^{\dagger}(t)
