are typically an idealization of discrete time data collected at instances
\tau_{k}=k\delta
,
\delta>0
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
only.
13
13

13

Non-equally spaced data is also of relevance in this context; but we do not consider it here.
In order to make use of continuous time
algorithms and theory it is then useful to construct a continuous time
approximation
{z}^{{\dagger},\delta}
; to be concrete we will use piecewise linear interpolation. With this set-up we need to deal with two small parameters; the time-step
\Delta t
used in (
84
) to obtain
a continuous time limit, and the data sampling interval
\delta
.
The following remark addresses choices that we make in these
notes about the manner in which we take the limit

(\Delta t,\delta)\to 0
.

Remark 3.1
.

There are results for continuous time filtering which imply that the desired limiting equation can be found in either Itô or Stratonovich forms by considering different orders of the limits
\Delta t\to 0
and
\delta\to 0
; see the bibliographic remarks in Subsection
3.7
.
Many theoretical results are derived by first taking
\delta\to 0
and then
\Delta t\to 0
. From a practical and theoretical perspective, however, it is sometimes more convenient to first consider the limit
\Delta t\to 0
followed by the limit
\delta\to 0
. We will utilize the latter sequence of limits in the following subsection in order to derive a set of evolution equations for the conditional probability measure
\mu(v,t)
solving Objective 2. These equations will in turn guide our choice of the gain matrix
K
in (
93
a). We emphasize that the choice about the order in which to take the limits
(\Delta t,\delta)\to 0
is problem dependent and should be
considered carefully whenever continuous time modeling is employed.

When the data
{z}^{\dagger}
arises itself from numerical simulations of a continuous problem, then it is most convenient to set
\delta=\Delta t
, and hence
\tau_{k}=t_{k}
; this is implicitly used in the derivation of the continuous time sample path equations in the two preceding subsections.

\blacksquare

3.3.1
Unconditioned Dynamics

First consider the continuous time limit of the evolution
associated with
\mathcal{P}
from (
17
b), (
17
c) which,
with the scaling adopted in this section, is defined by

\displaystyle(\mathcal{P}\mu)(\mathrm{d}v)

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\int_{u\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}}p(u,v)\mu(\mathrm{d}u)\Bigr)\mathrm{d}v,

(94a)

\displaystyle p(u,v)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi\Delta t)^{d_{v}/2}\sqrt{{\rm det}(\mathsf{\Sigma})}}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{2\Delta t}|v-u-\Delta tf(u)|_{\mathsf{\Sigma}}^{2}\Bigr).

(94b)

Now view
r_{n}
given by (
17
a) as approximating
r(n\Delta t).

Recall that the underlying continuous time limit of the
sample path evolution is given by the SDE (
88
a). Thus
the time evolution of the probability density
r(\cdot,t)
is given by the
Fokker--Planck equation

14
14

14

Here, and in what follows, we use the standard
notation from continuum mechanics for the divergence of vector
and second order tensor fields, and for the gradient of scalar and vector fields;
see the bibliography Subsection
3.7
for references.

\displaystyle\partial_{t}r=-\nabla\cdot(rf)+\frac{1}{2}\nabla\cdot(\nabla\cdot(r\mathsf{\Sigma})).

(95)

Note that, as in discrete time, this evolution is linear and decoupled from the state
space evolution (
88
a). We refer to the latter as a
continuous time Markov process.

3.3.2
The Filtering Distribution

As in discrete time, we now consider the evolution equation for the state conditioned on
observations. Our starting point here is the iteration on measures,
the filtering cycle, defined by (
23
),
under the scalings (
84
).
We assume that
\delta
is an integer multiple of
\Delta t
so that the
\{\tau_{k}\}
are a subset of
the
\{t_{n}\}.
In what follows we will first fix
\delta
and let
\Delta t\to 0
; in order to obtain the integer multiple property we thus consider
\Delta t\to 0
along a subsequence. We replace the true observation path
{z}^{\dagger}(t)
by
its piecewise linear approximation
{z}^{{\dagger},\delta}(t)
based on linear interpolation of values

\{{z}^{\dagger}(\tau_{k})\}
. To be precise we assume that the derivative is

cadlag
.
15
15

15

Continuous from the right, limits exist from
the left.
We then have that the implied observation increments
\Delta{z}^{\dagger}_{n+1}
are constant over the time intervals
[t_{n},t_{n+1})
and are given by

\displaystyle\Delta{z}^{\dagger}_{n+1}=\frac{\mathrm{d}{z}^{{\dagger},\delta}}{\mathrm{d}t}(t_{n})\Delta t.

(96)

In this setting, the operators
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{L}_{n}
, defined by (
23
a) and (
23
b), respectively, become

\displaystyle(\mathcal{P}\mu)(\mathrm{d}v)

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\int_{u\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}}p(u,v)\mu(\mathrm{d}u)\Bigr)\mathrm{d}v,

(97a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mu)(\mathrm{d}v)

\displaystyle=q(v,\Delta{z}^{\dagger}_{n+1})\mu(\mathrm{d}v)\Big/\Bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}}q(v,\Delta{z}^{\dagger}_{n+1})\mu(\mathrm{d}v)\Bigr),

(97b)

where, from (
18
) with the scalings (
84
),(
86
b),

\displaystyle p(u,v)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi\Delta t)^{d_{v}/2}\sqrt{{\rm det}(\mathsf{\Sigma})}}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{2\Delta t}|v-u-\Delta tf(u)|_{\mathsf{\Sigma}}^{2}\Bigr),

(98a)

\displaystyle q(v,\Delta z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi\Delta t)^{d_{y}/2}\sqrt{{\rm det}(\mathsf{\Gamma})}}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{2\Delta t}|\Delta z-\Delta t\mathsf{h}(v)|_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}^{2}\Bigr).

(98b)

With these formulae in hand we may now derive the continuous time analog of (
23
), for
\mu(v,t)
.

For ease of exposition we
assume that
\mu
has density
\rho
and derive the
equation satisfied by
\rho.
To do this we employ
the
split-step principle
: we find the
continuous time evolution equation
associated with each of
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{L}_{n}
(equations
(
97
a) and (
97
b) respectively)
separately, and then add the right-hand sides of the resulting
evolution equations to obtain the desired continuous time limit
resulting from the composition of
\mathcal{L}_{n}
and
\mathcal{P}
. We use
r
as
a dummy variable to denote
the density being evolved, for both of the split-steps, and in both discrete
(
r_{n}
) and continuous (
r(t)
) time, in what follows.

First recall that the continuous time limit of the evolution
associated with
\mathcal{P}
, as defined by (
97
a) and (
98
a).
is given by the Fokker–Planck equation (
95
).
Secondly, consider the second component of the split-step argument: we determine a
continuous time limit of the evolution
associated with
\mathcal{L}_{n}
as described by (
97
b) and (
98
b). The following lemma presents an evolution equation for
r
associated with
\mathcal{L}_{n}
, describing how observation of the piecewise continuous interpolated data
{z}^{{\dagger},\delta}(t)
changes the density
r(t,v)
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Assume that
\mathsf{\Gamma}\succ 0.
The continuous time limit of the evolution
associated with
\mathcal{L}_{n}
, as described by (
97
b) and (
98
b), is given by

\displaystyle\partial_{t}r=\left\langle\mathsf{h}-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h},\frac{\mathrm{d}{z}^{{\dagger},\delta}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}r-\frac{1}{2}\left\{\left|\mathsf{h}\right|^{2}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}-\mathbb{E}\left|\mathsf{h}\right|^{2}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\right\}r.

(99)

\Diamond

Proof 3.3
.

Consider the discrete-time evolution

\displaystyle r_{n+1}=\mathcal{L}_{n}r_{n},

(100)

where
\mathcal{L}_{n}
is defined by (
97
b) and (
98
b). By Taylor expansion we have

\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\Delta t}\left|\Delta{z}^{\dagger}_{n+1}-\Delta t\mathsf{h}(v)\right|_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}^{2}\right)=1-\frac{\Delta t}{2}\left|\frac{\Delta{z}^{\dagger}_{n+1}}{\Delta t}-\mathsf{h}(v)\right|^{2}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2}).

Then, using expressions (
97
b) and (
98
b), we obtain

(\mathcal{L}_{n}r_{n})(v)=\frac{1}{C(\Delta t)}\left(1-\frac{\Delta t}{2}\left|\frac{\Delta{z}^{\dagger}_{n+1}}{\Delta t}-\mathsf{h}(v)\right|^{2}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})\right)r_{n}(v),

(101)

where

C(\Delta t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}}\left(1-\frac{\Delta t}{2}\left|\frac{\Delta{z}^{\dagger}_{n+1}}{\Delta t}-\mathsf{h}(v)\right|^{2}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})\right)r_{n}(v)\mathrm{d}v.

By integrating and noting that
r_{n}
is a density, it follows that

C(\Delta t)=1-\frac{\Delta t}{2}\mathbb{E}\left|\frac{\Delta{z}^{\dagger}_{n+1}}{\Delta t}-\mathsf{h}(v)\right|^{2}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2}),

(102)

where expectation is with respect to
v
distributed as random
variable with density
r_{n}
. Hence, combining (
101
) and (
100
) we obtain, to leading order in
\Delta t
,

r_{n+1}=r_{n}\left\{1+\Delta t\left\langle\mathsf{h}-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h},\frac{\mathrm{d}{z}^{{\dagger},\delta}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}-\frac{\Delta t}{2}\left\{\left|\mathsf{h}\right|^{2}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}-\mathbb{E}\left|\mathsf{h}\right|^{2}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\right\}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})\right\};

here we have used that the data increments
\Delta{z}^{\dagger}_{n+1}
are given by (
96
) for fixed
\delta
. Taking the time continuous limit
\Delta t\to 0
with fixed observation interval
\delta>0
leads to the evolution equation (
99
).

Now taking the
\delta\to 0
limit in (
99
), we obtain the
following nonlocal nonlinear stochastic evolution equation for density
r(v,t):

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}r=\left\langle\mathsf{h}-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h},\circ\mathrm{d}{z}^{\dagger}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}r-\frac{1}{2}\left\{\left|\mathsf{h}\right|^{2}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}-\mathbb{E}\left|\mathsf{h}\right|^{2}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\right\}r\mathrm{d}t.

(103)

Here
\circ
denotes Stratonovitch integration; this form of integration arises
in the limit
\delta\to 0
because the equation
is derived by making a smooth approximation
{z}^{{\dagger},\delta}
of
{z}^{\dagger}
and passing to
the limit. Recall that
{z}^{\dagger}
is given by (
92
).
The equation is nonlocal and nonlinear because
\mathbb{E}
denotes
expectation at time
t
with respect to density
r(\cdot,t).

We now wish to invoke the split-step principle and combine the evolutions
(
95
) and (
103
). However before doing this
we proceed to convert the equation (
103
) into its more common
Itô representation. For this, the following lemma is crucial.
In proving it we will use the concepts of quadratic variation
and covariation. For an introduction to these concepts consult the lecture notes by Eberle, which are referenced in the bibliography Subsection
3.7
.
We note that quadratic variation and covariation are first defined between scalar-valued process and
can then be lifted to define: (i) the covariation of an
inner-product
\langle x,y\rangle
between vector processes
x

and
y
, which is scalar-valued; (ii) the quadratic variation of vector process
x
, which is matrix-valued; and (iii) the covariation of vector process
x
with scalar process
z
, which is vector-valued.

Use of quadratic variation and covariation leads to a succinct, streamlined proof. Furthermore, in Appendix
10
, we provide explicit calculations
for the reader who is interested in understanding the details of the conversion by means of the definitions of Itô and Stratonovitch integrals as limits. In particular, the concepts underlying the quadratic variation and covariation calculations in the following are derived from first principles in Lemma
10.1
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Assume that
\mathsf{\Gamma}\succ 0
and that
{z}^{\dagger}
is given by (
92
). The Itô and Stratonovich interpretations of the stochastic forcing term in (
103
) are related through

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathsf{h}-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h},\circ\mathrm{d}{z}^{\dagger}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}r-\frac{1}{2}\left\{\left|\mathsf{h}\right|^{2}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}-\mathbb{E}\left|\mathsf{h}\right|^{2}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\right\}r\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathsf{h}-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h},\mathrm{d}{z}^{\dagger}-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h}\mathrm{d}t\right\rangle_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}r.

\Diamond

Proof 3.5
.

Using the formula for the Itô–Stratonovich conversion
between two semimartingales, we have

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathsf{h}-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h},\circ\mathrm{d}{z}^{\dagger}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}r=\left\langle\mathsf{h}-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h},\mathrm{d}{z}^{\dagger}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}r+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{d}\left[\left\langle(\mathsf{h}-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h})r,{z}^{\dagger}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\right],

(104)

where
[\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle]
denotes covariation of the
inner-product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
.
Note that (
92
b) implies that the quadratic variation of
{z}^{\dagger}
is given by the matrix identity

[{z}^{\dagger},{z}^{\dagger}]=t\mathsf{\Gamma}
. Furthermore, because of (
103
), the conversion formula (
104
) and the fact that
\mathrm{d}[{z}^{\dagger},{z}^{\dagger}]=\mathsf{\Gamma}\mathrm{d}t
, it follows that the covariation between scalar
r
and vector
{z}^{\dagger}
satisfies the vector identity

\mathrm{d}[r,{z}^{\dagger}]=(\mathsf{h}-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h})r\mathrm{d}t.

(105)

Consider
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{y}}
so that derivative
f^{\prime}(r)
may be identified with an element in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{y}}.
Then, for any such differentiable
f
,

\mathrm{d}[\langle f(r),{z}^{\dagger}\rangle_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}]=\langle f^{\prime}(r),\mathrm{d}[r,{z}^{\dagger}]\rangle_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}.

(106)

We now apply this identity in the setting where
f(r)=(\mathsf{h}-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h})r
, noting that
r
defines
the expectation in this definition. Thus

f^{\prime}(r)\,\delta r=(\mathsf{h}-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h})\,\delta r-\Bigl(\int\mathsf{h}\,\delta r\,\mathrm{d}v\Bigr)r.

Hence the covariation in (
104
) satisfies, using (
106
) for the first equality and (
105
) for the second equality,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}[\langle(\mathsf{h}-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h})r,{z}^{\dagger}\rangle_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}]

\displaystyle=\langle\mathsf{h}-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h},\mathrm{d}[r,{z}^{\dagger}]\rangle_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}-\Bigl(\int\langle\mathsf{h},\mathrm{d}[r,{z}^{\dagger}]\rangle_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\mathrm{d}v\Bigr)r

\displaystyle=\left|\mathsf{h}-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h}\right|^{2}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}r\mathrm{d}t-\,\mathbb{E}\bigl(\langle\mathsf{h},\mathsf{h}-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h}\rangle_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\bigr)r\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\left\{\left|\mathsf{h}-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h}\right|^{2}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}-\mathbb{E}\left|\mathsf{h}-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h}\right|^{2}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\right\}r\mathrm{d}t.

Using this identity in (
104
) and rearranging, we find that

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathsf{h}-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h},\circ\mathrm{d}{z}^{\dagger}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}r-\frac{1}{2}\left\{\left|\mathsf{h}\right|^{2}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}-\mathbb{E}\left|\mathsf{h}\right|^{2}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\right\}r\mathrm{d}t=\left\langle\mathsf{h}-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h},\mathrm{d}{z}^{\dagger}-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h}\mathrm{d}t\right\rangle_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}r,

which in turn leads to the following desired
Itô representation of (
103
):

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}r=\left\langle\mathsf{h}-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h},\mathrm{d}{z}^{\dagger}-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h}\mathrm{d}t\right\rangle_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}r.

(107)

Using the Itô form of the equation from the preceding lemma, the Fokker-Planck equation (
95
)
and the split-step principle delivers the following:

Theorem 3.6
.

Assume that
\mathsf{\Gamma}\succ 0

and that
{z}^{\dagger}
is given by (
92
). The time evolution of the density
\rho(\cdot,t)
for the random variable
v(t)|Z^{\dagger}(t)
is characterized by the Itô SPDE

\mathrm{d}\rho=-\nabla\cdot(\rho f)\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{2}\nabla\cdot(\nabla\cdot(\rho\mathsf{\Sigma}))\mathrm{d}t+\left\langle\mathsf{h}-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h},\mathrm{d}{z}^{\dagger}-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h}\mathrm{d}t\right\rangle_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\rho.

(108)

\Diamond

Equation (
108
) is known as the Kushner–Stratonovich equation. The equation (
108
) is to be interpreted
in the Itô sense with respect to the driving noise
{z}^{\dagger}(t)
. The equation is nonlinear, unlike the unconditioned dynamics governed
by the Fokker-Planck equation (
95
).
The corresponding Stratonovich formulation follows immediately from (
103
) in combination with (
95
):

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\rho

\displaystyle=\Bigl(-\nabla\cdot(\rho f)+\frac{1}{2}\nabla\cdot(\nabla\cdot(\rho\mathsf{\Sigma}))-\frac{1}{2}\left\{\left|\mathsf{h}\right|^{2}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}-\mathbb{E}\left|\mathsf{h}\right|^{2}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\right\}\rho\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t

(109a)

\displaystyle\hskip 198.7425pt+\left\langle\mathsf{h}-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h},\circ\mathrm{d}{z}^{\dagger}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\rho.

(109b)

Example 3.7
.

We consider the setting where

f(\cdot)=F\cdot,\quad\mathsf{h}(\cdot)=\mathsf{H}\cdot.

(110)

in equations (
88
) so that

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=Fv\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\mathsf{\Sigma}}\mathrm{d}W,\quad v(0)\sim\mathsf{N}(m_{0},C_{0}),

(111a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=\mathsf{H}v\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\mathrm{d}B,\quad z(0)=0.

(111b)

Now consider data
{z}^{\dagger}(t)
generated by

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}{v}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=F{v}^{\dagger}\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\mathsf{\Sigma}}\mathrm{d}W^{\dagger},\quad{v}^{\dagger}(0)\sim\mathsf{N}(m_{0},C_{0}),

(112a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}{z}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{H}{v}^{\dagger}\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\mathrm{d}B^{\dagger},\quad{z}^{\dagger}(0)=0.

(112b)

We are interested in he filtering distribution for
v(t)|{Z}^{\dagger}(t)
.

Becase of the linearity and additive Gaussian noise, this
filtering distribution is Gaussian and is
given by the Kalman-Bucy filter, the continuous time
analog of the Kalman filter described in Example
2.6
.
Indeed if
\mathsf{\Gamma}\succ 0
in (
111
)
then the density
\rho(\cdot,t)
associated with the random variable

v(t)|Z^{\dagger}(t)
, with
{z}^{\dagger}
given by (
112
),
is Gaussian with mean
m(\cdot)

and covariance
C(\cdot)
given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}m

\displaystyle=Fm\mathrm{d}t+C\mathsf{H}^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}\left(\mathrm{d}z^{{\dagger}}-\mathsf{H}m\mathrm{d}t\right),\quad m(0)=m_{0},

(113a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}C

\displaystyle=FC\mathrm{d}t+CF^{\top}\mathrm{d}t+\mathsf{\Sigma}\mathrm{d}t-C\mathsf{H}^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}\mathsf{H}C\mathrm{d}t,C(0)=C_{0}.

(113b)

These equations for the mean and covariance of
the Kalman-Bucy filter may be obtained by taking the continuous time limit of the Kalman filter from Example
2.6
under the scalings (
84
b), (
85
) and (
86
b).

The Kalman-Bucy filter also yields an exact solution of the
Kushner-Stratonovich equation (
108
) under (
110
).
Indeed, if
\rho(\cdot,0)
is initialized at the Gaussian
with mean
m_{0}
and covariance
C_{0}
then
\rho(\cdot,t)
solving the Kushner-Stratonovich equation (
108
) has solution given by the Gaussian
\mathsf{N}\bigl(m(t),C(t)\bigr).

\blacksquare

3.3.3
The Sample Path Perspective

Similarly to the discrete time setting, a key idea in developing algorithms
for filtering in continuous time is the
sample path perspective.

We will seek to identify (for implementable algorithms only approximately) mean field SDEs
with the property that their solution
v(t)
, has
\textit{Law}\,\bigl(v(t)\bigr)
equal to that given by the density
r
governed by the Kushner-Stratonovich equation (
108
). Analogously to discrete time the mean field SDEs will couple to the solution of the equation for evolution of the density; the resulting processes are termed
nonlinear Markov processes.

We will first introduce Gaussian projected filters and then discuss mean field models
derived through the sample path perspective. To these ends we now introduce some
important notational conventions.

3.3.4
Important Repeatedly Used Notation

The notation we define here is used primarily to discuss
exact and approximate mean field models, here evolving in
continuous time, to (approximately) solve the filtering problem.
In discrete time expectations may be taken under the law of
the (possibly approximate) discretely evolving mean field model,
or under the predictive distribution found from pushing
this law forward under the model.
This distinction disappears in continuous time and we simply
need to compute expectations under the (possibly approximate)
continuously evolving mean field models that we will introduce later. To this end we define, with

m:=\mathbb{E}v
,

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}\bigl((v-m)\otimes(v-m)\bigr),

(114a)

\displaystyle C^{vf}

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\bigl(v-m\bigr)\otimes\bigl(f(v)-\mathbb{E}f(v)\bigr)\Bigr),

(114b)

\displaystyle C^{v\mathsf{h}}

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\bigl(v-m\bigr)\otimes\bigl(\mathsf{h}(v)-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h}(v)\bigr)\Bigr).

(114c)

All expectations are under the mean field model for
v
.
The covariances should be viewed as functions of time
t
.
In deriving continuous time models
from discrete time models, we will also use discrete
time analogs, computed under the law of random variable
v_{n}
and denoted

C_{n},C_{n}^{vf}
and
C_{n}^{v\mathsf{h}}.

3.4
Gaussian Projected Filtering Distribution

As in discrete time, the Gaussian projected filtering distribution
plays an important conceptual role in understanding later filtering
algorithms. The evolution equations for the mean
m
and the covariance matrix
C
follow naturally from a continuous time limit of the associated discrete time filtering formulations. We summarize the resulting equations in the following:

Theorem 3.8
.

Assume that
\mathsf{\Gamma}\succ 0.
and that
{z}^{\dagger}
is given by (
92
). Consider the discrete time Gaussian projected filter, namely map from
(m_{n},C_{n})
to
(m_{n+1},C_{n+1})
defined by choosing
v_{n}\sim\mathsf{N}(m_{n},C_{n})
and then using (
28
), (
31
), (
34
) and
(
41
). Under the rescalings (
84
), and in the limit
\Delta t\to 0
, we obtain the following continuous-time limit of this map:

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}m

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}f(v)\mathrm{d}t+C^{v\mathsf{h}}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}\bigl(\mathrm{d}{z}^{\dagger}-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h}(v)\mathrm{d}t\bigr),

(115a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}C

\displaystyle=C^{vf}\mathrm{d}t+(C^{vf})^{\top}\mathrm{d}t+\mathsf{\Sigma}\mathrm{d}t-C^{v\mathsf{h}}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}(C^{v\mathsf{h}})^{\top}\mathrm{d}t,

(115b)

where the expectations are computed under
\mathsf{N}\bigl(m(t),C(t)\bigr)
, using (
114
).

\Diamond

Remark 3.9
.

Note that the preceding equation implicitly defines gain matrix

K=C^{v\mathsf{h}}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}.

(116)

This specific choice of gain will play a central role in what follows.

\blacksquare

Proof 3.10
.

(Theorem
3.8
)
The Gaussian projected filter is defined by
evolution of mean and covariance given by equations
(
31
), (
34
) and
(
41
), repeated and reordered here for convenience:

\displaystyle\widehat{m}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Psi(v_{n}),

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\bigl(\Psi(v_{n})-\widehat{m}_{n+1})\otimes\bigl(\Psi(v_{n})-\widehat{m}_{n+1}\bigr)\Bigr)+\Sigma,

\displaystyle m_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{m}_{n+1}+\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{vh}(\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{hh}+\Gamma)^{-1}\bigl({y}^{\dagger}_{n+1}-\mathbb{E}\widehat{h}_{n+1}\bigr),

\displaystyle C_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{C}_{n+1}-\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{vh}(\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{hh}+\Gamma)^{-1}\left({\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{vh}}\right)^{\top},

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{vh}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\bigl(\widehat{v}_{n+1}-\mathbb{E}\widehat{v}_{n+1}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(\widehat{h}_{n+1}-\mathbb{E}\widehat{h}_{n+1}\bigr)\Bigr),

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{hh}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\bigl(\widehat{h}_{n+1}-\mathbb{E}\widehat{h}_{n+1}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(\widehat{h}_{n+1}-\mathbb{E}\widehat{h}_{n+1}\bigr)\Bigr).

Recall that
\widehat{h}_{n+1}:=h(\widehat{v}_{n+1}).
Expectations in the prediction step
are with respect to the law of
v_{n}\sim\mathsf{N}(m_{n},C_{n});
and expectations in the analysis
step are with respect to the law of
\widehat{v}_{n+1}
given by (
28
a),
assuming that
v_{n}\sim\mathsf{N}(m_{n},C_{n});
thus
\textit{Law}\,(\widehat{v}_{n+1})=\mathcal{P}\,\textit{Law}\,(v_{n})
.

We now impose the rescalings (
84
) on these equations.
The reader’s attention is drawn to the fact that
h=\Delta t\mathsf{h}
when reading the next formula in order to understand the scalings with
\Delta t;
similar notational shift from regular to mathsf occurs in other formulae that follow it and are crucial to understanding
orders of magnitude with respect to
\Delta t.

Under the rescalings and
using that
\xi_{n+1}=\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{\frac{1}{2}})
has mean zero,

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{vh}

\displaystyle=\Delta t\mathbb{E}\left(\bigl(v_{n}-\mathbb{E}v_{n}+\xi_{n}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t)\bigr)\otimes\bigl(\mathsf{h}(v_{n})-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h}(v_{n})+D\mathsf{h}(v_{n})\xi_{n}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t)\bigr)\right)

\displaystyle=\Delta t\mathbb{E}\left(\bigl(v_{n}-\mathbb{E}v_{n}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(\mathsf{h}(v_{n})-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h}(v_{n})\bigr)\right)+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})

\displaystyle=\Delta tC^{v\mathsf{h}}_{n}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2}).

Similarly

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{hh}

\displaystyle=\Delta t^{2}\mathbb{E}\left(\bigl(\mathsf{h}(v_{n})-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h}(v_{n})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{\frac{1}{2}})\bigr)\otimes\bigl(\mathsf{h}(v_{n})-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h}(v_{n})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{\frac{1}{2}})\bigr)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2}).

Thus, since
\Gamma=\Delta t\mathsf{\Gamma}
,

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{vh}(\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{hh}+\Gamma)^{-1}

\displaystyle=C^{v\mathsf{h}}_{n}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t),

(117a)

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{vh}(\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{hh}+\Gamma)^{-1}\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{vh}

\displaystyle=\Delta tC^{v\mathsf{h}}_{n}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}(C^{v\mathsf{h}}_{n})^{\top}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2}).

(117b)

Furthermore, since
\Psi(v_{n})=v_{n}+\Delta tf(v_{n})
and
\widehat{m}_{n+1}=\mathbb{E}v_{n}+\Delta t\mathbb{E}f(v_{n})
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\bigl(\Psi(v_{n})

\displaystyle-\widehat{m}_{n+1}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(\Psi(v_{n})-\widehat{m}_{n+1}\bigr)\Bigr)=C_{n}+\Delta tC_{n}^{vf}+\Delta t(C_{n}^{vf})^{\top}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2}).

Using these approximations, and the fact that

\Delta{z}^{\dagger}_{n+1}=\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{\frac{1}{2}})
,
we obtain

\displaystyle\widehat{m}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=m_{n}+\Delta t\mathbb{E}f(v_{n}),

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=C_{n}+\Delta tC_{n}^{vf}+\Delta t(C_{n}^{vf})^{\top}+\Delta t\mathsf{\Sigma}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2}),

\displaystyle m_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{m}_{n+1}+C^{v\mathsf{h}}_{n}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}\bigl(\Delta{z}^{\dagger}_{n+1}-\Delta t\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h}(\widehat{v}_{n+1})\bigr)+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{\frac{3}{2}}),

\displaystyle C_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{C}_{n+1}-\Delta tC^{v\mathsf{h}}_{n}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}\left(C^{v\mathsf{h}}_{n}\right)^{\top}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2}),

\displaystyle C^{vf}_{n}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl((v_{n}-m_{n})\otimes(f(v_{n})-\mathbb{E}f(v_{n}))\bigr),

\displaystyle C^{v\mathsf{h}}_{n}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl((v_{n}-m_{n})\otimes(\mathsf{h}(v_{n})-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h}(v_{n}))\bigr).

In the following
C^{v\mathsf{h}},C^{vf}
are functions of time, defined by (
114
), and
C_{n}^{v\mathsf{h}},C_{n}^{vf}
are discrete time
analogs computed under the law of
v_{n}.

Combining the prediction and analysis steps we find that

\displaystyle m_{n+1}

\displaystyle=m_{n}+\Delta t\mathbb{E}f(v_{n})+C_{n}^{v\mathsf{h}}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}\bigl(\Delta{z}^{\dagger}_{n+1}-\Delta t\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h}(\widehat{v}_{n+1})\bigr)+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{\frac{3}{2}}),

\displaystyle C_{n+1}

\displaystyle=C_{n}+\Delta tC^{vf}_{n}+\Delta t\left(C^{vf}_{n}\right)^{\top}+\Delta t\mathsf{\Sigma}-\Delta tC^{v\mathsf{h}}_{n}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}\left(C^{v\mathsf{h}}_{n}\right)^{\top}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2}).

Taking the
\Delta t\rightarrow 0
limit, we deduce the continuous-time analog of the equations (
31
), (
34
), namely (
115
) as desired.

Example 3.11
.

In the linear setting (
110
), the Gaussian projected filter
(
115
) reduces to the Kalman-Bucy filter from
Example
3.7
.

3.5
Mean Field Evolution Equations

In the preceding subsection we approximated the evolution
of the filtering distribution by the evolution of a Gaussian.
Here we take a different approach, seeking a sample path
perspective, identifying mean field SDEs which (possibly only
approximately) have solutions with law given by the filtering distribution.
We develop a continuous time analog of the discrete
time mean field approach from Subsection
2.5
.

In Subsection
3.5.1
we describe work concerning the
derivation of explicit mean field SDEs equal in law to the
filtering distribution; this is a departure from our discussion
of this topic in discrete time where no explicit maps were
identified in the general setting. Subsections
3.5.2

and
3.5.3
describe a variety of explicit approximate
mean field SDEs, based on matching first and second order moment information,
and arising from rescaling of the discrete time setting.

3.5.1
Perfect Transport

Here we seek a mean field dynamical system with law given
by that of the Kushner-Stratonovich equation. The
analog of the discrete-time transport map
T^{S}_{n}
, is
to find transport evolution equations for
v
and
\widehat{z}

in the form a mean field SDE. We will seek to achieve
this in the specific sample path form

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=f(v)\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\mathsf{\Sigma}}\mathrm{d}W+a(v;\rho)\mathrm{d}t+K(v;\rho)(\mathrm{d}{z}^{\dagger}-\mathrm{d}\widehat{z}),

(118a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\widehat{z}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{h}(v)\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\mathrm{d}B,

(118b)

where
\rho
is the time-dependent density of
v
. Note that
(
118
b) simply generates simulated data from the state
v
.
On the other hand (
118
a) combines the underlying known
model evolution with a nudged innovation term, based on the
difference between the observed and simulated data, and a correction
to the drift
f
. The nudging and correction terms are defined by
gain
K
and drift correction
a
.
The goal is to chose drift correction
a
and gain
K
such that the induced time evolution of the density
\rho
of
v
agrees with the density

\rho
given by the Kushner-Stratonovich equation (
108
).
We thus find a controlled SDE, similar in form to that proposed in
(
93
) but with an additional drift term and with mean field dependence.

Remark 3.12
.

We emphasize that, as in the discrete-time transport formulation,
there is a considerable degree of non-uniqueness in the choice
of transport in general, and here in the choice of
a
and
K

specifically. We make specific, simple, choices in the theorem that follows.
Working in continuous time enables very explicit identification of
exact mean field models; this is not possible in discrete time.

\blacksquare

The following theorem identifies a sample path perspective that
exactly captures evolution of the filtering distribution.

Theorem 3.13
.

Assume that
\mathsf{\Gamma}\succ 0
and that there exists
K=K(v;\rho)
satisfying the identity

\displaystyle-\nabla\cdot(\rho K^{\top})=\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}(\mathsf{h}-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h})\rho.

(119)

Consider the stochastic mean field dynamics given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=f(v)\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\mathsf{\Sigma}}\mathrm{d}W+\nabla\cdot(K\mathsf{\Gamma}K^{\top})\mathrm{d}t-K\mathsf{\Gamma}\nabla\cdot K^{\top}\mathrm{d}t+K(\mathrm{d}{z}^{\dagger}-\mathrm{d}\widehat{z}),

(120a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\widehat{z}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{h}(v)\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\mathrm{d}B,

(120b)

where
{z}^{\dagger}
given by (
92
).
Assume that solution
v
has law with smooth density and that
the Kushner-Stratonovich equation (
108
) has smooth
density as solution. Then the law of
v
has density given
by the Kushner-Stratonovich equation (
108
).

\Diamond

Proof 3.14
.

To simplify calculations we will first look at choosing
a
and
K

to get agreement, at the level of densities of
v
,
with (
107
). A straightforward modification, using
the split-step principle again, then
provides the generalization to (
108
).
This split-step approach enables us to consider only the case where
f(\cdot)\equiv 0
and
\mathsf{\Sigma}=0.

Note that, in (
118
),
{z}^{\dagger}
is a fixed, given, trajectory
and we are interested in the evolution of the probability density
induced for
v
by the randomness over the distribution on trajectories
\widehat{z}
. Using (
118
b)
in (
118
a), and recalling that it suffices to set
f
and
\mathsf{\Sigma}
to zero, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=a(v;\rho)\mathrm{d}t+K(v;\rho)\bigl(\mathrm{d}{z}^{\dagger}-\mathsf{h}(v)\mathrm{d}t-\sqrt{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\mathrm{d}B\bigr).

(121)

Applying Fokker-Planck analysis, modified to the mean field
setting, shows that the time evolution of the density
\rho
of
v
under (
118
) is provided by the
nonlinear (because of dependence of
a,K
on
\rho
) SPDE
16
16

16

Here we use the standard convention from
continuum mechanics that the divergence of a matrix is to be
interpreted via computation of derivatives with respect to the second index; see
Subsection
3.7
for references to relevant continuum mechanics
textbooks. Strictly speaking equation (
122
) is an SPDE only if we now view
{z}^{\dagger}
as a random variable rather than a fixed realization.
to be interpreted in the Itô sense:

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\rho

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot(\rho(a-K\mathsf{h}))\mathrm{d}t-\langle\nabla\cdot(\rho K^{\top}),\mathrm{d}{z}^{\dagger}\rangle+\nabla\cdot(\nabla\cdot(\rho K\mathsf{\Gamma}K^{\top}))\mathrm{d}t.

(122)

Note that, although
{z}^{\dagger}
is a fixed trajectory, it contributes to the diffusion term in this Fokker-Planck equation, explaining the factor
1
rather than
1/2.
This arises as a contribution from
the quadratic variation of the path of
{z}^{\dagger}
to the evolution of
\rho.
For further details on the derivation of (
122
) see Lemma
10.3
in Appendix
10
. To get agreement with (
107
),
a
and
K
have to be chosen such that

\displaystyle\rho\langle(\mathsf{h}-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h}),\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}(\mathrm{d}{z}^{\dagger}-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h}\mathrm{d}t)\rangle=

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\nabla\cdot(\rho(a-K\mathsf{h}))\mathrm{d}t-\langle\nabla\cdot(\rho K^{\top}),\mathrm{d}{z}^{\dagger}\rangle+\nabla\cdot(\nabla\cdot(\rho K\mathsf{\Gamma}K^{\top}))\mathrm{d}t.

Equating the two terms involving the data

{z}^{\dagger}
shows immediately that
K(v;\rho)
has to satisfy the (vector-valued) PDE
(
119
).
From this, equating the terms that do not involve the data
{z}^{\dagger}
,
it follows that
a(v;\rho)
has to satisfy

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot(\rho a)

\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot(\nabla\cdot(\rho K\mathsf{\Gamma}K^{\top}))+\nabla\cdot(\rho K(\mathsf{h}-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h}))

\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot(\nabla\cdot(\rho K\mathsf{\Gamma}K^{\top}))-\nabla\cdot(K\mathsf{\Gamma}\nabla\cdot(\rho K^{\top}))

\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot\left(\rho\left(\nabla\cdot(K\mathsf{\Gamma}K^{\top})-K\mathsf{\Gamma}\nabla\cdot K^{\top}\right)\right).

A natural choice for
a
is provided by asking that the term
on which the divergence acts is zero. This yields

\displaystyle a=\nabla\cdot(K\mathsf{\Gamma}K^{\top})-K\mathsf{\Gamma}\nabla\cdot K^{\top},

(123)

a solution for
a
with no explicit dependence on
\rho;
note, however, that
a
does depend on
\rho
implicitly through
the dependence of
K
on
\rho.

With these choices of
a,K
, and applying the split-step principle
so that the mean field dynamics are consistent with (
108
)
rather than (
107
), we obtain a version of the
feedback particle filter; in particular we find
the equation in its stochastic mean field formulation given by
(
120
) where
K=K(v;\rho)
solves (
119
)
and
\rho
evolves according to the Kushner-Stratonovich
equation (
108
), an equation which also defines the law of
v.

Remark 3.15
.

There is an interesting interpretation of the contribution
a

to the drift term in (
120
): it is simply the Itô-to-Stratonovich-like correction with regard to the
v
-dependence in
K(v;\rho)
. However there is a subtlety that the equation for
\rho
itself depends on the data
{z}^{\dagger}
and
the correction does not account for the
\rho-
dependence of

K(v;\rho)
– the Stratonovich correction is only with respect
to
v-
dependence of the drift; an Itô interpretation is retained
with respect to the
\rho
dependence. We refer to Appendix
10.3
for discussion of this unusual form of stochastic integration. There we also derive the full Stratonovich correction (with respect to both
v
and
\rho
dependence) of the exact mean field model.

\blacksquare

Remark 3.16
.

The mean field equations (
120
) require a gain
K(v;\rho)
which satisfies (
119
). Let
\mathbb{E}
denote expectation with respect to random variable
v
distributed according to probability measure with density
\rho
. Using appropriately regular test functions
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}
, chosen to have mean-zero under
\mathbb{E}
, equation (
119
) can be rephrased in the weak form

17
17

17

Recall that the conventions from continuum mechanics that we use to define the divergence and gradient of vector fields are discussed in the bibliography Subsection
3.7
.

\mathbb{E}\left(K^{\top}\nabla\psi\right)=\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}C^{\mathsf{h}\psi};

(124)

here
C^{\mathsf{h}\psi}
denotes the covariance between
\mathsf{h}
under
\mathbb{E}

and, in what follows, we also let
C^{v\mathsf{h}}
denote covariance between
v
and
\mathsf{h}
. The particular choice
\psi(v)=v-\mathbb{E}v
leads to

\mathbb{E}K=C^{v\mathsf{h}}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}.

(125)

Note, in particular, that this identity can be satisfied by making the
constant gain
ansatz that
K
is independent of
v
and is then given by

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle=C^{v\mathsf{h}}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1},

(126a)

\displaystyle C^{v\mathsf{h}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\bigl(v-\mathbb{E}v\bigr)\otimes\bigl(\mathsf{h}(v)-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h}(v)\bigr)\Bigr).

(126b)

More generally speaking, we note that (
124
) is amenable to numerical approximations. This will be discussed further in Subsection
3.6.1
below.

\blacksquare

We close this subsection with the mean field formulation using deterministic innovations:

\mathrm{d}v=f(v)\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\mathsf{\Sigma}}\mathrm{d}W+\frac{1}{2}\left(\nabla\cdot(K\mathsf{\Gamma}K^{\top})-K\mathsf{\Gamma}\nabla\cdot K^{\top}\right)\mathrm{d}t+K\left(\mathrm{d}{z}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}(\mathsf{h}+\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h})\mathrm{d}t\right),

(127)

where
{z}^{\dagger}
is given by (
92
).
The relationship between this equation and (
120
),
is analogous to the relationship between innovation terms (
61
) and (
15
) already encountered in the discrete time setting. See the bibliography
Subsection
3.7
for discussion on the historical development
of the various exact mean field models presented here.

3.5.2
Second Order Transport – Stochastic Case

Recall that, in the discrete-time setting, there are an uncountable set of maps which effect
approximate transport, in the sense of matching the first
and second order statistics of the analysis map, using either
stochastic or deterministic models. These are elucidated in Appendix
9
. We, however, concentrated in the main text on a handful of examples. In this and the next subsection we study continuous time analogs of some of these examples, starting with the Kalman transport map (
54
) recalled here, and reformulated, for convenience:
18
18

18

The Gaussian notation
used in the first two equations is shorthand for the first two equations appearing in (
14
), together with the
assumptions detailed following those equations. Thus we use
\mathsf{N}(0,\Sigma)
to denote
an i.i.d. realization from the stated Gaussian distribution, and similarly for other variables. We use variants of this notation
in what follows.

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{n})+\mathsf{N}(0,\Sigma),

(128a)

\displaystyle\widehat{y}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=h(\widehat{v}_{n+1})+\mathsf{N}(0,\Gamma),

(128b)

\displaystyle v_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{v}_{n+1}+\widehat{C}^{vh}_{n+1}(\widehat{C}^{hh}_{n+1}+\Gamma)^{-1}({y}^{\dagger}_{n+1}-\widehat{y}_{n+1}).

(128c)

We now apply the rescaling (
84
) to obtain, using (
117
a),

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=v_{n}+\Delta tf(v_{n})+\sqrt{\Delta t}\mathsf{N}(0,\mathsf{\Sigma}),

\displaystyle\widehat{z}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{z}_{n}+\Delta t\mathsf{h}(\widehat{v}_{n+1})+\sqrt{\Delta t}\mathsf{N}(0,\mathsf{\Gamma}),

\displaystyle v_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{v}_{n+1}+C^{v\mathsf{h}}_{n}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}(\Delta{z}^{\dagger}_{n+1}-\Delta\widehat{z}_{n+1})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{3/2}).

The preceding calculation, leading to
\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{3/2})
error,
uses the fact that the noises entering the equations for
\widehat{v}_{n+1},

\widehat{z}_{n+1}
and
z^{\dagger}_{n+1}
are independent.
Taking the continuous time limit we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=f(v)\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\mathsf{\Sigma}}\mathrm{d}W+C^{v\mathsf{h}}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}(\mathrm{d}{z}^{\dagger}-\mathrm{d}\widehat{z}),

(129a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\widehat{z}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{h}(v)\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\mathrm{d}B.

(129b)

Again
W,B
are independent unit Brownian motions of appropriate dimensions
and
{z}^{\dagger}
is given by (
92
). This is an instance of a sample path perspective that leads to approximation of the true
filtering evolution.

Remark 3.17
.

Note the recurrence of the continuous time gain
K
first identified in Remark
3.9
.
In this subsection we have derived the mean field equations (
129
) from the associated stochastic discrete time formulation (
128
). However there is another way to derive the gain as outlined in Remark
3.16
.

\blacksquare

3.5.3
Second Order Transport – Deterministic Case

We may apply a similar analysis to that in Subsection

3.5.2
but instead working in the setting of
deterministic transport, starting from the discrete time
transport (
59
). We may
rewrite and reformulate this equation here to obtain

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi(v_{n})+\mathsf{N}(0,\Sigma),\quad\widehat{h}_{n+1}=h(\widehat{v}_{n+1}),

\displaystyle v_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{v}_{n+1}-\widetilde{K}_{n}(\widehat{h}_{n+1}-\mathbb{E}\widehat{h}_{n+1})+K_{n}({y}^{\dagger}_{n+1}-\mathbb{E}\widehat{h}_{n+1}),

where

\displaystyle K_{n}

\displaystyle=\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{vh}\left(\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{hh}+\Gamma\right)^{-1},\quad\widetilde{K}_{n}=\widehat{C}^{vh}_{n+1}\Bigl((\widehat{C}^{hh}_{n+1}+\Gamma)+\Gamma^{1/2}(\widehat{C}^{hh}_{n+1}+\Gamma)^{1/2}\Bigr)^{-1}.

Applying the rescalings (
84
) gives

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=v_{n}+\Delta tf(v_{n})+\sqrt{\Delta t}\mathsf{N}(0,\mathsf{\Sigma}),

\displaystyle v_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{v}_{n+1}+C_{n}^{v\mathsf{h}}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}\Bigl(\Delta{z}^{\dagger}_{n+1}-\frac{1}{2}(\mathsf{h}(\widehat{v}_{n+1})+\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h}(\widehat{v}_{n+1})\Delta t\Bigr)+{\mathcal{O}}(\Delta t^{2}),

where we have used

\displaystyle K_{n}

\displaystyle=C_{n}^{v\mathsf{h}}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t),\quad\widetilde{K}_{n}=\frac{1}{2}C_{n}^{v\mathsf{h}}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t).

Taking the continuous time limit we obtain

\mathrm{d}v=f(v)\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\mathsf{\Sigma}}\mathrm{d}W+C^{v\mathsf{h}}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}\Bigl(\mathrm{d}{z}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\mathsf{h}(v)+\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h}(v)\bigr)\mathrm{d}t\Bigr),

(130)

where, once again,
{z}^{\dagger}
is given by (
92
).

These mean field equations can also be derived directly from the general mean field equation (
127
), assuming a deterministic innovation,
invoking the constant gain approximation
K=K(\rho)
and using (
125
). The reasoning is similar to that in Remark
3.17
.

Example 3.18
.

We now consider mean field formulations of the Kalman-Bucy Filter from
Example
3.7
. First consider the evolution of random variable

v
initialized at a Gaussian and satisfying

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=Fv\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\mathsf{\Sigma}}\mathrm{d}W+CH^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}(\mathrm{d}{z}^{\dagger}-\mathrm{d}\widehat{z}),

(131a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\widehat{z}

\displaystyle=Hv\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\mathrm{d}B,

(131b)

where
C
is the covariance of
v
and where

{z}^{\dagger}
is given by (
112
).
Then direct calculation shows that the mean and covariance of
v
satisfy equations (
113
).
Thus we have identified a sample path perspective leading to a
mean field SDE for
v
with law equal to that of the Kalman-Bucy filter.

Similar considerations, starting
at (
130
), give the following variant of the
preceding mean field Kalman-Bucy filter:

\mathrm{d}v=Fv\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\mathsf{\Sigma}}\mathrm{d}W+CH^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}\Bigl(\mathrm{d}{z}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}H\bigl(v+m\bigr)\mathrm{d}t\Bigr),

(132)

where
(m,C)
are the mean and covariance of
v

and where
{z}^{\dagger}
is given by (
112
).
Again direct computations verify this.
An important observation highlighted by this example is that
mean field models consistent with a given measure evolution
are not typically unique.

\blacksquare

3.6
Ensemble Kalman Methods

We now discuss several particle approximations of the
mean field equations derived in the preceding subsections.
We start with the mean field equations (
120
), based on perfect
transport, before considering particle approximations for time continuous
approximate sample path, and hence transport, formulations.
Throughout this subsection
{z}^{\dagger}
is given by (
112
).

3.6.1
Perfect Particle Filters

As in Subsection
2.6.1
we define
\mathsf{J}:=\{1,\cdots,J\}.

The desired approximation to the mean field model (
120
)
evolves particle ensemble
\{{v}^{(j)}\}_{j\in\mathsf{J}}
and its associated
empirical measure

\mu^{\mathsf{J}}(t)=\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\delta_{{v}^{(j)}}

(133)

according to the interacting particle system

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}{v}^{(j)}

\displaystyle=f({v}^{(j)})\mathrm{d}t+a^{(j)}\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\mathsf{\Sigma}}\mathrm{d}{W}^{(j)}+K^{(j)}(\mathrm{d}{z}^{\dagger}-\mathrm{d}\widehat{z}^{(j)}),

(134a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\widehat{z}^{(j)}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{h}({v}^{(j)})\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\mathrm{d}{B}^{(j)},

(134b)

where
{z}^{\dagger}
is given by (
92
) and
the
\{{W}^{(j)}\}_{j\in\mathsf{J}}
and
\{{B}^{(j)}\}_{j\in\mathsf{J}}

are mutually independent collections of i.i.d. Brownian motions
in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}
and
\mathbb{R}^{d_{y}}
respectively. The interaction between the particles arises from the gain matrices

K^{(j)}:=K^{\mathsf{J}}({v}^{(j)};\mu^{\mathsf{J}}),

(135)

j\in\mathsf{J}
, where the matrix valued function
K^{\mathsf{J}}
approximates the solution
K
of (
119
);
the drift term
a^{(j)}
is defined by (
123
) with
K(v)
replaced
by
K^{\mathsf{J}}(v;\mu^{\mathsf{J}})
and then evaluated at
v=v^{(j)}
. The
key analysis question underlying the particle methodology is
to show that the empirical measure (
133
) approximates the law of
v
satisfying (
120
); this type of question
is widely studied for numerous problems in the physical, biological
and social sciences – see bibliography Subsection
3.7
.

Remark 3.19
.

In practice this particle approximation of the perfect particle filter
is impractical except in low dimensional systems. This is because
of the challenge of numerically approximating the equation (
119
) for
K
, or its weak formulation (
124
). In this remark we discuss various approximation approaches that make this problem tractable.

We start with the numerical approximation of the weak formulation (
124
). Let
\mathcal{F}
denote a space of vector-valued functions mapping
\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}
into
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and that are mean zero with respect to expectation
\mathbb{E}
under density
\rho
.
19
19

19

We will consider different choices for

d
but use the same notation for the space.

We make the ansatz that
K^{\top}=\nabla\Psi
, for some

\Psi\in\mathcal{F}.
Then (
124
) can be rewritten as

\mathbb{E}(\nabla\Psi\nabla\psi)=\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}C^{\mathsf{h}\psi},

assumed to hold for all
\psi\in\mathcal{F}.

In order to obtain the desired numerical approximation
K^{\mathsf{J}}
let
\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}
denote expectation with respect to the empirical measure (
133
) and consider
(K^{\mathsf{J}})^{\top}=\nabla\Psi^{\mathsf{J}}
and

\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}(\nabla\Psi^{\mathsf{J}}\nabla\psi)=\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}C^{\mathsf{h}\psi}

(136)

with the correlation
C^{\mathsf{h}\psi}
approximated by

C^{\mathsf{h}\psi}=\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}\Bigl(\bigl(\mathsf{h}(v)-\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}\mathsf{h}(v)\bigr)\otimes\bigl(\psi(v)-\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}\psi(v)\bigr)\Bigr).

The final step in the numerical approximation is to choose an appropriate finite dimensional subspace
\mathcal{F}^{L}\subset\mathcal{F}
of dimension
L\ll J
and to determine

\Psi^{\mathsf{J}}\in\mathcal{F}^{L}
such that (
136
) holds for all
\psi\in\mathcal{F}^{L}
.

Alternatively we may return to the strong formulation (
119
). We again make the ansatz
K^{\top}=\nabla\Psi
, giving rise to the differential equation

\mathcal{L}_{\rho}\Psi=-\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}(\mathsf{h}-\mathbb{E}\mathsf{h})

with differential operator
\mathcal{L}_{\rho}
20
20

20

Not to be confused
with operator
\mathcal{L}_{n}
defined by viewing Bayes Theorem, within filtering,
as a prior to posterior map at time
n
.

defined by

\mathcal{L}_{\rho}\Psi=\rho^{-1}\nabla\cdot(\rho\nabla\Psi)=\Delta\Psi+\nabla\Psi\nabla\log\rho.

Note that in the case of scalar observations,
\mathcal{L}_{\rho}
is the infinitesimal generator of a diffusion process with invariant density
\rho
; in the vector case the same statement holds component-by-component. This fact may be used to approximate the action of
\mathcal{L}_{\rho}
via its heat semigroup, and this may be used as the basis for numerical approximations. See the bibliography Subsection
3.7
for references to the relevant literature.

\blacksquare

The two general approaches to
approximate the gain matrix in (
135
), outlined
in the preceding remark, are interesting theoretically and perhaps hold promise in the future as computer power grows, but they lead to very expensive computations. To address this we now return to the constant gain approximation from Remark
3.16
, now in the particle setting. In the current context the identification of
K^{\mathsf{J}}
arises from (
136
), by making the choice
\Psi(v)=(K^{\mathsf{J}})^{\top}(v-\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}v)
and
\psi(v)=v-\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}v
. These choices result in the following
approximation of (
126
):

\displaystyle K^{\mathsf{J}}

\displaystyle=C^{v\mathsf{h}}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1},

(137a)

\displaystyle C^{v\mathsf{h}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}\Bigl(\bigl(v-\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}v\bigr)\otimes\bigl(\mathsf{h}(v)-\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}\mathsf{h}(v)\bigr)\Bigr).

(137b)

Note that the constant gain approximation (
137
) also implies that the drift term
a^{(j)}
in (
134
) vanishes. We summarize numerical implementation details in the following two subsections.

3.6.2
Stochastic Ensemble Kalman Filters

We now consider the mean field model (
129
) and its
numerical approximation. Since the drift correction
a
does not
appear here the only significant difference, in comparison
with the interacting particle approximation (
134
), arises from the choice of the interaction term, that is,
K^{(j)}
. This gain is now independent of
j
and determined by (
137
).
In summary, we obtain the following SDEs: for
j\in\mathsf{J}:=\{1,\dots,J\}
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}{v}^{(j)}

\displaystyle=f({v}^{(j)})\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\mathsf{\Sigma}}\mathrm{d}{W}^{(j)}+C^{v\mathsf{h}}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}(\mathrm{d}{z}^{\dagger}-\mathrm{d}\widehat{z}^{(j)}),

(138a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\widehat{z}^{(j)}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{h}({v}^{(j)})\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\mathrm{d}{B}^{(j)}.

(138b)

Here, again,
{z}^{\dagger}
is given by (
92
) and
the
\{{W}^{(j)}\}_{j\in\mathsf{J}}
and
\{{B}^{(j)}\}_{j\in\mathsf{J}}

are mutually independent collections of i.i.d. Brownian motions.
The collection
\{{v}^{(j)}\}_{j=1}^{J}
provide a time-evolving
ensemble which approximates the filtering distribution via
(
133
); the equations are derived based on
use of second order transport approximation of perfect transport. Each equation for
{v}^{(j)}
evolves according to the underlying dynamics model, together with a nudging term based on the difference between simulated data
\widehat{z}^{(j)}
and observed data
{z}^{\dagger}.
The gain couples the particles together.

3.6.3
Deterministic Ensemble Kalman Filters

Similarly we may make an empirical approximation of the mean field model (
130
) as follows:
for
j\in\mathsf{J}:=\{1,\dots,J\}
we consider

\mathrm{d}{v}^{(j)}=f({v}^{(j)})\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\mathsf{\Sigma}}\mathrm{d}{W}^{(j)}+C^{v\mathsf{h}}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}\Bigl(\mathrm{d}{z}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\mathsf{h}({v}^{(j)})+\mathbb{E}^{\mathsf{J}}\mathsf{h}(v)\bigr)\mathrm{d}t\Bigr).

(139)

The notation is as in the preceding subsection and, in particular,
the formula (
133
) again gives the
particle approximation of the approximate filter; now
the relevant approximate
filter is defined by the distribution of (
139
).

3.7
Bibliographical Notes

The entirety of Section
3
is
framed in the language of SDEs; see

Evans 2012
,
Oksendal 2013
for
background in this area. In passing from discrete to continuous
time we often invoke ideas from the numerical solution of
SDEs; see
Kloeden and Platen 1991
and

Higham 2001
for introductions to this area.
The conventions from continuum mechanics, that we use to define the divergence and gradient of vector fields, are the same as those adopted, and described in detail, in
Gonzalez and Stuart 2008
,
Gurtin 1982
.

Next we overview literature in the control theoretic approach introduced in Subsection
3.2
. For a general overview of control theory in continuous time see
Sontag 2013
. Our presentation of control theoretical
methods for data assimilation focusses on 3DVAR.
Theoretical analysis of the continuous time 3DVAR algorithm
(
91
) may be found in

Law et al. 2014
,

Blömker et al. 2013
,

Azouani et al. 2014
,

Gesho et al. 2016
,

Olson and Titi 2003
,

Mondaini and Titi 2018
and

Larios and Pei 2024
.

We now overview the probabilistic approach introduced in
Subsection
3.3
. The Kalman-Bucy filter
(
Kalman and Bucy 1961
)
contains
what is perhaps the first systematic derivation and analysis of an
algorithm for the incorporation of continuous time data into estimation of a sample path of an SDE. Its extension to nonlinear and non-Gaussian distributions is provided by the Kushner-Stratonovich equation (
108
). An heuristic derivation of both the Kalman-Bucy filter as well as the Kushner-Stratonovich equation can be found in
Jazwinski 2007
while
Bain and Crisan 2008
covers the field of continuous time filtering in full detail.

The idea of Strang-splitting, which we use to derive the Kushner-Stratonovich equation, originates in
Strang 1968
; for an overview of splitting methods see
McLachlan and Quispel 2002
. Furthermore, we rely on robustness results for continuous time filtering
(
Clark and Crisan 2005
)
,
which imply that smooth approximations
z^{\dagger,\delta}

to stochastic observations
{z}^{\dagger}
are justified and that
the order of taking limits
\Delta t\to 0
and
\delta\to 0
can be accounted for by appropriate Stratonovich to Itô correction terms. An introduction to the required covariation formulae used in proof of Lemma
3.4
and identity (
106
), which follows from covariation of stochastic integrals, can be found in
Eberle 2019
. We note that robustness results do not carry over to associated mean field equations and filtering problems with correlated noise
(
Coghi et al. 2023
)
.

The numerical approximation of the Kushner-Stratonovich equation (
108
) has a long history. The paper
Crisan and Lyons 1999
demonstrated how (
108
) can be approximated by a particle method; this is a generalization of the bootstrap particle filter to continuous time
Crisan et al. 1999
. See also
Bain and Crisan 2008
for a detailed discussion of alternative approximation techniques. The paper

Hu et al. 2002
discussed solution of the unnormalized and linear version of the Kushner-Stratonovich equation known as the Zakai equation.

A mean field approach to the Kushner-Stratonovich equation (
108
) appeared first in the work of
Crisan and Xiong 2010
, which utilizes robustness results and smoothed data
z^{\dagger,\delta}
in the
\delta\to 0
limit. The mean field equations (
127
)
were proposed in
Yang et al. 2013
(in the one dimensional setting) while the stochastic counterpart appeared first in
Reich 2019
. The mathematical relationship between the various mean field formulations has been analyzed in
Pathiraja et al. 2021
. Numerical implementations of the mean field equations (
120
) are discussed in
Taghvaei et al. 2017
while
Taghvaei et al. 2020
provides a detailed analysis of the diffusion map approximation discussed after (
3.19
). The constant gain approximation
K=C^{v\mathsf{h}}\mathsf{\Gamma}
, which corresponds to the ensemble Kalman filter,
arises as a particular scaling limit from the diffusion map approach as discussed, for example, in
Taghvaei et al. 2017
.
See also the recent survey by
Taghvaei and Mehta 2023
.

The continuous time ensemble Kalman filter formulations (
138
) and (
139
) appeared first in
Bergemann and Reich 2012
. These formulations are based on earlier work on homotopy formulations of the Bayesian inference step by
Bergemann and Reich 2010b
and
Reich 2011
. See also the subsequent derivations in

Law et al. 2015
which contains a unified derivation of the Kalman-Bucy filter, continuous time 3DVAR and continuous time ensemble Kalman methods, starting from their discrete time counterparts.

Rigorous derivation of continuous time ensemble Kalman filter formulations from their discrete time counterparts can be found in
Lange and Stannat 2021a
,
Lange and Stannat 2021b
,
Blömker et al. 2018
and
Blömker et al. 2022
. Derivation and analysis of the properties of continuous time limits
in the context of solving inverse problems may be found in

Schillings and Stuart 2017
; see bibliography Subsection

5.6
.
Numerical time-stepping methods for continuous time ensemble Kalman filter
formulations are analyzed in
Amezcua et al. 2014
.

The papers
Ding et al. 2021
,
Ding and Li 2021a
,
Ding and Li 2021b
undertake a systematic analysis of the link between interacting particle systems and mean field systems in continuous time, mostly focused on the solution of inverse problems; however the methods developed are more widely applicable. Similar to the stochastic ensemble Kalman filter, particle implementation (
138
) leads to undesirable correlations via the synthetic data
\widehat{z}^{(j)}
, which appear both in the gain
K^{\mathsf{J}}=C^{v\mathsf{h}}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}
and the innovation term
\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{I}=\mathrm{d}{z}^{\dagger}-\mathrm{d}\widehat{z}^{(j)}
effectively giving rise to colored noise. These numerically induced correlations vanish in the limit
J\to\infty
.

Well-posedness, stability, and accuracy results for the ensemble Kalman filter first appeared in
Kelly et al. 2014
, where the incompressible Navier-Stokes equations were studied, using
the continuous time formulations of stochastic ensemble Kalman methods first identified in
Reich 2011

and reviewed in
Law et al. 2015
. Subsequent analyses of related issues for
variants on the continuous time ensemble Kalman filter may be found in
de Wiljes et al. 2018
and
de Wiljes and Tong 2020
.
The paper
Del Moral and Tugaut 2018
initiates a line of research
related to the use of particle approximations of mean field models to understand long-time error estimates for particle systems approximating the Kalman-Bucy filter, the setting in which mean field ensemble Kalman filters exactly reproduce the true filtering distribution; see
Bishop et al. 2018
,
Bishop et al. 2019
,
Bishop and Del Moral 2019
,
Bishop et al. 2020

and, for an overview,
Bishop and Del Moral 2023
. The control perspective deployed in
Law et al. 2014
and
Azouani et al. 2014
to study filter stability and accuracy has been unified and extended to ensemble Kalman filter formulations
in
Biswas and Branicki 2024
, utilizing a particular form of covariance localization and additive inflation.

The classical Kalman filter can be viewed from the perspective of minimum variance estimation (discussed in discrete time in Subsection
9.3
) and optimal control
(
Kalman and Bucy 1961
)
. This perspective has recently been extended to nonlinear filtering in
Kim and Mehta 2024a
,
Kim and Mehta 2024b
, which opens up new perspectives for developing and analyzing numerical algorithms.

4
Inverse Problems: Discrete Time

In this section we adapt the ideas of Section
2
to solve inverse problems.
We start in Section
4.1
with statement of the inverse problem,
followed in Subsections
4.2
and
4.3
by discussion of
optimization and Bayesian approaches respectively; these are analogous to the
presentation of control and probabilistic approaches to the data assimilation problem
in Subsections
2.2
and
2.3
.

The basic methodology we highlight is to formulate filtering problems which
(possibly only approximately) solve the inverse problem.
The Subsection
4.4

is devoted to Bayesian probabilistic filtering methods which solve the inverse problem by morphing the
prior into the posterior in a finite time; Subsection
4.5
discusses filtering methods
which work on infinite time horizons, exhibiting exponential convergence to approximate
solutions of the optimization or Bayesian formulations of the problem from arbitrary starting points. In both of Subsections
4.4
and

4.5
we demonstrate application of Gaussian projected filtering
and ensemble Kalman methods to solve the filtering problems arising.
In Section
4.6
we present numerical examples illustrating
ensemble Kalman methods for inverse problems.
We conclude in Section
4.7
with bibliographic notes.

Remark 4.1
.

In Subsections
4.4
and
4.5

we present only mean field statements of the Gaussian projected
filter and ensemble Kalman based methods. The reader
can generalize the ideas in Section
2
, based on
interacting particle system approximations, to derive implementable algorithms from the ensemble based mean field algorithms introduced. Similarly, the unscented Kalman filter can be used to derive implementable algorithms from the Gaussian projected filters introduced here, as discussed in Subsection
2.7
. Pseudo-code for the schemes implemented in the numerical examples of Subsection
4.6
may be found in Appendix
7
.

\blacksquare

4.1
Set-Up

This and the next Section
5

are entirely devoted to solution of
the inverse problem of finding
unknown parameter
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}

from data
w\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{w}}
, when
w
is
related to
u
via the equation

w=G(u)+\gamma.

(140)

Here
G:\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{w}}
is the
forward model

and
\gamma
represents noise polluting the data.
We assume that
G
is measurable with respect to the Borel algebra on input and output spaces, and is bounded on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}.

The basic methodology we highlight is to formulate filtering problems which
(possibly only approximately) solve the inverse problem.

Remark 4.2
.

The filtering problem from Subsection
2.1
requires solution of
an inverse problem, defined by (
1
b), at each step
n;
this inverse
problem is a specific instance of (
140
). Indeed
the map
\mathcal{L}_{n}
in (
23
b) denotes Bayesian solution of this inverse problem,
a concept we will define, in the more general setting of
this section, in Subsection
4.3
.
Furthermore, the smoothing problem, referred to at the very end of the bibliography Subsection
2.7
, can also be formulated as an instance of the general inverse problem (
140
).

\blacksquare

We will work in a setting where we assume a probabilistic model
for the joint random variable
(u,w)
. We then assume that we
have available to us
{w}^{\dagger}
, the observation
coordinate of a specific realization
({u}^{\dagger},{w}^{\dagger})
under this probabilistic
model. This realization is itself generated by

{\gamma}^{\dagger}
, a specific realization of the observational noise.
We will consider two approaches to the inverse problem.

•

Objective 1: design an algorithm producing output

u
from
{w}^{\dagger}
so that

u
estimates

{u}^{\dagger}
, the true state underlying the data;

•

Objective 2: design an algorithm which estimates
the distribution of random variable
u|{w}^{\dagger}
.

In the next subsection we define an optimization approach to
determine an approximation of
{u}^{\dagger}
from
{w}^{\dagger},
addressing
Objective 1. In the subsection
following that we define the Bayesian probabilistic formulation,
which also underpins the algorithms derived in the remainder of the section,
addressing Objective 2.

4.2
Optimization Formulation

Given matrices
C_{0}\succ 0
,
\mathsf{\Gamma}\succ 0
, vector
m_{0}
and the specific data
realization. namely
{w}^{\dagger}
,
we may define the nonlinear least squares loss function
\Phi
and its Tikhonov-regularized counterpart

\Phi_{R}
as follows:

\displaystyle\Phi(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}|{w}^{\dagger}-G(u)|_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}^{2},

(141a)

\displaystyle\Phi_{R}(u)

\displaystyle=\Phi(u)+\frac{1}{2}|u-m_{0}|_{C_{0}}^{2}.

(141b)

Minimization of
\Phi_{R}
constitutes a solution to the inverse problem.
The specific weighted norms used in the least squares loss
\Phi
, and the regularization
leading to
\Phi_{R}
, are best understood from the probabilistic formulation
in the following subsection; however the remainder of this subsection can be understood
without recourse to this probabilistic formulation.

The Tikhonov regularized least squares problem associated with the
inverse problem (
140
) may be viewed as an unregularized
least squares problem arising from
the modified inverse problem

w_{R}=G_{R}(u)+\gamma_{R},

where we write

w_{R}:=\begin{pmatrix}w\\
m_{0}\end{pmatrix},\quad\quad\quad G_{R}(u):=\begin{pmatrix}G(u)\\
u\end{pmatrix},\quad\quad\quad\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}:=\begin{pmatrix}\mathsf{\Gamma}&0\\
0&C_{0}\end{pmatrix},

(142)

for
\gamma_{R}
being the generalized observation error. In particular, the cost functional (
141
b) can be rewritten as

\Phi_{R}(u)=\frac{1}{2}|{w}^{\dagger}_{R}-G_{R}(u)|^{2}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}},

(143)

for

{w}^{\dagger}_{R}:=\begin{pmatrix}{w}^{\dagger}\\
m_{0}\end{pmatrix}.

(144)

Remark 4.3
.

A building block in many algorithms for minimization of

\Psi:\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
is
gradient descent.
In basic form,
this is an iteration for sequence
\{u_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}

defined by

u_{n+1}=u_{n}-\alpha\nabla\Psi(u_{n}).

(145)

To solve the inverse problem, we can use iteration (
145
) with
\Psi=\Phi
or
\Psi=\Phi_{R}
. In Subsection
4.5.2
we develop derivative-free
affine invariant
algorithms
21
21

21

See
Remark
4.19
for a discussion of the implications of affine invariance, in the discrete time setting. We will study affine invariance in detail in continuous time in Section
5
;
see Definition
5.12
and Remark
5.13
.
based on Gaussian projected and
ensemble Kalman filters. These algorithms offer an alternative to (
145
); they only
approximately minimize the least squares objective, in general.
However in the quadratic case they reproduce an exact mean-field
gradient descent algorithm, as we will show as this section unfolds.

\blacksquare

4.3
Bayesian Formulation

We now consider the
Bayesian
approach to this inverse problem.
We again assume
C_{0}\succ 0
and
\mathsf{\Gamma}\succ 0
, as in the
previous subsection. To be concrete
we assume
prior

u\sim\mathsf{N}(m_{0},C_{0})
, that
\gamma\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\mathsf{\Gamma})
and that
u
and
\gamma
are independent. It then follows that the likelihood
w|u\sim\mathsf{N}\bigl(G(u),\mathsf{\Gamma}\bigr)
. Application of Bayes Theorem shows that the
posterior distribution
on
u|{w}^{\dagger}
is measure
\mu
given by

\displaystyle\mu(\mathrm{d}u)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathscr{Z}}\exp\bigl(-\Phi_{R}(u)\bigr)\mathrm{d}u,

(146a)

\displaystyle\mathscr{Z}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}}\exp\bigl(-\Phi_{R}(u)\bigr)\mathrm{d}u.

(146b)

We note that the least-squares based optimization approaches to the inverse problem introduced in the preceding subsection can now be explicitly linked to the probabilistic formulation of the
inverse problem. In particular, minimizing
\Phi
is referred to as the
maximum likelihood approach
, whilst minimizing
\Phi_{R}
as the
maximum a posteriori approach
.

Example 4.4
.

Consider the case of linear forward model:

G(\cdot)=L\cdot.

(147)

Thus
\Phi_{R}
is quadratic and it is straightforward to show that
the Hessian of
\Phi_{R}
is greater than or equal to, in the sense of quadratic
forms,
C_{0}^{-1}.
Since
C_{0}^{-1}\succ 0
we deduce
that
\Phi_{R}
has a unique critical point and this critical point is
a global minimizer. It is then natural to define

L_{R}:=\begin{pmatrix}L\\
I\end{pmatrix}

(148)

and note that, with this definition,
G_{R}(\cdot)=L_{R}\cdot.

We then have

\Phi_{R}(u)=\frac{1}{2}|{w}^{\dagger}_{R}-L_{R}u|^{2}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}}.

Since
\Phi_{R}
is quadratic we deduce that the posterior
\mu
is Gaussian. We denote the mean by
m_{\rm post}
and by
C_{\rm post}
the covariance. Matrix
C_{\rm post}
is readily defined via its precision, the Hessian of
\Phi_{R}:

C_{\rm post}^{-1}=\mathsf{S}=L_{R}^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}^{-1}L_{R}.

(149)

As discussed above
C_{\rm post}^{-1}\succ 0
so that
C_{\rm post}\succ 0
; in particular
C_{\rm post}
is hence indeed invertible.
The minimizer of
\Phi_{R}
is at the mean
m_{\rm post}
of the posterior which solves the
normal equations

C_{\rm post}^{-1}m_{\rm post}=L_{R}^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}^{-1}{w}^{\dagger}_{R}.

(150)

This representation of the posterior, which is Gaussian, is in terms of the precision matrix and the mean. There is an alternative and useful representation formula for the posterior covariance and mean, derived as follows. Consider
the Gaussian random variable
(u,w)
defined by choosing
u\sim\mathsf{N}(m_{0},C_{0})
and
w|u\sim\mathsf{N}(Lu,\mathsf{\Gamma}).
Then the solution of the Bayesian inverse problem (
146
) is given in this linear setting by the distribution of
u|{w}^{\dagger}.
By using standard conditioning formulae for Gaussian random variables we obtain

\displaystyle m_{\rm post}

\displaystyle=m_{0}+C_{0}L^{\top}(LC_{0}L^{\top}+\mathsf{\Gamma})^{-1}({w}^{\dagger}-Lm_{0}),

(151a)

\displaystyle C_{\rm post}

\displaystyle=C_{0}-C_{0}L^{\top}(LC_{0}L^{\top}+\mathsf{\Gamma})^{-1}LC_{0}.

(151b)

These formulae are equivalent to
(m_{1},C_{1})
found from the Kalman filter Bayesian update
step (
27
) of Example
2.6
, with the choices

M={\rm Id},\Sigma=0,H=L
and
\Gamma=\mathsf{\Gamma}.

\blacksquare

Remark 4.5
.

A commonly used methodology for sampling from target distribution

\mu
on
\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}
is MCMC. At abstract level
this defines a Markov chain for density
\rho_{n}
given by transition
kernel
\mathcal{K}(\alpha)
, where
\alpha
describes hyper-parameters
that define the specific method used. Thus

\rho_{n+1}=\mathcal{K}(\alpha)\rho_{n}.

(152)

This is a linear iteration for the density
\rho_{n}
, designed to converge
to the target density, defined by the posterior, as
n\to\infty.
In Subsection

4.5.3
we show how mean field ensemble Kalman methods, which induce
a nonlinear iteration on density
\rho_{n}
, may be used as an alternative to
(
152
), defining an approximate Bayesian
posterior by iterating to infinity. Furthermore, this iteration will
be shown to be exact for Gaussian posteriors, and to benefit from
affine invariance.
22
22

22

Recall that discussion of the implications of affine invariance may be found in Remark
4.19
, in the discrete time
setting; and that we will study affine invariance in detail in continuous
time, in Section
5
.

\blacksquare

4.4
Finite-Time Algorithms

The basic idea used in this subsection, to address the solution of
inverse problems, is rooted in a sequential formulation of Bayesian inference.
From this sequential formulation we derive a filtering problem whose
solution, at a particular time, gives the desired posterior.
Subsection
4.4.1
describes the formulation,
Subsection
4.4.2
the use of Gaussian projected filters
and Subsection
4.4.3
the use of ensemble Kalman methods.

4.4.1
Formulation

To understand this sequential approach we define, for integer
N>1
,

\Phi_{R,n}(u)=\frac{n}{N}\Phi(u)+\frac{1}{2}|u-m_{0}|_{C_{0}}^{2},

(153)

noting that
\Phi_{R,N}(u)=\Phi_{R}(u).

Now consider the sequence
\mu_{n}
of probability measures with negative log
density given (up to an additive constant with respect to variation of
u
)
by
\Phi_{R,n}(u).
Then the Bayesian inference problem (
146
)
can be reformulated as a sequence of
N
Bayesian inference steps
where the prior
\mu_{n}
is
morphed into posterior
\mu_{n+1}
using the data likelihood

\exp(-\Phi(u)/N)
in each step:

\mu_{n+1}(\mathrm{d}u)\propto\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{N}\Phi(u)\Bigr)\mu_{n}(\mathrm{d}u).

(154)

Hence

\mu_{n}(\mathrm{d}u)\propto\exp\Bigl(-\frac{n}{N}\Phi(u)\Bigr)\mu_{0}(\mathrm{d}u).

(155)

Thus

\mu_{N}(\mathrm{d}u)\propto\exp\Bigl(-\Phi(u)\Bigr)\mu_{0}(\mathrm{d}u).

(156)

The initial prior is set to

\mu_{0}=\mathsf{N}(m_{0},C_{0})
and the
N^{th}
posterior
\mu_{N}

delivers the desired Bayesian solution to the
inverse problem, given in (
146
).

Remark 4.6
.

Here we have introduced an iteration index
n
to morph from prior to posterior.
In what follows we will identify
n
with an
artificial
time and then
import ideas from filtering to solve the inverse problem. Notice that, in this
approach, the single inverse problem (
156
) of interest,
is replaced by
N
inverse problems of the form (
154
).
This can be beneficial because each of the
N
inverse problems
(
154
) is easier to solve than the single inverse
problem (
156
), because the defining change of measure
is closer to the identity.

A variant on this idea is to morph from prior to posterior
by (possibly artificially) considering the data
w
as sequentially acquired
and incrementally including components of the data at each step
n
, again leading
to a sequence of measures
\{\mu_{n}\}_{n=0}^{N}
, with
\mu_{N}
equal to the
posterior.

\blacksquare

Given this sequence of measures
\mu_{n}
, it is possible to identify a stochastic dynamical system with filtering distribution
\mu_{n}.
Application of any filtering method to this filtering problem, and ensemble Kalman filters in particular, then
provides a method to approximate the posterior distribution.
These sequential formulations of Bayesian inversion are well known and have, for example, been exploited in the use of sequential Monte Carlo methods for Bayesian inference; see Subsection
4.7
for details.

Remark 4.7
.

To employ sequential Monte Carlo methods based on (
154
)
it is necessary to invoke some form of approximation. The resulting outcome
of such approximations depends on the choice of positive integer
N
.
Empirically it is found that choosing
N\gg 1
gives better approximations
of the desired posterior (
156
); however this must be traded
against the additional cost of taking
N
steps.

Standard particle filter-based sequential Monte Carlo methods
do not always scale well to high dimensions, in the same way that the particle filter for state estimation scales poorly. Consequently ensemble Kalman variants of
sequential Monte Carlo methods have an important place in the field and we
will deploy these, and variants of them, after introducing the stochastic dynamical system underlying sequential Monte Carlo.

\blacksquare

Our first step is to show how to realize (
154
) via a filtering problem which we refer to as a
transport problem
:
23
23

23

Note that we have introduced transport ideas in Section
2
to underpin algorithms which
approximate the Bayesian inference that defines the analysis
step in filtering.
it transports the prior initial condition into the desired posterior, through a discrete time evolution. See Theorem
4.8
below and note that in Subsection

5.4.1
analogous developments are made in continuous time.
The transport problem is exact: it introduces no approximations. To
derive algorithms we proceed to discuss approximations to the transport. We follow discussion of exact transport with study of the Gaussian projected filter, applied in this inverse problem context, in Subsection
4.4.2
, with continuous time analog in Subsection
5.4.2
. Study of Gaussian projection is then followed by discussion of the application of mean field Kalman transport algorithms to the transport problem, see Subsection
4.4.3
; continuous time analogs are covered in Subsection
5.4.3
.

In the following development we define
\Delta t
so that

N\Delta t=1.

(157)

Now consider the combined state-observation system in the form

\displaystyle u_{n+1}

\displaystyle=u_{n},

(158a)

\displaystyle w_{n+1}

\displaystyle=G(u_{n+1})+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Delta t}}\gamma_{n+1},

(158b)

for

n\in\{0,\cdots,N-1\}
, where
\{\gamma_{n+1}\}_{n=0}^{N-1}
is an i.i.d. sequence with variance
\mathsf{N}(0,\mathsf{\Gamma}).

It is intuitive that, since
N\Delta t=1
,
u_{0}\sim\mathsf{N}(m_{0},C_{0})
and the observed data
w^{\dagger}_{n+1}={w}^{\dagger}
for all

n\in\{0,\cdots,N-1\}
, then
u_{N}
conditioned on
W_{N}^{\dagger}:=\{w^{\dagger}_{n+1}\}_{n=0}^{N-1}

is distributed as
\mu
, defined as in (
146
). Indeed, using (
158
b) for
n\in\{0,\cdots,N-1\}
corresponds to making
N
independent noisy observations of
G(u_{0})
, all with noise variance
\Delta t^{-1}\mathsf{\Gamma};
this is statistically equivalent to a single noisy observation of
G(u_{0})
with noise variance
\mathsf{\Gamma}
. Since
u_{0}
is initialized as
\mathsf{N}(m_{0},C_{0})
(the prior) the problem reduces to the Bayesian inverse problem for
u|{w}^{\dagger}.

This intuition may be substantiated by using the discussion around equations (
153
), (
154
). In order to avail ourselves of the results from Section
2
, we first rescale the observation equation in (
158
) to obtain

\displaystyle u_{n+1}

\displaystyle=u_{n},

(159a)

\displaystyle y_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\Delta tG(u_{n+1})+\eta_{n+1},

(159b)

with
\eta_{n+1}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\Delta t\mathsf{\Gamma})
and
y_{n+1}=\Delta tw_{n+1}
. Denote the observed data by
{Y}^{\dagger}_{n}=\{{y}^{\dagger}_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{n}
, where
{y}^{\dagger}_{\ell}=\Delta t{w}^{\dagger}_{\ell}
. We may then show the following:

Theorem 4.8
.

Consider the dynamical system (
159
) and
assume that
C_{0}\succ 0
,
\mathsf{\Gamma}\succ 0
and
N\Delta t=1
.
Assume also that
u_{0}\sim\mathsf{N}(m_{0},C_{0})
and
\eta_{n+1}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\Delta t\mathsf{\Gamma});
furthermore, assume that
\{\eta_{n}\}_{n=1}^{N}
forms an i.i.d. sequence, independent of
u_{0}.

Then
\mu_{n}
, the law of
u_{n}|{Y}^{\dagger}_{n}
defined by (
159
), satisfies (
154
),
and in particular
\mu_{N}
is equal to the posterior distribution
\mu
, if the data is chosen as
{y}^{\dagger}_{n}=\Delta t\,{w}^{\dagger},
for
n\in\{1,\cdots,N\}
.

\Diamond

Proof 4.9
.

Let
\mu_{n}
be the law of
u_{n}|{Y}^{\dagger}_{n}
.
Since (in the notation of Section
2
)
\widehat{\mu}_{n+1}=\mu_{n}
we see that
the mapping
\mu_{n}
to
\mu_{n+1}
is simply given by Bayes theorem:
\mu_{n+1}=\mathcal{L}_{n}(\mu_{n}).

This observation yields the following
identity, expressed in terms of

\rho_{n}
the Lebesgue density of measure
\mu_{n}:

\displaystyle\log\rho_{n+1}-\log\rho_{n}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\Delta t}|{y}^{\dagger}_{n}-\Delta tG(u)|_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}^{2}+{\rm const},

\displaystyle=-\frac{\Delta t}{2}|w-G(u)|_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}^{2}+{\rm const}.

Summing over
n\in\{0,\cdots,N-1\}
, using the fact that

\displaystyle\log\rho_{0}
