\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}|u-m_{0}|_{C_{0}}^{2}+{\rm const},

we deduce that

\log\rho_{n}=-\Phi_{R,n}+{\rm const},

with
\Phi_{R,n}
given by (
153
). Choosing
n=N

gives the desired result concerning the posterior.

Thus we may approach the problem of (approximately) sampling from
\mu

by (approximately) solving the filtering problem defined by
(
159
), for
\mu_{n}
, until discrete time
n=N
. In particular, we may use the Gaussian projected filter or ensemble Kalman methods to approximate this filtering problem.
In the next two subsections we consider, respectively, these two approximation methods.

4.4.2
Algorithms: Gaussian Projected Filter

We now apply the ideas from Subsection
2.4
, which concerns the Gaussian projected filter in the general setting, to the specific setting
of the stochastic dynamical system (
159
).
Let
\mathbb{E}
denote expectation under
u\sim\mathsf{N}(m_{n},C_{n})
and define

\displaystyle C_{n}^{uG}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\bigl(u-\mathbb{E}u\bigr)\otimes\bigl(G(u)-\mathbb{E}G(u)\bigr)\Bigr),

(160a)

\displaystyle C_{n}^{GG}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\bigl(G(u)-\mathbb{E}G(u)\bigr)\otimes\bigl(G(u)-\mathbb{E}G(u)\bigr)\Bigr).

(160b)

Noting that prediction under (
159
a) is trivial it follows
that the predicted mean and covariance satisfy

\widehat{m}_{n+1}=m_{n}
and
\widehat{C}_{n+1}=C_{n}.
Hence, using (
41
)
in the specific setting of (
159
), yields

\displaystyle m_{n+1}

\displaystyle=m_{n}+\Delta tC_{n}^{uG}(\mathsf{\Gamma}+\Delta tC_{n}^{GG})^{-1}\bigl({w}^{\dagger}-\mathbb{E}G(u)\bigr),

(161a)

\displaystyle C_{n+1}

\displaystyle=C_{n}-\Delta tC_{n}^{uG}(\mathsf{\Gamma}+\Delta tC_{n}^{GG})^{-1}\bigl(C_{n}^{uG}\bigr)^{\top}.

(161b)

Note that the difference between the data
{w}^{\dagger}
and the
mean of
G(u)
under the Gaussian at step
n
acts as a forcing
term in the evolution of the mean from
n
to
n+1
,
promoting a Gaussian which agrees with the data. This forcing
term is weighted by covariance information. The covariance of the
Gaussian projected filter is non-increasing from step to step since

\langle u,C_{n+1}u\rangle\leq\langle u,C_{n}u\rangle

for all
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}};
this reflects the fact that more information
is received at each step
n\mapsto n+1
as the unknown

u
is repeatedly observed.

Example 4.10
.

In the setting of the linear inverse
problem (
4.4
), where
G(u)=Lu
, the Gaussian projected filter
equations (
161
) reduce to

\displaystyle m_{n+1}

\displaystyle=m_{n}+\Delta tC_{n}L^{\top}(\mathsf{\Gamma}+\Delta tLC_{n}L^{\top})^{-1}\bigl({w}^{\dagger}-Lm_{n}\bigr),

(162a)

\displaystyle C_{n+1}

\displaystyle=C_{n}-\Delta tC_{n}L^{\top}(\mathsf{\Gamma}+\Delta tLC_{n}L^{\top})^{-1}LC_{n}.

(162b)

These equations may be iterated to map from
(m_{0},C_{0})
directly to
(m_{n},C_{n})
,
obtaining

\displaystyle m_{n}

\displaystyle=m_{0}+n\Delta tC_{0}L^{\top}\left(\mathsf{\Gamma}+n\Delta tLC_{0}L^{\top}\right)^{-1}\bigl({w}^{\dagger}-Lm_{0}\bigr),

(163a)

\displaystyle C_{n}

\displaystyle=C_{0}-n\Delta tC_{0}L^{\top}\left(\mathsf{\Gamma}+n\Delta tLC_{0}L^{\top}\right)^{-1}LC_{0},

(163b)

These formulae may also be obtained by applying Bayes formula, in the linear
setting, to (
155
) and using (
157
), namely
N\Delta t=1.

The Gaussian posterior measure
\mu=\mathsf{N}(m_{\rm post},C_{\rm post})
given
by (
149
), (
150
) may now
be found by choosing mean and covariance
(m_{\rm post},C_{\rm post})=(m_{N},C_{N}).

This follows from Section
2
because the Gaussian projected
filter recovers the Kalman filter, which is exact for linear Gaussian problems.

\blacksquare

4.4.3
Algorithms: Ensemble Kalman Filter

Recall that mean field models lead to ensemble Kalman methods through particle approximation. In this section we simply highlight use of one of the mean field models, in the context of inverse problems, namely the stochastic Kalman transport approach from Subsection
2.5.4
and its
deterministic variant from Subsection
2.5.5
. We leave details of particle
approximations of these mean field models to the reader, and to
Appendix
7
for related pseudo-code.

Employing the state-observation model (
159
) within the stochastic Kalman transport model (
54
) we obtain the mean field dynamical system, for i.i.d. unit Gaussian sequence
\{\xi_{n}\}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{w}}
,

\displaystyle u_{n+1}

\displaystyle=u_{n}+\Delta tC^{uG}_{n}(\Delta tC^{GG}_{n}+\mathsf{\Gamma})^{-1}\Bigl({w}^{\dagger}-G(u_{n})-\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{\Gamma}}{\Delta t}}\xi_{n}\Bigr),

(164a)

\displaystyle C^{uG}_{n}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\bigl(u_{n}-\mathbb{E}u_{n}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(G(u_{n})-\mathbb{E}G(u_{n})\bigr)\Bigr),

(164b)

\displaystyle C^{GG}_{n}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\bigl(G(u_{n})-\mathbb{E}G(u_{n})\bigr)\otimes\bigl(G(u_{n})-\mathbb{E}G(u_{n})\bigr)\Bigr).

(164c)

Note that, here, expectation
\mathbb{E}
is computed under the law of
u_{n}
itself. As for the Gaussian projected filter (
161
) the evolution promotes a distribution which is compatible with the data, here with a forcing term, weighted by covariance information, applied to the evolution of the state
u_{n}
; but
unlike the mean field evolution equation for the mean, there is additional noise for the state evolution.

Recall Theorem
4.8
. Since the ensemble Kalman transport algorithm
used here provides an approximation of the filtering distribution for
the dynamical system (
159
), it follows that the random variable
u_{N}
provides an approximation to the posterior distribution
\mu
,
provided that
u_{0}\sim\mathsf{N}(m_{0},C_{0})
, the prior distribution.
This statement can be made exact in the linear case as the following example shows.

Example 4.11
.

Assume
G(u)=Lu
for
L\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{w}\times d_{u}}
so that the posterior
distribution of the Bayesian inverse problem
\mu
is given in Example
4.4
. Then the solution of the mean field model (
164
)
satisfies
u_{N}\sim\mu.
This is a specific instance of
what we observed in Example
2.17
, namely that the mean field model
reproduces the Kalman filter on linear Gaussian problems.
We note also that the Gaussian projected filter is identical
to the Kalman filter in this case – see Example
4.10
.

Although it is implicit in Example
4.4
, in this
specific inverse problem context we demonstrate the equivalence with the Kalman filter explicitly. To do this we note that, in the linear setting, equation (
164
) defines a closed evolution in the set of Gaussian probability measures. The updates for the mean
m_{n}
and covariance
C_{n}
of
u_{n}
then coincide with the Kalman filter, and hence the Gaussian
projected filter, in this linear case given by equations
(
162
). Indeed, by taking the expectation under the law of
u_{n}
of (
164
a), it is readily checked that in the linear setting we obtain (
162
a)
for the mean update. To obtain the evolution equation of the covariance, recall that

C_{n+1}=\mathbb{E}\Bigl((u_{n+1}-m_{n+1})\otimes(u_{n+1}-m_{n+1})\Bigr).

(165)

Substituting into (
165
) the expression for
u_{n+1}
, given by (
164
a) in the linear setting
G(u)=Lu
, and the expression for
m_{n+1}
, given by (
162
a), and then computing the expectation yields (
162
b).
It follows from the calculations of Example
4.10

that
u_{N}\sim\mu.

\blacksquare

We conclude this subsection by stating the corresponding deterministic transport formulation. We employ
the approximation (
60
). This holds in our case provided
\Delta t
is small enough. Choosing
K_{n}
as
implicitly defined in (
164
), we obtain,
with expectation
\mathbb{E}
computed under the law of
u_{n}
itself, the following mean field model:

\displaystyle u_{n+1}

\displaystyle=u_{n}+\Delta tC^{uG}_{n}(\Delta tC^{GG}_{n}+\mathsf{\Gamma})^{-1}\Bigl({w}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\bigl(G(u_{n})+\mathbb{E}G(u_{n})\bigr)\Bigr),

(166a)

\displaystyle C^{uG}_{n}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\bigl(u_{n}-\mathbb{E}u_{n}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(G(u_{n})-\mathbb{E}G(u_{n})\bigr)\Bigr),

(166b)

\displaystyle C^{GG}_{n}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\bigl(G(u_{n})-\mathbb{E}G(u_{n})\bigr)\otimes\bigl(G(u_{n})-\mathbb{E}G(u_{n})\bigr)\Bigr).

(166c)

4.5
Infinite-Time Algorithms

Algorithms which (approximately) transport prior to posterior in finite time,
as described in the preceding subsection,
are attractive. However they can be quite rigid as they do not benefit from
strong stability to perturbations. An alternative, pursued in this section,
is to seek algorithms which converge to the desired solution on an infinite
time-horizon, from arbitrary starting points, and which exhibit exponential stability.
This is hard to achieve in general, but can be achieved exactly
for Gaussian problems. When applied beyond the Gaussian setting this
hence leads to a methodology consistent with the application of
ensemble Kalman filter approximations, which themselves invoke a Gaussian
ansatz and yet are used beyond the Gaussian setting.
Subsection
4.5.1
describes the infinite time-horizon
formulation.
In Subsection
4.5.2
we consider this infinite time-horizon perspective
for the solution of optimization problems associated with the inverse problem
(
140
). Subsection
4.5.3
considers the same perspective for Bayesian inversion.

4.5.1
Formulation

To motivate what follows we consider algorithms that solve the optimization
problem by extending ideas from the previous section to iterate a filtering
problem over an infinite time-horizon. To explain this idea recall the identity (
155
),
restated here for convenience:

\mu_{n}(\mathrm{d}u)\propto\exp\Bigl(-\frac{n}{N}\Phi(u)\Bigr)\mu_{0}(\mathrm{d}u).

We note that if we evaluate this identity at
n=N
then we obtain the Bayesian
posterior distribution; the resulting algorithms are based on solving the
associated filtering problem on interval
n=0,1,\cdots,N.

Now we observe that if, instead,
we iterate
n\to\infty
for fixed
N
(and hence fixed
\Delta t
),
then
\mu_{n}
will converge to a sum of Dirac measures supported
at global minimizers of
\Phi
that are contained in the support of
\mu_{0}
. Thus we can iterate algorithms such as the Gaussian projected filter from Subsection
4.4.2
or the
ensemble Kalman filter from Subsection
4.4.3
to
n=\infty
in order to obtain an approximate solution of the optimization problem for
\Phi,
within the support of
\mu_{0}.

In the following example we study both the Gaussian projected
filter, and the ensemble Kalman filter, in the linear Gaussian setting.
We consider their application when we iterate
n\to\infty
for fixed
N.

The two algorithms coincide in this linear Gaussian setting. Studying
their properties gives insight into the proposed iterative approach
to optimization. In particular it motivates the use of
regularization
and

variance inflation
as introduced following the example.

Example 4.12
.

We consider the setting of Example
4.10
, where the linear
inverse problem with
G(u)=Lu
is considered, making the additional assumption
that
LC_{0}L^{\top}
has full rank. The Gaussian projected filter
equations (
161
), iterated over
n
steps give,
the single step update (
163
).
Since
LC_{0}L^{\top}
has full rank, this delivers the following
closed form update in the image of
L
:

\displaystyle Lm_{n}

\displaystyle=Lm_{0}+\Delta tLC_{0}L^{\top}\left(\frac{1}{n}\mathsf{\Gamma}+\Delta tLC_{0}L^{\top}\right)^{-1}\bigl({w}^{\dagger}-Lm_{0}\bigr),

(167a)

\displaystyle LC_{n}L^{\top}

\displaystyle=LC_{0}L^{\top}-\Delta tLC_{0}L^{\top}\left(\frac{1}{n}\mathsf{\Gamma}+\Delta tLC_{0}L^{\top}\right)^{-1}LC_{0}L^{\top}.

(167b)

If we fix
\Delta t
and let
n\to\infty
then we see that

\displaystyle Lm_{n}

\displaystyle={w}^{\dagger}+\mathcal{O}(1/n),

\displaystyle LC_{n}L^{\top}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(1/n).

We notice from the previous example that there are two issues when
letting
n\to\infty
for fixed
\Delta t
. First, the mean converges
to a point
m_{\infty}
solving
Lm_{\infty}={w}^{\dagger}
, rather than a
minimizer of the regularized functional
\Phi_{R}(\cdot)
. Secondly,
the convergence rate is only of order
1/n
.

\blacksquare

We now seek to address the two problems identified in this
example, to develop improved methodology.

Regularization

The first problem identified in Example
4.12
,
namely that regularization disappears when taking

n\to\infty
, can be addressed by considering the iteration defined by

\mu_{n}(\mathrm{d}u)\propto\exp\bigl(-n\Delta t\Phi_{R}(u)\bigr)\,\mu_{0}(\mathrm{d}u)

(168)

instead of the iteration defined by (
155
).
Recalling

G_{R},\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}
defined by (
142
) and assuming that

\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}\succ 0

then sequence of measures
\mu_{n}
given by (
168
)
may be generated by the filtering
distribution associated with the following
modification of (
159
):

\displaystyle u_{n+1}

\displaystyle=u_{n},

(169a)

\displaystyle y_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\Delta tG_{R}(u_{n+1})+\eta_{n+1};

(169b)

here
\eta_{n+1}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\Delta t\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R})
and we consider
the setting where the observed data is
y_{n+1}=\Delta t{w}^{\dagger}_{R}
.
24
24

24

Note
that
\ell
indexes an iteration in
{w}^{\dagger}_{\ell}
but that
{w}^{\dagger}_{R}
is the fixed vector defined in (
144
).
We may apply sequential filtering techniques, such as the Gaussian projected
filter and the ensemble Kalman filter, to the filtering problem defined
by (
169
). Since
\mu_{n}
converges to
a Dirac delta distribution centred about the minimizer of
\Phi_{R}
, within
the support of
\mu_{0},
this addresses the first problem. However, the rate
of convergence remains of order
1/n
so that the second problem is not
addressed; this is verified explicitly for the linear case in the
forthcoming Example
4.16
.

Variance Inflation

The second problem identified in Example
4.12
is algebraic convergence. The root cause of the algebraic convergence
is the collapse of the covariance
C_{n}
to zero.
Therefore, in order to accelerate the convergence rate, we need
to modify the sequential update steps to ensure that the covariance
of (approximate) filters does not collapse to zero; at the same time
we must ensure that the mean
m_{n}
still converges,
exactly in the linear setting and approximately in the general nonlinear case,
to the minimizer of
\Phi_{R}
as
n\to\infty
.

In order to achieve this non-collapsing covariance we modify (
169
)
by adding a form of variance inflation to the evolution
of the parameter
u_{n}
and
consider the stochastic dynamical system given by

\displaystyle u_{n+1}

\displaystyle=u_{n}+\xi_{n},

(170a)

\displaystyle y_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\Delta tG_{R}(u_{n+1})+\eta_{n+1}.

(170b)

Here
\xi_{n}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\beta\Delta t\mathsf{\Sigma}_{n})
,
\beta\geq 0
and
\eta_{n+1}\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\Delta t\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R})
for covariance inflation matrix
\mathsf{\Sigma}_{n}

to be defined. Note that if
\beta=0
we simply recover (
169
).

We now discuss the choice of
\mathsf{\Sigma}_{n}
. Because the true covariance that we
wish to recover is that of filtering distribution it is natural
\mathsf{\Sigma}_{n}

is defined in terms of the covariance of the filter. Defining
{Y}^{\dagger}_{n}=\{{y}^{\dagger}_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{n}
with
{y}^{\dagger}_{\ell}:=\Delta t{w}^{\dagger}_{R}
we may consider the filtering distribution defined by random variable
u_{n}|{Y}^{\dagger}_{n}.
We let
C_{n}
denote the covariance under this filtered random variable.
We then set
\mathsf{\Sigma}_{n}:=C_{n}
.

Remark 4.13
.

With this choice of
C_{n}
the equation (
170
) defines a form of mean field
model for state-observation evolution. Previously in this paper the state-observation models we have considered have not been of mean field type; we only introduced mean field models as the basis of sample path based algorithms to (approximately) solve a filtering problem. In contrast, here, the mean field dependence of the proposed model (
170
), with
\mathsf{\Sigma}_{n}:=C_{n}
, is through the filtering distribution associated with
u_{n}|{Y}^{\dagger}_{n}
. Thus, even before we develop mean field
models to approximate the law of the filtering distribution via sample path based algorithms, the underlying state-observation model is linked to filtering.

Although the filtering distribution depends on the history
{Y}^{\dagger}_{n}
, equation (
170
) can be rendered Markovian by coupling it to the evolution of the filtering distribution
\mu_{n}\mapsto\mu_{n+1}
, and noting that
C_{n}
is computed under
\mu_{n}
.

We note that, in practice, identification of the exact covariance of the filtering distribution is not possible. Thus in the Gaussian projected filter and ensemble Kalman filter that follow we will use approximations of
C_{n}
; however we will also denote these approximations by
C_{n}
to avoid proliferation of notation.

\blacksquare

We now derive implementable algorithms to approximate the filtering
distribution defined by (
170
).

Gaussian Projected Filter

We begin by applying the ideas from Subsection
2.4
, which concerns the Gaussian projected filter in the general setting, to the specific setting of the stochastic dynamical system (
170
). Using (
41
)
in the specific setting of (
170
), yields

\displaystyle\widehat{m}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=m_{n},

(171a)

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=(1+\beta\Delta t)C_{n},

(171b)

\displaystyle m_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{m}_{n+1}+\Delta t\widehat{C}^{uG}_{R,n+1}(\Delta t\widehat{C}^{GG}_{R,n+1}+\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R})^{-1}\bigl({w}^{\dagger}_{R}-\widehat{\mathrm{o}}_{n+1}\bigr),

(171c)

\displaystyle C_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{C}_{n+1}-\Delta t\widehat{C}^{uG}_{R,n+1}(\Delta t\widehat{C}^{GG}_{R,n+1}+\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R})^{-1}\bigl(\widehat{C}^{uG}_{R,n}\bigr)^{\top}.

(171d)

Here we define

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{o}}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}G_{R}(\widehat{u}_{n+1}),

(172a)

\displaystyle\widehat{C}^{uG}_{R,n+1}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\bigl(\widehat{u}_{n+1}-\mathbb{E}\widehat{u}_{n+1}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(G_{R}(\widehat{u}_{n+1})-\mathbb{E}G_{R}(\widehat{u}_{n+1})\bigr)\Bigr),

(172b)

\displaystyle\widehat{C}^{GG}_{R,n+1}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\bigl(G_{R}(\hat{u}_{n+1})-\mathbb{E}G_{R}(\widehat{u}_{n+1})\bigr)\otimes\bigl(G_{R}(\widehat{u}_{n+1})-\mathbb{E}G_{R}(\widehat{u}_{n+1})\bigr)\Bigr),

(172c)

where, in (
171
), all expectations are with
respect to
\widehat{u}_{n+1}\sim\mathsf{N}(\widehat{m}_{n+1},\widehat{C}_{n+1}).

Note that we have used the covariance of the Gaussian projected
filter to define the variance inflation required to determine
(
171
b), since we do not have the
covariance under the true filtering distribution.

Ensemble Kalman Filter

Instead of the Gaussian projected filter, we may use
the ensemble Kalman filter.
We use the covariance
C_{n}
of the ensemble Kalman filter
to define the variance inflation since, again, we do not have the
covariance under the true filtering distribution.
With these considerations in hand, application
of the stochastic Kalman transport mean field model
(
54
) to (
170
) yields

\displaystyle\widehat{u}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=u_{n}+\xi_{n},

(173a)

\displaystyle\widehat{y}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\Delta tG_{R}(\widehat{u}_{n+1})+\eta_{n+1},

(173b)

\displaystyle u_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{u}_{n+1}+\widehat{C}^{uG}_{R,n+1}\bigl(\Delta t\widehat{C}^{GG}_{R,n+1}+\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}\bigr)^{-1}\bigl(\Delta t{w}^{\dagger}_{R}-\widehat{y}_{n+1}\bigr).

(173c)

Here
\xi_{n}\sim\mathsf{N}\bigl(0,\beta\Delta tC_{n}\bigr),

\eta_{n+1}\sim\mathsf{N}\bigl(0,\Delta t\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R})
and
expecations appearing in (
172
), to define

(\widehat{C}^{uG}_{R,n+1},\widehat{C}^{GG}_{R,n+1})
, are computed
under the law of
\widehat{u}_{n+1}
.

Remark 4.14
.

Recall the mean field dynamical
system (
170
) and consider its filtering distribution.
In Subsection
4.5.2
we show that in the linear case the mean of
the filtering distribution converges to the posterior mean of the
underlying Bayesian inverse problem, and hence to a minimizer
of the Tikhonov regularized least squares function
\Phi_{R}.

For
\beta=0
convergence is algebraic,
whilst it is exponential for
\beta>0.

Furthermore, in Subsection
4.5.3
, we show that for a particular
choice of
\beta
, in the linear case the filtering distribution converges to the Bayesian posterior
distribution defined by the inverse problem.

Recall that the Gaussian projected filter (
171
)
and the mean field ensemble Kalman filter (
173
) exactly reproduce the evolution of
the filtering distribution, for linear Gaussian problems. As a consequence
everything stated in this remark for the filtering distribution applies also to the law
defined by (
171
) and by (
173
).

\blacksquare

We also note that it is possible to use corresponding deterministic
transport formulations in place of (
173
). We employ the approximation (
60
).
This holds in our case provided
\Delta t
is small enough. Choosing
K_{n}
as
implicitly defined in (
173
), we obtain the following mean field model:

\displaystyle\widehat{u}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=u_{n}+\xi_{n},

(174a)

\displaystyle u_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{u}_{n+1}+\Delta tK_{n}\Bigl({w}^{\dagger}_{R}-\frac{1}{2}\bigl(G_{R}(\widehat{u}_{n+1})+\widehat{o}_{n+1}\bigr)\Bigr),

(174b)

\displaystyle K_{n}

\displaystyle=\widehat{C}^{uG}_{R,n+1}\bigl(\Delta t\widehat{C}^{GG}_{R,n+1}+\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}\bigr)^{-1}.

(174c)

Here
\xi_{n}\sim\mathsf{N}\bigl(0,\beta\Delta tC_{n}\bigr).

All expectations used to define
(\widehat{C}^{uG}_{R,n+1},\widehat{C}^{GG}_{R,n+1},\widehat{o}_{n+1})

are given by (
172
), computed under the law of
\widehat{u}_{n+1}
.
This also exactly solves the filtering problem defined by (
170
),
in the linear Gaussian setting.

Remark 4.15
.

We note that it is possible to replace (
173
) by the
mean field model

\displaystyle\widehat{u}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=u_{n}+\frac{\gamma}{2}(u_{n}-\mathbb{E}u_{n}),

(175a)

\displaystyle\widehat{y}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\Delta tG_{R}(\widehat{u}_{n+1})+\eta_{n+1},

(175b)

\displaystyle u_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{u}_{n+1}+\widehat{C}^{uG}_{R,n+1}\bigl(\Delta t\widehat{C}^{GG}_{R,n+1}+\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}\bigr)^{-1}\bigl(\Delta t{w}^{\dagger}_{R}-\widehat{y}_{n+1}\bigr).

(175c)

Here the expectation on
u_{n}
is with respect to the approximate filtering distribution
generated by this model. Now note that the predictive mean and covariance defined by
(
173
) are governed by (
171
a), (
171
b).
The same equations govern the evolution of predictive mean and covariance of (
175
)
provided that we choose
\gamma
to be the unique positive solution of the equation

\gamma+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}=\Delta t\beta.
Thus the resulting methodology will coincide with
the Gaussian projected filter and with the ensemble Kalman filter on linear Gaussian problems.

\blacksquare

4.5.2
Algorithms for Optimization Formulation

Recall that we have introduced the non-standard mean field dynamical
system (
170
).
We have also described how the filtering distribution of the dynamical system
may be approximated by the Gaussian projected filter (
171
)
and the mean field ensemble Kalman filter (
173
).
In this subsection we substantiate the statements made about these algorithms in
Remark
4.14
.
We initially discuss algorithms with algebraic convergence, for
\beta=0
;
and then we introduce
generalization of the analysis to
\beta>0
which allow us to obtain exponential convergence.

Algebraic Convergence

Here we consider the setting of (
170
) where
\beta=0
.
Recall that for this choice of
\beta
, the stochastic dynamical system (
170
) reduces to (
169
). The following example illustrates that in the linear case where

G_{R}(\cdot)=L_{R}\cdot
, we recover an algebraic rate of convergence to the
posterior distribution when fixing
\Delta t
and taking
n\to\infty
.
This is analogous to Example
4.12
which considers algorithms
based on
L
, not
L_{R}
.

Example 4.16
.

Assume that
u_{0}
is initialized at a Gaussian

\mathsf{N}(m_{0},C_{0})
and assume also that
C_{0},\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}\succ 0
.
Consider the setting where

G_{R}(\cdot)=L_{R}\cdot
for matrix
L_{R}\in\mathbb{R}^{(d_{w}+d_{u})\times d_{u}}
.
Now consider the filtering distribution
u_{n}|Y^{\dagger}_{n}
given in
(
169
),
with
Y^{\dagger}_{n}
data defined
{y}^{\dagger}_{n}=\Delta t{w}^{\dagger}_{R},

with
{w}^{\dagger}_{R}
as in (
144
).

The desired filtering distribution is Gaussian
\mathsf{N}(m_{n},C_{n})
.
We now show that the iteration
(m_{n},C_{n})\mapsto(m_{n+1},C_{n+1})

converges to the posterior distribution, and does so at an algebraic rate.
The reader should compare this with Example
4.12
which,
using filtering based on
G
rather than
G_{R}
, and again in the linear case,
also results in algebraic convergence; furthermore convergence
is only in the image space under the forward map
L
.

To prove convergence to the posterior distribution we first identify the update equations for
(m_{n},C_{n})
. Note that the
predictive mean
\widehat{m}_{n+1}
and covariance
\widehat{C}_{n+1}

defined by (
169
a) trivially satisfy

\displaystyle\widehat{m}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=m_{n},

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=C_{n}.

To find
(m_{n+1},C_{n+1})
it is again convenient
to derive the formulae using
precision rather than covariance matrices. To this
end we view the Gaussian
\mathsf{N}(\widehat{m}_{n+1},\widehat{C}_{n+1})
as prior distribution
for the linear inverse problem defined by (
169
b) with data realization
{y}^{\dagger}_{n+1}=\Delta t{w}^{\dagger}_{R}.

Note that the likelihood, since linear and Gaussian, is conjugate to the prior
so that the posterior on
u_{n+1}|Y^{\dagger}_{n+1}
is Gaussian with mean
and covariance
(m_{n+1},C_{n+1})
which can be found by completing
the square:

\displaystyle C_{n+1}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{-1}+\Delta tL_{R}^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}^{-1}L_{R},

\displaystyle C_{n+1}^{-1}m_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{-1}\widehat{m}_{n+1}+\Delta tL_{R}^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}^{-1}{w}^{\dagger}_{R}.

We therefore find that

C_{n}^{-1}=C_{0}^{-1}+n\Delta tL_{R}^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}^{-1}L_{R}.

(176)

Recall that the posterior covariance
C_{\rm post}=(L_{R}^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}^{-1}L_{R})^{-1}

from (
149
)
is positive-definite. Hence it follows that the covariance converges to zero algebraically fast:
C_{n}=\mathcal{O}(1/n).

Now note that

C_{n}^{-1}m_{n}=C_{0}^{-1}m_{0}+n\Delta tL_{R}^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}^{-1}{w}^{\dagger}_{R},

so that

m_{n}=\Bigl(C_{0}^{-1}+n\Delta tL_{R}^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}^{-1}L_{R}\Bigr)^{-1}\Bigl(C_{0}^{-1}m_{0}+n\Delta tL_{R}^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}^{-1}{w}^{\dagger}_{R}\Bigr).

We deduce that, since the posterior mean is given by
(
150
),

m_{n}=CL_{R}^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}^{-1}{w}^{\dagger}_{R}+\mathcal{O}\bigl(1/n\bigr)=m_{\rm post}+\mathcal{O}\bigl(1/n\bigr),

(177)

again exhibiting algebraic convergence.
Combining (
176
) and (
177
) yields the result.
\blacksquare

Exponential Convergence

Example
4.16
shows that when
\beta=0

filtering based on (
170
) leads to convergence
to the posterior distribution at algebraic rate, in the linear
setting, when fixing
\Delta t
and taking
n\to\infty
.
We now show that when
\beta>0
an exponential rate of convergence to the posterior distribution is obtained.

Proposition 4.17
.

Assume that
u_{0}
is initialized at a Gaussian

\mathsf{N}(m_{0},C_{0})
and assume also that
C_{0},\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}\succ 0
. Consider the setting where

G_{R}(\cdot)=L_{R}\cdot
for matrix
L_{R}\in\mathbb{R}^{(d_{w}+d_{u})\times d_{u}}
.
Now consider the filtering distribution
u_{n}|Y^{\dagger}_{n}
defined by (
170
) for
\beta>0
, with data
Y^{\dagger}_{n}
defined by
{y}^{\dagger}_{n}=\Delta t{w}^{\dagger}_{R}
, where
{w}^{\dagger}_{R}
is defined in (
144
). Then the filtering distribution is Gaussian
\mathsf{N}(m_{n},C_{n})
for all
n\geq 1
. For any fixed
\Delta t>0
the mean and covariance converge at an exponential rate
(1+\Delta t\beta)^{-n}
, as
n\to\infty
, to the limits
m_{\infty}=m_{\rm post}
and

C_{\infty}=\frac{\beta}{1+\beta\Delta t}C_{\rm post}
, where
(m_{\rm post},C_{\rm post})
are the posterior mean (
150
) and covariance (
149
).

\Diamond

Remark 4.18
.

Motivated by this proposition we may use the Gaussian Projected filter (
171
) or the stochastic or deterministic Kalman transport algorithms, (
173
) and (
174
) respectively, to approximate the filtering distribution implied by (
170
). In so doing we generate approximate
solutions of the Tikhonov regularized optimization problem defined by (
140
). Furthermore
the exact solution is recovered in the linear Gaussian setting.

\blacksquare

Remark 4.19
.

It is a remarkable fact that the rate of convergence is independent of
the properties of the limiting Gaussian posterior distribution, and in
particular of the conditioning of the posterior covariance. This desirable
property is a result of the
affine invariance
of the Gaussian projected filter
and ensemble Kalman methods that we deploy in this subsection.
Affine invariance is a subject
we will study in more detail in the context of continuous time approaches to
inversion, developed in Section
5
. In this context we note that
the Definition
5.12
may be extended to discrete time algorithms.

\blacksquare

Proof 4.20

(Proof of Proposition
4.17
)
.

We first identify the update equations for
(m_{n},C_{n})
. Note that the
predictive mean
\widehat{m}_{n+1}
and covariance
\widehat{C}_{n+1}

defined by (
170
a) satisfy

\displaystyle\widehat{m}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=m_{n},

(178a)

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=(1+\beta\Delta t)C_{n}.

(178b)

To find
(m_{n+1},C_{n+1})
it is convenient
to derive the formulae using
precision rather than covariance matrices. To this
end we view the Gaussian
\mathsf{N}(\widehat{m}_{n+1},\widehat{C}_{n+1})
as prior distribution
for the linear inverse problem defined by (
170
b) conditioned on specific realization of the data
{w}^{\dagger}_{n+1}={w}^{\dagger}_{R}.

Note that the likelihood, since linear and Gaussian, is conjugate to the prior
so that the posterior on
u_{n+1}|W^{\dagger}_{n+1}
is Gaussian with mean
and covariance
(m_{n+1},C_{n+1})
which can be found by completing
the square:

\displaystyle C_{n+1}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{-1}+\Delta tL_{R}^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}^{-1}L_{R},

(179a)

\displaystyle C_{n+1}^{-1}m_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{C}_{n+1}^{-1}\widehat{m}_{n+1}+\Delta tL_{R}^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}^{-1}{w}^{\dagger}_{R}.

(179b)

Combining (
178
) and (
179
) shows that
(m_{n},C_{n})
update according to the formulae

\displaystyle C_{n+1}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\frac{1}{1+\beta\Delta t}\Bigr)C_{n}^{-1}+\Delta tL_{R}^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}^{-1}L_{R},

\displaystyle C_{n+1}^{-1}m_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\frac{1}{1+\beta\Delta t}\Bigr)C_{n}^{-1}m_{n}+\Delta tL_{R}^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}^{-1}{w}^{\dagger}_{R}.

We can therefore write

C_{n}^{-1}=\Bigl(\frac{1}{1+\beta\Delta t}\Bigr)^{n}C_{0}^{-1}+\left(\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\Bigl(\frac{1}{1+\beta\Delta t}\Bigr)^{k}\right)\Delta tL_{R}^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}^{-1}L_{R}.

and so

C_{n}^{-1}=\Bigl(\frac{1}{1+\beta\Delta t}\Bigr)^{n}C_{0}^{-1}+\frac{1+\beta\Delta t}{\beta}\left(1-\left(\frac{1}{1+\beta\Delta t}\right)^{n}\right)L_{R}^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}^{-1}L_{R}.

(180)

Recall the posterior covariance

C_{\rm post}=(L_{R}^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}^{-1}L_{R})^{-1}

given in (
149
).
It is clear that the precision converges exponentially fast to

C_{\rm post}^{-1}
, scaled by
\frac{1+\beta\Delta t}{\beta}
, and hence that the covariance converges exponentially fast to the appropriately scaled
C_{\rm post}.

Similarly we may write the expression for the mean as

C_{n}^{-1}m_{n}=\Bigl(\frac{1}{1+\beta\Delta t}\Bigr)^{n}C_{0}^{-1}m_{0}+\frac{1+\beta\Delta t}{\beta}\left(1-\left(\frac{1}{1+\beta\Delta t}\right)^{n}\right)L_{R}^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}^{-1}{w}^{\dagger}_{R},

so that the exponential convergence of the mean to the steady state
m_{\rm post}
given by (
150
) may be deduced, using the expression (
180
).

Remark 4.21
.

Recall the Hessian
\mathsf{S}
of
\Phi_{R}
, defined in (
149
).
Using the formulae for the predictive mean and covariance it is also easy to deduce that the expression for
m_{n+1}
is given by

m_{n+1}=\Bigl(\frac{1}{1+\beta\Delta t}\Bigr)\cdot m_{n}+\Bigl(\frac{\beta\Delta t}{1+\beta\Delta t}\Bigr)\cdot\mathsf{S}^{-1}L^{\top}_{R}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}^{-1}{w}^{\dagger}_{R},

and hence

m_{n+1}=m_{n}+\Bigl(\frac{\beta\Delta t}{1+\beta\Delta t}\Bigr)\cdot\mathsf{S}^{-1}\bigl(\mathsf{S}m_{n}+L^{\top}_{R}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}^{-1}{w}^{\dagger}_{R}\bigr).

(181)

Since
\nabla\Phi_{R}(u)=\mathsf{S}u+L^{\top}_{R}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}^{-1}{w}^{\dagger}_{R}
and
D^{2}\Phi_{R}(u)=\mathsf{S}
, the iteration (
181
) may be viewed as a Gauss-Newton scheme for minimizing
\Phi_{R}
.

\blacksquare

4.5.3
Algorithms for Bayesian Formulation

Again recall that we have introduced the non-standard mean field dynamical
system (
170
) and shown how
the filtering distribution of the dynamical system
may be approximated by the Gaussian projected filter (
171
)
and the mean field ensemble Kalman filter (
173
).
In this subsection we substantiate the statements made about these algorithms in
Remark
4.14
in relation to Bayesian sampling. In particular
we show that they exactly recover the posterior in the linear Gaussian setting
by choosing

\beta=\frac{1}{1-\Delta t}.

(182)

The following is a direct corollary of Proposition
4.17
. As in
Remark
4.19
we note that the rate of convergence, in this case to
the posterior distribution, is universal across all Gaussian posteriors.

Corollary 4.22
.

Assume that
u_{0}
is initialized at a Gaussian

\mathsf{N}(m_{0},C_{0})
and assume also that
C_{0},\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}\succ 0
. Consider the setting where

G_{R}(\cdot)=L_{R}\cdot
for matrix
L_{R}\in\mathbb{R}^{(d_{w}+d_{u})\times d_{u}}
.
Now consider the filtering distribution
u_{n}|Y^{\dagger}_{n}
defined by (
170
) for
\beta
given by (
182
), with data
Y^{\dagger}_{n}
defined by
{y}^{\dagger}_{n}=\Delta t{w}^{\dagger}_{R}
, where
{w}^{\dagger}_{R}
is defined in (
144
). Then the filtering distribution is Gaussian
\mathsf{N}(m_{n},C_{n})
for all
n\geq 1
. For any fixed
\Delta t>0
the mean and covariance converge at an exponential rate

(1-\Delta t)^{n}
, as
n\to\infty
, to the limits
m_{\infty}=m_{\rm post}
and

C_{\infty}=C_{\rm post}
, where
(m_{\rm post},C_{\rm post})
are the posterior mean (
150
) and covariance (
149
).

\Diamond

Remark 4.23
.

Motivated by this corollary, in the context of (
170
) we may use the Gaussian Projected filter (
171
) or the stochastic or deterministic Kalman transport algorithms, (
173
) and (
174
) respectively, to generate approximate
solutions of the Bayesian inverse problem
defined by (
140
), in the general nonlinear setting.
Of course, because these algorithms employ Gaussian approximations, this does not produce the exact filtering distribution.
Furthermore, to make resulting algorithms tractable we will predict in (
170
a)
using the covariance
C_{n}
of the Gaussian projected filter or
the Kalman transport algorithm, rather than the covariance under the true filtering distribution, which is not tractable: see final paragraph in Remark
4.13
. We note, however, that in the linear Gaussian case all the algorithms are exact and hence recover the true posterior.

\blacksquare

4.6
Ensemble Kalman Methods For Inversion: Examples

In this Section
4
we have concentrated, so far, entirely on mean field models, leaving the details of deriving particle approximations to the reader. In this subsection, however, we make a brief foray into finite particle ensemble approximations of the mean field models introduced in our discussion of inverse problems. The algorithms we employ can be found in Appendix
7
as Algorithms
3
,
4
and
5
.

Algorithms
3
,
4
are based on finite particle approximations
of the mean field model in (
164
). Algorithm
3
is based on iterating
until
N
satisfying
N\Delta t=1
and, at that time-step, aims to approximate the posterior;
Algorithm
4
performs the same iteration but to
N_{\infty}
assumed to satisfy

N_{\infty}\Delta t\gg 1
so that it approximately solves an optimization problem – see the discussion
at the start of Subsection
4.5.1
.
Algorithm
5
is based on (
173
) and aims to approximate the
posterior by iterating to
N_{\infty}:N_{\infty}\Delta t\gg 1.

In Example
4.24
we study a one-dimensional nonlinear inverse problem;
working in one dimension enables comparison of the true posterior distribution with approximations
arising from the various ensemble Kalman inversion schemes described in preceding subsections. We also demonstrate an optimization approach to inversion, in the context of Example
4.24
. Subsequently, in Example
4.25
, we return to the setting of the Lorenz ‘96 dynamical system,
now to estimate unknown parameters rather than the state; our focus is on studying ensemble Kalman methods from the perspective of the optimization approach to the parameter estimation problem.

Example 4.24
.

Recall Example
4.10
concerning the linear Gaussian inverse
problem; there we show that the exact posterior is obtained either by iterating
(
162
)
N
times or by evaluating (
163
) at
n=N.
Although derived in the context of the Gaussian projected filter
the example also applies to
mean field ensemble Kalman methods since they, like the Gaussian projected
filter, are exact for linear Gaussian problems.
However in the nonlinear case this equivalence does not hold exactly,
because of approximations that are made by the Gaussian projected and
ensemble Kalman filtering methods. In this example
we study the effect of these approximations by examining the behaviour of
particle-based ensemble Kalman methods applied to a nonlinear inverse problem.

We consider the setting of (
140
) with a nonlinear forward map
G:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, given by

G(u)=\frac{7}{12}u^{3}-\frac{7}{2}u^{2}+8u.

(183)

The observational noise is assumed to be of the form
\eta\sim\mathsf{N}(0,1)
. Assuming observation
{w}^{\dagger}=2
results in likelihood

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\pi}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{2}(G(u)-2)^{2}\Bigr).

(184)

Furthermore, assuming a Gaussian prior of mean
-2
and variance
1/2
, the posterior on
u|{w}^{\dagger}
is proportional to

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\pi}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{2}(G(u)-2)^{2}-(u+2)^{2}\Bigr).

(185)

We note that the forward map
G(u)
is monotonic and the posterior (
185
) is unimodal: in the first panel of Figure
4.1
we display the true posterior distribution, computed via quadrature. We also show the Gaussian prior and the likelihood (
184
).

The second panel of Figure
4.1
shows the ensemble Kalman inversion iteration (
164
) from Subsection
4.4
, which is designed to transport prior to posterior in finite time, fixing
N
iterations and
\Delta t
so that
N\Delta t=1
; see Algorithm
3
. Recall that the iteration exactly recovers the posterior, in the mean field limit, when applied to linear Gaussian inverse problems (Example
4.10
) but that here the inverse problem is nonlinear and non-Gaussian. We employ this algorithm with
J=2\cdot 10^{3}
ensemble members. We run Algorithm
3
with two choices of
N:

N=4\cdot 10^{3}
and hence
\Delta t=2.5\cdot 10^{-4};
and with
N=1
and hence
\Delta t=1.
The second panel of Figure
4.1
shows that the one-step approach, with
N=1
, leads to a very poor approximation of the posterior. In contrast, the scheme with
N=4\cdot 10^{3}
yields reasonable approximation quality of the posterior; see Remark
4.6
. In this panel we also run Algorithm
4
for the finite number of iterations
N_{\infty}=10^{6}
, with
\Delta t=2.5\cdot 10^{-4}
. The result reflects the theoretical interpretation: iterating
n\to\infty
leads to solution of the optimization formulation of ensemble Kalman inversion and, as discussed in Subsection
4.5
, results in convergence to a Dirac centered at the minimizer of the least squares functional
\Phi,
the maximum likelihood estimate found by maximizing (
184
);
this simply delivers the point
G^{-1}(2).

The third panel of Figure
4.1
shows the ensemble Kalman inversion iteration (
173
) from Subsection
4.5
, namely Algorithm
5
. This is designed to approximate the true posterior, when
1/\beta=1-\Delta t
: indeed Corollary
4.22
shows that in the linear setting the mean field model (
173
) converges to the true posterior in limit
n\to\infty
, with this choice of
\beta.
Our numerical results,
which are conducted with this choice of
\beta
, show that use of the Algorithm
5
, when applied to the nonlinear and non-Gaussian inverse problem, produces an excellent posterior
approximation; this demonstrates that the linear theory is indicative of
behaviour of the algorithm beyond
the linear Gaussian setting.

\blacksquare

Figure 4.1
:
The plots display the results obtained for the inverse problem described by the one-dimensional nonlinear forward map (
183
). The first panel displays the prior, likelihood and posterior computed via quadrature. In the second panel, we compare the true posterior pdf with the approximation obtained using the finite ensemble Kalman inversion iteration (
164
), as detailed in Algorithm
3
, with
\Delta t=2.5\cdot 10^{-4}
and
\Delta t=1
,
iterated to time
n=N
where
N\Delta t=1.
The second
panel also includes results found from applying the optimization Algorithm
4
, iterating over
N_{\infty}=10^{6}
steps with
\Delta t=2.5\cdot 10^{-4}
; in this case, the ensemble approaches the Dirac measure supported on the minimizer of the unregularized least-squares loss
\Phi,
given by the peak of the likelihood.
Similarly in the third panel we display the posterior approximation using the ensemble Kalman inversion iteration with covariance inflation as described by the mean field model (
173
), as detailed in Algorithm
5
, with
\beta=\frac{1}{1-\Delta t}
for
parameter
\Delta t=2.5\cdot 10^{-4}
; the algorithm is iterated over
N_{\infty}=10^{5}
steps. It is clear that this scheme yields the highest quality posterior approximation.

Example 4.25
.

As in the examples from Section
2
, we again consider the Lorenz ’96 (singlescale) model for
v\in C(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathbb{R}^{L})
satisfying the equations

\displaystyle\dot{v}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=-v_{\ell-1}(v_{\ell-2}-v_{\ell+1})-v_{\ell}+u+h_{v}m\bigl(v_{\ell}\bigr),\quad\ell=1\dots L\,,

(186a)

\displaystyle v_{\ell+L}

\displaystyle=v_{\ell},\quad\ell=1\dots L\,.

(186b)

As before we set
L=9,h_{v}=-0.8
and
u=10
.
We recall that function
m
is shown in Figure
2.1
.
In Section
2
we focused on recovering the state
v
from partial and
noisy observations. Here we concentrate on recovering the
parameter
u
.
25
25

25

We have used the notation
u
instead of
F
,
for the forcing parameter, to align with
the notation for the unknown parameter used throughout the section
concerning inverse problems.

Our objective is to recover parameter

u
from time-averaged data. We assume that the system is ergodic so that infinite time-averages
produce averages over the invariant measure. Furthermore we assume that convergence in time,
of averages, is governed by a central limit theorem. We let

G_{T}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}
denote the mean and variance,
defined via averaging over time
T
and
over the
L
components of
v
, of the state of system (
186
).
In principle
G_{T}
depends also on intialization, but this
effect is negligible for
T
large, and zero for
T=\infty
, by ergodicity.
In particular, with system state
{v}^{\dagger}
evolving according to

{v}^{\dagger}_{n+1}=\Psi({v}^{\dagger}_{n}),

(187)

where
\Psi
is the solution operator for (
186
) over the observation time interval
\tau
, with true parameter
u=u^{\dagger}
,
the action of forward operator
G_{T}
on
u
is defined as follows:

G_{T}(u)=\begin{pmatrix}w_{1}\\
w_{2}\end{pmatrix},

with, for
M\tau=T
,

\displaystyle w_{1}=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\bar{v}^{\dagger}_{l},\quad w_{2}=\frac{1}{L\cdot{M}}\sum_{n=1}^{M}\sum_{l=1}^{L}(v^{\dagger}_{n;l}-\bar{v}^{\dagger}_{l})^{2},\quad\bar{v}^{\dagger}_{l}=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{n=1}^{M}v^{\dagger}_{n;l},

where we have used
v^{\dagger}_{n;l}
to denote the
l^{th}
variable in
vector
{v}^{\dagger}_{n}
.

We consider finding
u
from an observation
w\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
arising from the model

w=G_{\infty}(u)+\gamma.

(188)

In practice the specific realization
{w}^{\dagger}
, from which we invert to find
u
,
is found by integrating
G_{T}
to a finite time
T=100
, not
T=\infty
.
Variable
\gamma\sim\text{N}(0,\mathsf{\Gamma})
accounts for the
resulting central limit theorem correction.
To solve the parameter estimation problem for
u
we use ensemble Kalman methods in Algorithms
3
,
4
and
5
. We do not have access to
G_{\infty}
and so, instead,
the algorithms are implemented by using
G_{T}
with
T=10
, initialized after a burn-in time of duration
t^{*}=10
.
The burn-in phase itself results from an initial condition chosen at random from a Gaussian distribution with mean
0
and standard deviation
40
. In the experiments shown we take
\mathsf{\Gamma}=\sigma^{2}I
, with
\sigma=10^{-1}
. All the ensemble Kalman inversion schemes are initialized
from a prior Gaussian of mean
0
and standard deviation
10
, and use an
ensemble of size
J=30.

In Figure
4.2
we display the ensemble approximations obtained via application of the EKI methodology for optimization, namely Algorithm
4
. In practice, the scheme is run for a finite number
N_{\infty}
of iterations. Indeed, in the first panel of Figure
4.2
the scheme is applied with
\Delta t=5\cdot 10^{-2}
and run up to
N_{\infty}=40
iterations. On the other hand, in the second panel the scheme is run for
20
iterations with
\Delta t=1
. In both settings ensemble collapse occurs as the number of iterations grow. The ensemble mean, displayed as the central line in each box plot, converges to a point yielding a qualitatively good estimate of the true forcing parameter, with an error of
\mathcal{O}(10^{-1})
.

Figure 4.2
:
The figure displays box and whisker plots for the ensembles produced by Algorithm
4
with
\Delta t=5\cdot 10^{-2}
(top panel) and
\Delta t=1
(bottom panel). The box and whisker plots represent the ensembles by depicting the ensemble mean, as a line within the shaded region. Furthermore, the edges of the boxes represent the first and third quartiles, i.e. the values of ensemble members corresponding to the median of the first half of the samples, and the median of the second half of the samples, respectively. Finally, the whiskers mark the furthest samples lying within a distance from the box of
1.5
times the distance between the first and third quartiles (the interquartile range). In both cases we note ensemble collapse onto a value close to the truth underlying the data.

In Figure
4.3
we show application of Algorithm
3
with
\Delta t
set to
5\cdot 10^{-2}
, running for
N=20
steps (
N\Delta t=1
) and of Algorithm
5
with

1/\beta=1-\Delta t
and
\Delta t=5\cdot 10^{-2}
for
N_{\infty}=40
steps.
For linear inverse problems, the output of both algorithms at these specific steps
delivers the posterior distribution exactly in the mean field limit, by Example
4.10

and Corollary
4.22
. As noted in the previous paragraph, such a posterior approximation should be interpreted with caution for this nonlinear inverse problem. However we show in Figure
4.3
that the ensemble means accurately predict the true forcing up to an error of
\mathcal{O}(10^{-1})

and that, furthermore, the two ensembles are similar. However, interpreting the posterior
distributions in this case is harder as we do not have access to the true posterior. We note that in the preceding Example
4.24
we were able to demonstrate that Algorithm
5
delivered a better posterior approximation than Algorithm
3
and it would be interesting
to determine whether such a conclusion holds more generally.

\blacksquare

Figure 4.3
:
The figure displays box and whisker plots for the Bayesian posterior ensemble approximation produced by Algorithm
3
with
\Delta t=5\cdot 10^{-2}
and Algorithm
5
with
\Delta t=5\cdot 10^{-2}
. The box and whisker plots represent the ensembles by depicting the ensemble mean, as a line within the shaded region. Furthermore, the edges of the boxes represent the first and third quartiles, i.e. the values of ensemble members corresponding to the median of the first half of the samples, and the median of the second half of the samples, respectively. Finally, the whiskers mark the furthest samples lying within a distance from the box of
1.5
times the distance between the first and third quartiles (the interquartile range). The posterior mean in both cases in close to the true value of the parameter underlying the data; the size of the posterior spread is also similar. Note, however, that in this problem we do not have a true posterior distribution against which to compare.

4.7
Bibliographical Notes

Sequential Monte Carlo methods may be used to approximately morph one probability distribution (source) into another (target), using empirical approximation and a discrete-time homotopy
(
Del Moral et al. 2006
,
Chopin and Papaspiliopoulos 2020
)
. In general the methodology does not scale well to high dimensional problems
(
Beskos et al. 2014
)
. However some success has been achieved in this direction
(
Kantas et al. 2014
)
,
and a basic underlying theory is described in
Beskos et al. 2015
. Our presentation in this paper is confined to the setting of ensemble Kalman methods
because of their empirical success and scalability to high dimensions.

The development of ensemble Kalman methods for inverse
problems was pioneered in the study of reservoir simulation,
in the context of learning subsurface properties from localized
flow measurements
(
Chen and Oliver 2012
,
Gu and Oliver 2007
,
Li and Reynolds 2009
,
Emerick and Reynolds 2013a
,
Emerick and Reynolds 2013b
,
Evensen 2018
)
.
Subsequent work has studied parameter estimation in chaotic dynamical systems, such as those arising in weather forecasting
using ensemble methods for joint state and parameter estimation

(
Pulido et al. 2018
,
Bocquet et al. 2020
,
Gottwald and Reich 2021
)
, and by matching to
time-averaged statistics
(
Schneider et al. 2017
,
Cleary et al. 2021
,
Dunbar et al. 2021
)
, motivated by climate modeling.
The chaotic dynamics that underlie weather and climate models lead to complicated energy landscapes for minimization and sampling
(
Lea et al. 2000
)
. The papers
Huang et al. 2022b
,
Dunbar et al. 2022
demonstrate the benefits of using ensemble methods, rather than computing exact
derivatives, for such problems: the ensemble approach effectively works in
a smoothened energy landscape.

The idea of transporting prior to posterior, as developed in
Subsection
4.4.1
, is widely used in the statistics literature; see
Chopin and Papaspiliopoulos 2020
,
Del Moral et al. 2006
for
a unified perspective and for citations to earlier works which characterize the deformation of one measure to the other either through incrementally building up the available data, or through a temperature like annealing
parameter in the likelihood. In the context of data assimilation, and ensemble Kalman methods in particular, these ideas were developed by
Li and Reynolds 2009
,
Gu and Oliver 2007
,
Daum et al. 2010
,
Reich 2011
, and
Sakov et al. 2012
.

Filtering using dynamical systems with equilibrium filtering distribution which solves the inverse problem, the approach adopted in Section
4.5
,
is an idea developed in
Iglesias et al. 2013
. That paper also highlights
an invariant subspace property of finite ensemble Kalman methods for
inverse problems: the iteration remains in the linear span of the initial
ensemble. More recent
methodology is developed in the context of
optimization in
Huang et al. 2022b
, although the methodology therein is
not affine invariant; it is also developed
for sampling in
Huang et al. 2022a
s, resulting in an affine
invariant methodology. See those papers for details concerning uniqueness of, and exponential convergence to, steady state solutions in the linear Gaussian setting. Here we have adopted the approach of
Huang et al. 2022a
to both optimization and sampling, which yields exponential convergence under both settings, as discussed in Proposition
4.17
.
A geometric picture of iterative ensemble Kalman methods for inverse problems is developed in
Qian and Beattie 2024
; the authors consider the fundamental observed
and unobserved subspaces defined by linear inverse problems and their
interaction with the invariant subspace defined by ensemble Kalman iteration.

The stochastic perturbations utilized in
(
170
) are closely related to multiplicative ensemble inflation methods as widely used in ensemble Kalman filter implementations
(
Asch et al. 2016
,
Evensen et al. 2022
)
. The effects of additive inflation and variable step-size implementations of ensemble Kalman inversion have been studied in
Chada and Tong 2022
and in
Weissmann et al. 2022
.

The optimization and sampling approaches for inverse problems can in principle be combined with ideas from stochastic annealing
(
Kushner and Yin 2003
)
, and stochastic gradient descent
(
Goodfellow et al. 2016
)
.
The papers
Haber et al. 2018
,
Kovachki and Stuart 2019
,
Pidstrigach and Reich 2023
demonstrate the use of ensemble Kalman methods for inversion,
when combined with stochastic gradient descent, and mini-batching in particular, as well as the application of ensemble Kalman methods beyond the setting of the
L_{2}
-loss functions
\Phi
and
\Phi_{R}
considered here.

However, despite the growing use of ensemble Kalman methods to solve inverse problems, it is important to appreciate that all ensemble Kalman-based methods invoke approximations which amount to matching only first and second order statistics, at some point in the algorithmic development. For this reason the methods are intuitively only useful as samplers for problems with posterior distribution close to a Gaussian. This idea is carefully explained in
Ernst et al. 2015
where the mean field limit of ensemble Kalman methods for inverse problems is compared with the desired posterior distribution;
as is the case for state estimation, analysis is required to justify use of ensemble Kalman methods beyond the linear and Gaussian regime. We also highlight that our analysis in this paper, which focuses on the mean field limit, does not capture important aspects of the performance of ensemble Kalman methods at finite ensemble size, and the important practical
issue of covariance localization; these issues are studied in

Al-Ghattas and Sanz-Alonso 2023
. Furthermore, the paper
Tong and Morzfeld 2023
studies the relationship between localization, in the solution of inverse problems
using ensemble Kalman methods, and the subspace property explained in
Iglesias et al. 2013
[Theorem 2.1]. As an alternative to localization, the concept of dropout combined with variable step-size has been shown to lead to optimal algorithmic performance in
Liu et al. 2023
.

The idea of using ensemble methods for performing the optimization step within variational data assimilation was introduced in
Zupanski 2005
. The connection between iterative applications of the ensemble Kalman filter and optimization were first investigated in
Iglesias et al. 2013
and developed to include constraints in
Albers et al. 2019
,
Chada et al. 2019
and Tikhonov regularization
Chada et al. 2020
. Recall from Subsection
4.5.2
the algebraic rates of convergence arising
in the basic optimization method arising from iterating to infinity. This undesirable feature of optimization methods based on statistical linearization of mean field gradient descent can be ameliorated to some extent by the use of adaptive time-steps, connections to the Levenberg-Marquadt algorithm, and the use of stopping criteria; see

Iglesias 2015
,

Iglesias 2016
,
and
Iglesias and Yang 2021
.
Recent work of
Parzer and Scherzer 2022
has developed a systematic theory for early stopping using ensemble Kalman inversion including incorporation of Nyström methodology. Other interacting particle system approaches to optimization have been proposed, including feedback particle
(
Zhang et al. 2017
)
, unscented Kalman approaches
(
Huang et al. 2022b
,
Huang et al. 2022a
)
, and concensus based optimization
(
Tsianos et al. 2012
,
Carrillo et al. 2018
,
Fornasier et al. 2020
,
Ha et al. 2021
)
.

Finally we note that Kalman methods have been related to approximate Bayesian computation (ABC) methodologies, utilizing a linear regression ansatz

(
Sisson et al. 2018
,
Nott et al. 2012
)
.
Such methods are in turn closely related to
Bayes linear and best linear unbiased estimators (BLUE) as discussed in
Goldstein and Rougier 2006
,

Goldstein and Wooff 2007
,

Lei and Bickel 2011
,

Nott et al. 2012
,

Snyder 2014
,

Goldstein 2014
,

Reich and Cotter 2015
, and

Latz 2016
. BLUE is discussed in more detail in
Appendix
9.3
.

5
Inverse Problems: Continuous Time

In this section we derive continuous time limits of the ideas developed in the preceding
Section
4
for the solution of inverse problems. As a consequence the
ideas may also be viewed as adaptations of Section
3
to the solution of inverse problems.
We start in Section
5.1
by recalling the inverse problem,
followed in Subsections
5.2
and
5.3
by discussion of the
optimization and Bayesian approaches respectively, focusing on gradient flows; this flow
perspective provides a conceptual basis for thinking about the algorithms for inverse problems
that we will subsequently develop. Subsection
5.4

is devoted to Bayesian probabilistic filtering methods which solve the inverse problem by morphing the
prior into the posterior in a finite time. Subsection
5.5
discusses filtering methods
which work on infinite time horizons, exhibiting exponential convergence to approximate
solutions of the optimization or Bayesian formulations of the problem, from arbitrary starting points.
Analogously to Section
4
we demonstrate application of Gaussian projected filtering and ensemble Kalman methods to solve the filtering problems defined in Subsections
5.4
and
5.5
; the comments from Remark
4.1
apply here too.
We conclude in Section
5.6
with bibliographic notes.

5.1
Set-Up

Recall the inverse problem (
140
) of recovering
u
from
w
where

w=G(u)+\gamma,

introduced in full detail in Subsection
4.1
.
Under the assumptions laid out in Subsection
4.3
, and in particular
Gaussianity and independence of unknown parameter
u
and noise
\eta
, and given a specific
realization
{w}^{\dagger}
of data
w
, we have a posterior
distribution on the random variable
u|{w}^{\dagger}
which is defined by
26
26

26

The normalization constant
\mathscr{Z}
is the probability of the observed data under the model, sometimes called the
evidence
.

\displaystyle\mu(\mathrm{d}u)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathscr{Z}}\exp\bigl(-\Phi_{R}(u)\bigr)\mathrm{d}u,

(189a)

\displaystyle\mathscr{Z}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}}\exp\bigl(-\Phi_{R}(u)\bigr)\mathrm{d}u.

(189b)

Here

\displaystyle\Phi(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}|{w}^{\dagger}-G(u)|_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}^{2},

(190a)

\displaystyle\Phi_{R}(u)

\displaystyle=\Phi(u)+\frac{1}{2}|u-m_{0}|_{C_{0}}^{2},

(190b)

for prior mean vector vector
m_{0}
, prior covariance matrix
C_{0}\succ 0
and noise
covariance matrix
\mathsf{\Gamma}\succ 0
.

Rather than solving the Bayesian inverse problem, which can be prohibitively expensive,
optimization methods may be developed to find a point estimate of
u|{w}^{\dagger}
as minimizer
of
\Phi
over a compact set, or as minimizer of
\Phi_{R}
over the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}.

The next two sections show, respectively, how we may develop continuous time gradient
flows which minimize
\Phi_{R}
, or
\Phi
, and gradient flows which find the posterior distribution
\mu
.

5.2
Optimization Formulation: Gradient Flows

The goal of this subsection is to study gradient flows to minimize
an objective function. For us particular focus is on the choice
of
\Phi
or
\Phi_{R}
as objective, but since some of the considerations are quite general we frame aspects of the discussion
in a general setting.

Deterministic Viewpoint

Consider the standard gradient descent, applied to an energy function
\Psi:\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
namely

\frac{\mathrm{d}u}{\mathrm{d}t}=-\nabla\Psi(u).

(191)

Note that this may be found as the continuous time limit of the
discrete time gradient descent algorithm (
145
),
choosing
\alpha=\Delta t
, letting
u(n\Delta t)=u_{n}
and taking the limit
\Delta t\to 0.

Along solutions of (
191
),

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Psi(u)

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\nabla\Psi(u),\frac{\mathrm{d}u}{\mathrm{d}t}\Big\rangle

(192a)

\displaystyle=-\Bigl|\frac{\mathrm{d}u}{\mathrm{d}t}\Bigr|^{2}.

(192b)

Equation (
191
) is said to possess a gradient flow structure in parameter space
\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}

because the vector field driving the evolution of
u
is tangential
to the gradient of the
energy

\Psi(u)
in the standard Euclidean

metric
; this is the geometric reason for the non-increasing
property of
\Psi(u)
along trajectories.

Geometric Perspective on the Space of Probability Densities

We now define gradient flow structure from a probabilistic viewpoint, studying evolution of probability densities.
Again we need both an energy and a metric. To this end we introduce some
notation that will be useful in the probabilistic
formulation of (
191
). It will also be used more generally in subsequent discussion of other gradient flows on the space of probability density functions.

We denote the manifold of all smooth probability
density functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}
by
{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}={\mathfrak{P}}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}})
.
We may then define the tangent space
T_{\rho}{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}
to
{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}
, at
\rho\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}
, by

\displaystyle T_{\rho}{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}=\left\{\sigma\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}})\,:\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}}\sigma(u)\,\mathrm{d}u=0\right\}\,.

(193)

Given the tangent space

T_{\rho}{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}
we define its dual
27
27

27

This informal definition of tangent space, and its dual space, requires careful handling for probability measures on non-compact manifolds, such as
\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}
; see citations to the literature in the bibliography Subsection
5.6
. The dual is also known as the cotangent space.

\displaystyle T_{\rho}{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}^{\star}=\left\{\psi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}})\,:\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}}\psi(u)\,\rho(u)\,\mathrm{d}u=0\right\}\,.

(194)

In this article, for simplicity of exposition, we will define
underlying metric structure via the positive operator
\mathcal{M}(\rho):T_{\rho}{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}\to T_{\rho}{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}^{\star}
. A precise mathematical treatment requires further assumptions on the considered set
{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}
. See the bibliography for relevant literature on this topic.
Operator
\mathcal{M}(\rho)
may be linked to an underlying Riemannian metric tensor
g_{\rho}\,:\,T_{\rho}{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}\times T_{\rho}{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
; however, since
this metric tensor plays no role in our presentation, we will
work directly with
\mathcal{M}(\rho)
, and with its inverse

\mathcal{M}(\rho)^{-1}:T_{\rho}{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}^{\star}\to T_{\rho}{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}
.
Note that
\mathcal{M}(\rho)^{-1}
maps into the tangent space
T_{\rho}{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}
, implying that it maps into functions that integrate to zero over
\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}
; see (
193
).

Probabilistic Viewpoint

We now demonstrate gradient structure inherent in the probabilistic viewpoint on the ODE (
191
), arising from allowing the initial condition
u(0)
to be random.
We will show that the Liouville equation governing the evolution
of the probability density function associated with the random
variable
u(t)
also has a gradient structure. In so doing
we must exhibit an appropriate energy and metric.

We assume
that
u(t)
has smooth probability density
\rho(u,t)
for
all
t\geq 0
.
Then
\rho
satisfies the Liouville equation

\partial_{t}\rho=\nabla\cdot(\rho\nabla\Psi).

(195)

The energy and metric defining the gradient structure for equation (
195
) are

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\rho)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}}\Psi(u)\rho(u)\mathrm{d}u,

(196a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(\rho)^{-1}\psi

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\nabla\cdot(\rho\nabla\psi)\in T_{\rho}{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}.

(196b)

Operator
\mathcal{M}(\rho)
corresponds to an underlying

Wasserstein-2 metric structure.

The standard
L_{2}
variational derivative
28
28

28

The
L_{2}
variational derivative is identified by writing
\mathcal{E}(\rho+\sigma)-\mathcal{E}(\rho)
as a linear operator acting on
\sigma
(plus higher order terms in
\sigma
for energies
\mathcal{E}(\rho)
which are
not linear in
\rho
).
of
\mathcal{E}
is given by

\frac{\delta\mathcal{E}}{\delta\rho}=\Psi.

The restriction of this variational derivative to the dual space
T_{\rho}{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}^{\star}
is provided by

\displaystyle\frac{\delta\mathcal{E}}{\delta\rho}_{|T_{\rho}{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}^{\ast}}=\Psi-\mathbb{E}[\Psi].

(197)

In the context of the Wasserstein-2 metric structure as presented
here it is not necessary to distinguish between these two formulations of the variational derivative; however the second formulation allows
for unique solvability of the elliptic equation required to define
\mathcal{M}(\rho).
For the Fisher–Rao metric structure considered later in Section
5.4
the second definition will play a more direct role. Hence we can rewrite (
195
) as

\partial_{t}\rho=\nabla\cdot\Bigl(\rho\nabla\frac{\delta\mathcal{E}}{\delta\rho}\Bigr).

(198)

This may be written abstractly as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho

\displaystyle=-{\mathcal{M}(\rho)^{-1}}\frac{\delta\mathcal{E}}{\delta{\rho}}(\rho).

(199)

From this it follows that

\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{E}(\rho)=\Bigl\langle\frac{\delta\mathcal{E}}{\delta{\rho}}(\rho),\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial t}\Bigr\rangle=-\Bigl\langle\mathcal{M}(\rho)\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial t},\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial t}\Bigr\rangle\leq 0.

(200)

Hence the energy is decreasing along trajectories and the
gradient structure is apparent.

Remark 5.1
.

It is interesting to compare the
gradient structure (
200
)
on
{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}
to the gradient flow structure on
\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}
defined by
(
192
). The state space
gradient flow on
\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}
ensures decrease of
\Psi\bigl(u(t)\bigr)

along trajectories whilst the probability space gradient flow on

{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}
ensures decrease of the expected value of
\Psi\bigl(u(t)\bigr)

across a distribution of trajectories found from random initialization of the state space problem.

\blacksquare

5.3
Bayesian Formulation: Gradient Flows

In this section we study the Langevin SDE

\mathrm{d}u=-\nabla\Phi_{R}(u)\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\mathrm{d}W.

(201)

This is a noisy version of (
191
) in the case where
\Psi=\Phi_{R}.
It may be also found as
a sample path instantiation of the continuous time limit of the MCMC algorithm (
152
), typically arising when
\alpha

is the standard deviation of the proposal, choosing
\alpha=\Delta t
, letting
\rho(\cdot,n\Delta t)=\rho_{n}(\cdot)
and sending
\Delta t\to 0;

see bibliography Subsection
5.6
for details.

The probability density function for the SDE (
201
)
is governed by the Fokker-Planck equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho

\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot(\rho\nabla\Phi_{R})+\nabla\cdot(\nabla\rho)

(202a)

\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot(\rho\nabla\Phi_{R}+\rho\nabla\ln\rho),

(202b)

This equation has the density of the Bayesian posterior distribution
(
189
) as steady state. This can be seen by noting that the right-hand side
is divergence of a quantity which is zero if

\nabla(\Phi_{R}+\ln\rho)=0.

This quantity can in turn be made zero by choosing

\rho\propto\exp(-\Phi_{R}),

so that
\rho
is given by the posterior distribution (
189
).
As a consequence of the fact that the posterior is a steady
state of the Fokker-Planck equation (
202
), the Langevin SDE
(
201
) plays an
important role in understanding algorithms for Bayesian inversion.

The machinery we established in the previous subsection, concerning
gradient flows in the space of probability measures, is very powerful and demonstrates that any evolution equation of type (
198
)
with appropriate potential
\mathcal{E}
induces a gradient flow on
{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}

with respect to the Wasserstein-2 metric.
The Fokker-Planck equation (
202
) associated with
the Langevin equation (
201
)
may be cast in this framework by making the choice

\mathcal{E}(\rho)=\int\left(\Phi_{R}+\ln\rho\right)\rho\,\mathrm{d}u

(203)

with Fréchet derivative given by

\frac{\delta\mathcal{E}}{\delta\rho}=\Phi_{R}+\ln\rho.

Remark 5.2
.

We observe that, for
\mathrm{KL}[\cdot\|\cdot]
denoting the

Kullback-Leibler divergence
,

\displaystyle{\rm{KL}}[\rho\|\pi]=\int\rho\log\Bigl(\frac{\rho}{\pi}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}u=\mathcal{E}(\rho)+\log\mathscr{Z},

(204)

where
\pi
is the posterior density associated with posterior measure
\mu
given by (
146
)
29
29

29

This notation is used throughout Section
5
and is not to be confused with the notation used for the joint law of state and data in previous sections.

and the normalization constant
\mathscr{Z}
is defined in (
189
b).
It is thus possible to choose the energy to be
\mathrm{KL}[\rho\|\pi]
,
since shifts by a constant in the energy do not change the evolution equations (
198
) and (
199
).

By the property of a divergence, the global minimizer of
\mathcal{E}(\rho)

is attained at
\rho=\pi
and hence solves the Bayesian inverse problem.
It is thus of considerable value to have
identified a gradient flow to minimize
\mathcal{E}(\rho)
since such
minimizers solve the Bayesian inverse problem.
Theory concerning the equation (
202
) as a gradient
flow is contained in the bibliography Subsection
5.6
.

\blacksquare

In summary, the Fokker-Planck equation may be written in the abstract gradient form (
199
). We choose

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\rho)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{E}(\rho)=\int\left(\Phi_{R}+\ln\rho\right)\rho\,\mathrm{d}u,

(205a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(\rho)^{-1}\psi

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\nabla\cdot(\rho\nabla\psi)\in T_{\rho}{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}.

(205b)

This should be compared with (
196
) with the choice
\Psi=\Phi_{R}
: the
metric structure defined by
\mathcal{M}
is the same, but the energy
\mathcal{E}(\rho)
has an additional
term accounting for the Brownian motion appearing in (
201
).

5.4
Finite-Time Algorithms

The idea used in this subsection, to address the solution of
inverse problems, is a continuous time analog of
Subsection
4.4
.
From a sequential formulation of Bayesian inference we derive
a filtering problem whose solution, at a particular time,
gives the desired posterior.
Subsection
5.4.1
is devoted to the formulation,
Subsection
5.4.2
to the use of Gaussian projected filters
and Subsection
5.4.3
to the use of ensemble Kalman methods.

5.4.1
Formulation

Employing the reparametrization (
86
a) in (
159
) and taking
the continuum limit yields the SDE

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=0,

(206a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=G(u)\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\mathrm{d}B,

(206b)

with
B
a unit Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}.

Given a specific realization of the observation process
z^{\dagger}(\cdot)
we define
Z^{\dagger}(t)=\{{z}^{\dagger}(s)\}_{0\leq s\leq t},
and consider the
filtering distribution for the random variable
u(t)|Z^{\dagger}(t).
However
there is a twist on the standard filtering setting: we are interested in
the case where the data has constant derivative:
\mathrm{d}{z}^{\dagger}(t)/\mathrm{d}t={w}^{\dagger}.
Since
the path
{z}^{\dagger}
has zero quadratic variation the probability distribution is found by setting
{z}^{\dagger}(t)=t{w}^{\dagger}
within the Stratonovich formulation of the nonlocal evolution equation for the density. Referring to
(
103
) we see that this yields the following
evolution for density
\rho(u,t)
of
u(t)|Z^{\dagger}(t):

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho=\left\langle G-\mathbb{E}G,{w}^{\dagger}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\rho-\frac{1}{2}\left\{\left|G\right|^{2}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}-\mathbb{E}\left|G\right|^{2}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\right\}\rho.

(207)

Here
\mathbb{E}
denotes integration with respect to density
\rho(\cdot,t)

so that the equation is nonlocal with respect to variable
u
and
nonlinear with respect to density
\rho.
This is the analog of the Kushner-Stratonovich equation for the filtering problem defined by (
206
), since the unconditioned variable
u
has trivial dynamics and since we are studying the case where the data
{z}^{\dagger}(t)=t{w}^{\dagger}
has zero quadratic variation
and is in fact differentiable. We may then show the following:

Theorem 5.3
.

Consider the dynamical system (
206
), and
assume that
C_{0}\succ 0
,
\mathsf{\Gamma}\succ 0
,

u_{0}\sim\mathsf{N}(m_{0},C_{0})
, and that
u_{0}
is independent of Brownian motion
B
. Let
\rho(\cdot,t)
denote the probability density function associated with the random variable
u(t)|Z^{\dagger}(t)
evolving according to (
206
),
with data chosen as
z^{\dagger}(t)=t{w}^{\dagger},
for
t\in(0,1)
.
Then the density
\rho(\cdot,t)
satisfies (
207
),
or equivalently for
\Phi
given by (
141
a),

\partial_{t}\rho=-(\Phi-\mathbb{E}\Phi)\rho.

(208)

Furthermore this equation has solution given by the formulae

\displaystyle\rho(u,t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{{\mathscr{Z}}(t)}\exp\bigl(-t\Phi(u)\bigr)\rho_{0}(u),

(209a)

\displaystyle{\mathscr{Z}}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}}\exp\bigl(-t\Phi(u)\bigr)\rho_{0}(u)\mathrm{d}u;

(209b)

in particular
\rho(\cdot,1)
is equal
to
\pi
, the density of the posterior distribution
\mu
.

\Diamond

Proof 5.4
.

Let
\rho_{0}
denote the probability density function of the prior
\mathsf{N}(m_{0},C_{0}).

Recall that
\mu(t)
has density given by
equation (
207
) with initial condition
\rho|_{t=0}=\rho_{0}.

Now note that, recalling definition (
190
a) of
\Phi
,

\displaystyle\left\langle G-\mathbb{E}G,{w}^{\dagger}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\rho-\frac{1}{2}\left\{\left|G\right|^{2}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}-\mathbb{E}\left|G\right|^{2}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\right\}\rho

(210a)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad=\bigl(\langle{w}^{\dagger},G\rangle_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}-\frac{1}{2}|G|_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}^{2}\bigr)\rho-\mathbb{E}\bigl(\langle{w}^{\dagger},G\rangle_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}-\frac{1}{2}|G|_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}^{2}\bigr)\rho

(210b)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad=-(\Phi-\mathbb{E}\Phi)\rho.

(210c)

This establishes the equivalence of (
208
) with (
207
) .

Note now that (
209
b) gives

\frac{\mathrm{d}{\mathscr{Z}}}{\mathrm{d}t}=-\mathbb{E}\Phi\,{\mathscr{Z}},

where expectation is under
\rho.

Hence it follows that differentiating (
209
a) gives
(
208
).
Since this is equivalent to (
207
) and since (
207
) characterizes
the law of
u(t)|Z^{\dagger}(t)
when
\frac{\mathrm{d}z^{\dagger}}{\mathrm{d}t}={w}^{\dagger}
, the result is proven.

The theorem establishes that the evolution equation
(
207
) can be rewritten in the form (
208
),
from which a gradient structure is apparent. Indeed equation (
208
)
can be written in the abstract form (
199
) with

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\rho)

\displaystyle:=\int\Phi\rho\mathrm{d}u,

(211a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(\rho)^{-1}\psi

\displaystyle:=\rho\,\psi\in T_{\rho}{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}

(211b)

for
\psi\in T_{\rho}{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}^{\ast}
. Note that the metric structure differs from what we have seen in (
205
). In particular we
no longer use the Wasserstein-2 metric:
the metric defined by the choice (
211
b) of
\mathcal{M}(\cdot)
is known as the
Fisher-Rao metric
. The Fisher-Rao metric requires a more careful consideration of the variational derivative of
\mathcal{E}(\rho)
as provided by (
197
). In particular, using

\psi=\frac{\delta\mathcal{E}}{\delta\rho}_{|T_{\rho}{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}^{\ast}}=\Phi-\mathbb{E}[\Phi]\in T_{\rho}{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}^{\ast}

in (
211
b) implies that the integral of the right hand side of (
211
b) over
\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}
is zero so that it is, indeed, an element of the tangent space
T_{\rho}{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}
given by (
193
).

Remark 5.5
.

The gradient flows defined by (
196
) and (
205
) both arose
from considering the evolution equation for the probability density function associated
with an evolution equation for
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}},
the state space, namely equations
(
191
) and (
201
) respectively.
In contrast, while (
208
) describes the evolution of the density
\rho(\cdot,t)
, we
did not derive it directly as the evolution equation for a random variable
u(t)
in state space.
However we may seek to find such an evolution. To this end we postulate a mean field differential equation

\frac{\mathrm{d}u}{\mathrm{d}t}=g(u,\rho).

Here
\rho
is the probability density function associated with the random variable
u
; since
u
is governed by a deterministic ordinary differential equation the randomness in
u
originates from
the initial condition
u(0).
We now ask how to choose
g
so that the evolution equation for the probability density of
u(t)
, started
from a random initialization, evolves according to (
208
).
The evolution of this density will satisfy the associated nonlinear Liouville equation

\partial_{t}u=-\nabla\cdot(\rho g(\cdot,\rho)).

(212)

Equating (
208
) and (
212
), we obtain the condition

\nabla\cdot(\rho g(\cdot,\rho))=\bigr(\Phi-\mathbb{E}(\Phi)\bigl)\rho

(213)

on
g
. Whether or not this equation can be solved for
g
depends on properties of
\Phi
, and hence
G
;
furthermore even if solvable, the solution may not be unique. We note that, writing
g(u,\rho)
as
the gradient of a
\rho-
dependent potential
E
, so that
g(u,\rho)=\nabla_{u}E(u,\rho)
,
renders the identity (
213
) as a nonlinear divergence form elliptic equation for
E.

This elliptic equation is parameterized by probability density function
\rho
and should be viewed as holding everywhere on
\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}
.

We have sought a state space model for
u
which is an ordinary differential equation, albeit of
mean field type. It is also possible to seek stochastic evolution equations, such a birth-death
processes or mean field stochastic differential equations.
\blacksquare

5.4.2
Algorithms: Gaussian Projected Filter

To further elucidate the structure of Gaussian projected filtering
for the inverse problem, we study its continuous time formulation
from Subsection
3.4
when applied
to the specific state-observation model (
206
).
To this end first recall the discrete time model (
159
) which has continuous time
limit (
206
),
Taking the limit
\Delta t\to 0
in (
161
), the Gaussian
projected filter for (
159
),
we obtain the following evolution equations for mean and covariance:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}m}{\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle=C^{uG}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}\bigl({w}^{\dagger}-\mathbb{E}G(u)\bigr),

(214a)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}C}{\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle=-C^{uG}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}(C^{uG})^{\top},

(214b)

\displaystyle C^{uG}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\bigl(u-\mathbb{E}u\bigr)\otimes\bigl(G(u)-\mathbb{E}G(u)\bigr)\Bigr).

(214c)

Here all expectations are computed under

u(t)\sim\mathsf{N}\bigl(m(t),C(t)\bigr)
.
This is the continuous time Gaussian projected filter for the inverse problem (
140
).

As in the discrete time case, the evolution for the mean promotes a Gaussian
which is compatible with the data, through an innovation term which is weighted by covariance information. We
now illustrate the filter by considering equations (
214
)
in the setting of linear
G
, when they may be solved exactly.

Example 5.6
.

Consider the setting of Example
4.4

in which
G(\cdot)=L\cdot
. The Gaussian projected filter (
214
) becomes

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}m}{\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle=CL^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}({w}^{\dagger}-Lm),

(215a)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}C}{\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle=-CL^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}LC.

(215b)

Note that this coincides with the Kalman-Bucy filter (
113
) for the specific filtering problem defined by (
206
