) and with observed data
\frac{\mathrm{d}z^{\dagger}}{\mathrm{d}t}={w}^{\dagger}.

Now note that (
207
), the Kushner-Stratonovich equation, is solved
by the Kalman-Bucy filter in the linear setting where
G(u)=Lu
. It follows
that the solution
\rho
is given by the Gaussian
\mathsf{N}\bigl(m(t),C(t)\bigr)
where
m(t),C(t)
solve the Gaussian projected filter equations (
215
). In particular the posterior measure
\mu
is Gaussian and given by
\mathsf{N}\bigl(m(1),C(1)\bigr).

To see this explicitly note that, from Theorem
5.3
, and in particular equation (
209
), in the linear case

G(\cdot)=L\cdot
, the solution of (
207
) is given by

\rho(u,t)\propto\exp\Bigl(-\frac{t}{2}|{w}^{\dagger}-Lu|_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}|u-m_{0}|_{C_{0}}^{2}\Bigr).

(216)

Completing the square shows that
this density corresponds to Gaussian
\mathsf{N}\bigl(m(t),C(t)\bigr)
with
mean and covariance satisfying

\displaystyle C(t)^{-1}m(t)

\displaystyle=tL^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}{w}^{\dagger}+C_{0}^{-1}m_{0},

(217a)

\displaystyle C(t)^{-1}

\displaystyle=C_{0}^{-1}+tL^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}L.

(217b)

Note that since
C_{0}\succ 0
it follows that

C_{0}^{-1}\succ 0
and hence (
217
b) shows that, for
all
t\geq 0
,

C(t)^{-1}\succ 0
and hence that
C(t)\succ 0
for
all
t\geq 0
; hence
C(t)
is well-defined by (
217
b)
and
m(t)
is well-defined by (
217
a).
It simply remains to show that
m(t)
and
C(t)
given by
these formulae solve (
215
)
when
m(0)=m_{0}
and
C(0)=C_{0}.

We thus turn our attention to the equations (
215
).
Note that
C(t)
solving (
215
b) satisfies

C(0)=C_{0}\succ 0.
Hence
C(0)^{-1}\succ 0.
Thus, by continuity,
C(t)
remains invertible for some positive interval of time
t\in[0,\tau)
and,
on this interval, direct computation with
(
215
b) shows that

\frac{\mathrm{d}C^{-1}}{\mathrm{d}t}=L^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}L.

(218)

From this it follows by integration
that
C^{-1}(t)\succeq C_{0}^{-1}\succ 0
for
all
t
and hence that we may take
\tau=\infty.
Furthermore, the
integration also shows that the solution of (
218
),
solving (
215
b),
delivers (
217
b) as desired.

We then notice that, from (
215
a),

\displaystyle C^{-1}\frac{\mathrm{d}m}{\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle=L^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}{w}^{\dagger}-L^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}Lm

\displaystyle=L^{T}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}{w}^{\dagger}-\frac{\mathrm{d}C^{-1}}{\mathrm{d}t}m.

It follows that

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\bigl(C^{-1}m)=L^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}{w}^{\dagger}

and integration, together with use of the initial conditions, shows that (
215
a) delivers the desired identity (
217
a).

It is also useful to write (
217
) using an
explicit formula for
C(t)
rather than the precision
C(t)^{-1}.

To this end fix any
t>0
and consider
the Gaussian random variable
(u,w)
defined by choosing
u\sim\mathsf{N}(m_{0},C_{0})

and
w|u=\mathsf{N}(Lu,t^{-1}\mathsf{\Gamma}).
Then the density
\rho
given in
(
216
) is solution of the Bayesian
inverse problem defined by the distribution of
u|w.

The mean and covariance may be found from (
151
) by
replacing
\mathsf{\Gamma}
by
t^{-1}\mathsf{\Gamma}
to yield

\displaystyle m(t)

\displaystyle=m_{0}+C_{0}L^{\top}(LC_{0}L^{\top}+t^{-1}\mathsf{\Gamma})^{-1}({w}^{\dagger}-Lm_{0}),

(219a)

\displaystyle C(t)

\displaystyle=C_{0}-C_{0}L^{\top}(LC_{0}L^{\top}+t^{-1}\mathsf{\Gamma})^{-1}LC_{0}.

(219b)

We also observe that the expression (
219
b) for
C(t)
may
be derived from (
217
b) by use of the Woodbury matrix identity.

\blacksquare

Remark 5.7
.

We obtained (
214
) as the time continuous limit of its discrete-time formulation (
171
). However the same evolution equations can be derived from the gradient flow (
208
) through a sequence of approximations. This perspective is outlined in
Appendix
11
.

\blacksquare

5.4.3
Algorithms: Ensemble Kalman Filter

We now study the inverse problem using Kalman transport from Subsection
4.4.3
, taking the continuous time limit. We consider the specific state-observation model (
206
).
and recall the discrete time model (
159
) which has continuous time
limit (
206
),
Taking the limit
\Delta t\to 0
in (
164
), the ensemble Kalman filter for (
159
),
we obtain the following evolution equations:

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=C^{uG}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}({w}^{\dagger}\,\mathrm{d}t-\mathrm{d}\widehat{z}),

(220a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\widehat{z}

\displaystyle=G(u)\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\mathrm{d}B,

(220b)

\displaystyle C^{uG}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\bigl(u-\mathbb{E}u\bigr)\otimes\bigl(G(u)-\mathbb{E}G(u)\bigr)\Bigr).

(220c)

Here
B\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{w}}
is a unit Brownian motion and expectation
is under the law of
u
itself. As in the discrete time case, the evolution for the state
u
promotes a solution which is compatible with the data, through an innovation term which is weighted by covariance information.

Remark 5.8
.

To obtain the resulting continuous time formulation we may also start
from the continuous time state estimation methodology from Subsection
3.4
and apply it
to the specific state-observation model (
206
).
The SDE (
220
) may then be seen as a consequence of
(
129
) applied to this state-observation model. However, special care is required in deriving the equation this way since the observations
z^{\dagger}
in Section
3
were assumed to have non vanishing quadratic variation; in contrast in this section we have
\mathrm{d}z^{\dagger}/\mathrm{d}t={w}^{\dagger}
, with
{w}^{\dagger}
constant,
and hence zero quadratic variation.

\blacksquare

To obtain further insight into the mean field dynamical system (
220
)
we once again consider the linear setting:

Example 5.9
.

Consider the SDE (
220
) in the setting where

G(u)=Lu
for matrix
L\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{w}\times d_{u}}
.
Then
u(1)\sim\mu
where
\mu
is given in Example
4.4
.
To see this recall that
the Gaussian projected filter is exact in the linear setting,
by Example
5.6
, and hence delivers the desired
posterior at time
t=1
, by Theorem
5.3
.
Thus it suffices to show that the mean and covariance
of
u
from (
220
) satisfy the Gaussian projected filter in the linear setting,
given by (
215
).
We first note that

\mathrm{d}u=CL^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}\bigl({w}^{\dagger}\,\mathrm{d}t-Lu\,\mathrm{d}t-\sqrt{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\mathrm{d}B\bigr),

(221)

where
C
is the covariance of
u
.
By the Itô formula,
m=\mathbb{E}u
satisfies (
215
a).
It follows that
e=u-m
satisfies

\mathrm{d}e=-CL^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}Le\,\mathrm{d}t-CL^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1/2}\mathrm{d}B.

A second use of the Itô formula shows that
C=\mathbb{E}(e\otimes e)
satisfies
(
215
b). The desired result is established.

\blacksquare

We can also derive continuous limits of deterministic transports.
Taking the
\Delta t\to 0
limit in (
166
) results in the mean field ODE formulation

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}u}{\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle=C^{uG}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}\Bigl({w}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\bigl(G(u)+\mathbb{E}G(u)\bigr)\Bigr),

(222a)

\displaystyle C^{uG}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\bigl(u-\mathbb{E}u\bigr)\otimes\bigl(G(u)-\mathbb{E}G(u)\bigr)\Bigr).

(222b)

Again the state evolution has a covariance weighted forcing term which promotes evolution towards the data. As
before we may study this formulation in the linear setting:

Example 5.10
.

Consider the mean field model (
222
) in the setting where

G(u)=Lu
for matrix
L\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{w}\times d_{u}}
.
Then
u(1)\sim\mu
where the posterior distribution
is given in Example
4.4
.
To show this we note that Gaussian projected filter is exact in the linear setting,
by Example
5.6
, and hence delivers the desired
posterior at time
t=1
, by Theorem
5.3
.
Thus it suffices to show that the mean and covariance
of
u
from (
222
) satisfy the Gaussian projected filter in the linear setting; this is given by (
215
).
We first note that the mean under (
222
) satisfies

\frac{\mathrm{d}m}{\mathrm{d}t}=CL^{T}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}({w}^{\dagger}-Lm),

which is (
215
a). Using this it also follows that
e=u-m
satisfies

\frac{\mathrm{d}e}{\mathrm{d}t}=-\frac{1}{2}CL^{T}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}Le,

from which it follows that the variance satisfies (
215
b).

\blacksquare

Remark 5.11
.

Note that the nonlinear Liouville equation associated with the
mean field model (
222
) has the form

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot(\rho g_{\rm KF}),

(223a)

\displaystyle g_{\rm KF}(u,\rho)

\displaystyle=C^{uG}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}\Bigl({w}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\bigl(G(u)+\mathbb{E}G(u)\bigr)\Bigr).

(223b)

This evolution equation
approximates
the evolution of the filtering distribution, except in the linear Gaussian setting when it is exact. On the other hand, as in Remark
5.5
,
we may seek a mean field differential equation of the form

\frac{\mathrm{d}u}{\mathrm{d}t}=g(u,\rho)

(224)

which
exactly
replicates the filtering distribution in
general. To do this requires that we choose
g
to solve (
213
):

\nabla\cdot(\rho g(\cdot,\rho))=\bigr(\Phi-\mathbb{E}(\Phi)\bigl)\rho.

In the linear Gaussian setting we can identify a solution of
this equation by asking that (
224
)
replicates (
222
) since we know the latter is exact in the linear
and Gaussian setting.

In order to derive this result, we note that (
223
b) takes
the form

g_{\rm KF}(u,\rho)=CL^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}\bigl({w}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}L(u+m)\bigr)

(225)

in the linear setting and
\rho
is Gaussian with mean
m
and covariance
C
.
Hence the right hand side of (
223
a) can now be evaluated explicitly giving rise to

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot(\rho g_{\rm KF})

\displaystyle=-\rho(u-m)^{\top}C^{-1}CL^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}\bigl({w}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}L(u+m)\bigr)+c_{1}\rho

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}|Lu-{w}^{\dagger}|_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}^{2}\rho+c_{2}\rho

with normalization constants

c_{1}=-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\left((u-m)^{\top}L^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}L(u+m)\right)

and

c_{2}=-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\left(|Lu-{w}^{\dagger}|_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}^{2}\right).

Hence we have shown that
g_{\rm KF}
satisfies (
213
) for
\Phi(u)=\frac{1}{2}|Lu-{w}^{\dagger}|_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}^{2}
in the linear Gaussian setting.
See the bibliography Subsection
5.6
for more details.

\blacksquare

5.5
Infinite-Time Algorithms

We now develop the ideas in Subsection
4.5

in the continuous time setting. In particular we study
algorithms posed on the infinite time-horizon to solve the optimization problem of minimizing

\Phi_{R}
given by (
190
), or to find the Bayesian posterior
distribution given by (
189
).
In Subsection
5.5.1
we consider this infinite time-horizon perspective
for the solution of optimization problems associated with the inverse problem
(
140
). Subsection
5.5.2
considers the same perspective for Bayesian inversion.

5.5.1
Algorithms for Optimization Formulation

This rather lengthy subsection is broken into
paragraphs concerning
preconditioned gradient flow
,

statistical linearization
,
gradient descent and statistical
linearization
,
algebraic convergence
and
exponential convergence
.
The initial development on preconditioning enables us to introduce

affine invariance
and the discussion of statistical linearization
enables us to connect preconditioned gradient descent with ensemble Kalman
methods. Then, as in Subsection
4.5.2
where similar issues are
discussed in discrete time, we initially discuss algorithms
with algebraic convergence. We then introduce
generalizations which allow us to obtain exponential convergence.

Preconditioned Gradient Flow

We start by generalizing the standard gradient descent introduced in
Subsection
5.2
. Given objective function
\Psi:\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}

and given symmetric positive-definite
preconditioner
B\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}\times d_{u}}
we introduce the equation

\frac{\mathrm{d}u}{\mathrm{d}t}=-B\nabla\Psi(u).

(226)

Note that, along solutions of (
226
),

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Psi(u)

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\nabla\Psi(u),\frac{\mathrm{d}u}{\mathrm{d}t}\Big\rangle

(227a)

\displaystyle=-\Bigl|\frac{\mathrm{d}u}{\mathrm{d}t}\Bigr|_{B}^{2}.

(227b)

Equation (
226
) possesses a gradient flow structure in parameter space
\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}

with respect to a Euclidean metric
weighted by
B
; this weighted metric changes the underlying
geometry of the gradient flow, in comparison to the standard setting of
equation (
191
); but it once again leads to the non-increasing
property of
\Psi(u)
along trajectories.

Consider now the affine transformation
u\mapsto\tilde{u}

given by

\tilde{u}=Au+b,

(228)

where
A
is an invertible matrix and
b
a vector.
An important issue in all vector space optimization problems
is the relative scaling of the components of the vector.
A highly desirable feature of an algorithm is that it be
insensitive to such scaling issues. This can be addressed
by looking at differences between: (i) the algorithm for
u

rewritten in terms of
\tilde{u}
given by (
228
); (ii)
the same algorithm applied directly to variable
\tilde{u}

optimizing
\tilde{\Psi}(\tilde{u})
, with the latter defined by

\tilde{\Psi}(\tilde{u})=\Psi\Bigl(A^{-1}(\tilde{u}-b)\Bigr).

(229)

Definition 5.12
.

When the two ways (i) and (ii) of using reparametrization (
228
)
lead to the same algorithm,
for all choices of
A,b
, we say that the algorithm is

affine invariant
.

\blacksquare

Remark 5.13
.

Affine invariant algorithms are highly
desirable as they are not sensitive to the scaling of
the variables. At the optimum, which is unknown
a priori
, this scaling is
not known and hence cannot be used to improve algorithms. Hence algorithms which are
blind to such scaling are highly desirable.

Applying the transformation (
228
) to (
226
) leads to

\frac{\mathrm{d}\tilde{u}}{\mathrm{d}t}=-AB\nabla\Psi\Bigl(A^{-1}(\tilde{u}-b)\Bigr),

(230)

the descent approach underlying algorithm viewed as in (i).
In contrast, applying the same gradient descent to
\tilde{\Psi}
given by (
229
),
leads to the descent approach underlying algorithms viewed
as in (ii):

\frac{\mathrm{d}\tilde{u}}{\mathrm{d}t}=-BA^{-\top}\nabla\Psi\Bigl(A^{-1}(\tilde{u}-b)\Bigr).

(231)

The two equations (
230
), (
231
) only agree if

AB=BA^{-\top}

and such an identity cannot hold for all
A
, for a fixed
B
.
Thus the basic gradient descent (
226
) is not affine invariant.
However it is a remarkable fact that, by generalizing (
226
)
to allow for mean field dependence, we can achieve affine invariance.

To this end consider the mean field generalization of (
226
)

\frac{\mathrm{d}u}{\mathrm{d}t}=-B(\rho)\nabla\Psi(u),

(232)

where
\rho(\cdot,t)
is the probability density function
associated with the law of
u
, assuming that
u_{0}=u(0)
is drawn at random
from probability density function
\rho_{0}(\cdot).
If we assume
that
B(\cdot)
is positive-definite symmetric for all possible
input densities, then similar arguments to before show
that
\Psi(u)
is non-increasing along trajectories of
(
232
). Furthermore similar arguments to before
show that the algorithm is affine invariant if, for all invertible

A\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}\times d_{u}}
,

AB(\rho)=B(\tilde{\rho})A^{-\top},

where
\tilde{\rho}
is the density of
\tilde{u}
related to
u
, with
density
\rho
, by (
228
). This identity holds for all invertible

A\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}\times d_{u}}
if
B(\cdot)
is chosen to be the covariance associated with its argument. We have thus discovered the affine invariant mean field gradient descent

\frac{\mathrm{d}u}{\mathrm{d}t}=-C\nabla\Psi(u),

(233)

where
C
is the covariance operator under the law of
u(t)

and
u(0)
is chosen at random from probability measure
with density
\rho_{0}.

Algorithms based on solving (
233
) are not themselves
ensemble Kalman methods, although we have drawn inspiration
from the power of mean field methods to motivate the approach.
In the next paragraph we introduce the idea of statistical
linearization, leading to a variety of ensemble Kalman methods
for optimization; and in the paragraph following it we use
this idea to approximate (
233
) by an ensemble Kalman
version of gradient descent which obviates the need for
computing adjoints of the forward model
G(\cdot)
in the setting where
\Psi(\cdot)=\Phi(\cdot)
given by (
190
).

Statistical Linearization

A basic building block in the Gaussian projected filter
and mean field ensemble Kalman models for inverse problems
that we have presented in Subsections
4.4.2
and
4.4.3
, and their
continuous time-analogs in Subsections
5.4.2
and
5.4.3
, is the object

C^{uG}=\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\bigl(u-\mathbb{E}u\bigr)\otimes\bigl(G(u)-\mathbb{E}G(u)\bigr)\Bigr),

(234)

here viewed as evolving in continuous time. Also of interest is the regularized
analog of
C^{uG}
arising when
G_{R}
, as defined in (
142
), is used in place of
G
.
In a methodology based on exact properties only for
first and second order statistics, it is natural that
C^{uG}
should appear
when solving the inverse problem: the correlation between the
parameter
u
, which we wish to estimate, and
G(u)
which we observe, albeit
polluted by additive noise. One way of understanding the role of
C^{uG}
in algorithms
for inversion is through the idea of statistical linearization, providing
a link between ensemble methods and derivatives of the objective
function. The underlying principle is that the differences used in
ensemble methods, and covariances in particular,
act as a surrogate for derivatives.

The expectation defining (
234
) is computed, for the algorithms we
consider, under the distribution of a Gaussian (for the
Gaussian projected filter) or a more general distribution
(for the mean field ensemble Kalman model). To get some insight into the connection
between ensemble methods and derivatives, we first consider
the setting where
u\sim\mathsf{N}\bigl(m,C\bigr).

Note that such
u
can be written as
u=m+\sqrt{C}\xi
where

\xi\sim\mathsf{N}(0,I).
With this assumption on
u
, (
234
) may be reformulated as

C^{uG}=\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\bigl(\sqrt{C}\xi\bigr)\otimes\bigl(G(m+\sqrt{C}\xi)-\mathbb{E}G(m+\sqrt{C}\xi)\bigr)\Bigr),\quad\xi\sim\mathsf{N}(0,I).

(235)

Using this we obtain the following connection
between
C^{uG}
and derivatives of
G:

Lemma 5.14
.

Assume that the second derivative of
G
is small: there
is
\epsilon\ll 1
such that

\sup_{u\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}}|D^{2}G(u)[\zeta,\zeta]|\leq\epsilon|\zeta|^{2}.

Then
C^{uG}
given by (
235
) satisfies

C^{uG}=CDG(m)^{\top}+{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon).

Thus, when
C^{-1}\succ\lambda I
, for some

\lambda>0
independent of
\epsilon
,

DG(m)=(C^{uG})^{\top}C^{-1}+{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon).

(236)

\Diamond

Proof 5.15
.

Note that

\displaystyle G(m+\sqrt{C}\xi)

\displaystyle=G(m)+DG(m)\sqrt{C}\xi+{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}G(m+\sqrt{C}\xi)

\displaystyle=G(m)+{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon).

From (
235
)

C^{uG}=\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\bigl(\sqrt{C}\xi\bigr)\otimes\bigl(DG(m)\sqrt{C}\xi+{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon)\bigr)\Bigr),\quad\xi\sim\mathsf{N}(0,I),

and the desired result follows.

Remark 5.16
.

Another perspective on the preceding lemma is via Stein’s identity.
This states that

C^{uG}=C(\mathbb{E}DG)^{\top}

for (
235
) when expectation is computed under a Gaussian measure
\mathsf{N}(m,C)
.
The identity can be verified via integration by parts.
Given the assumption stated in Lemma
5.14
,

\mathbb{E}DG(u)=DG(m)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)

and the approximation result (
236
) also follows.
\blacksquare

When
D^{2}G
is indeed small it is reasonable to
use (
236
) as the basis for an approximation to
DG(\cdot)

at points in an
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
ball around the mean. We now take
this idea further and consider random variable
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}

(not necessarily Gaussian) and compute
C
and
C^{uG}
as covariance
of
u
and cross-covariance of
u
with
G(u)
respectively.
We refer to use of the approximation

DG(u)\approx(C^{uG})^{\top}C^{-1},\quad\text{for all}\,u\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}},

(237)

as
statistical linearization.

The approximation can be invoked to replace the derivative within
any standard optimization or sampling algorithm to solve the inverse
problem (
140
). Doing so results in a mean field algorithm; that algorithm
in turn can be approximated by particle methods. Statistical linearization allows
the conversion of standard single particle optimization and sampling
algorithms for inverse problems, with dependence on the derivative of
the forward model, into derivative-free interacting particle system
optimizers and samplers. An important application of this methodological
approach is in the development of ensemble Kalman approximations of
Gauss-Newton and Levenberg-Marquadt algorithms; pointers to the literature
will be given in the bibliography Subsection
5.6
.
We now turn to the use of statistical linearization in gradient
descent, perhaps the most basic setting in which it can be used
for optimization.

Gradient Descent and Statistical Linearization

We provide further insight into the statistical linearization approach from the previous subsection by applying it in the context of gradient descent. Recall
\Phi
and
\Phi_{R}
defined in (
190
). We consider the regularized least squares function so that
\Psi(\cdot)=\Phi_{R}(\cdot)
in (
191
); but similar ideas may be developed in the unregularized setting where
\Psi(\cdot)=\Phi(\cdot).
Recall that in the regularized setting

\Phi_{R}(u)=\Phi(u)+\frac{1}{2}|u-m_{0}|_{C_{0}}^{2}.

Note that

\displaystyle\nabla\Phi(u)

\displaystyle=DG(u)^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}\bigl(G(u)-{w}^{\dagger}\big),

(238a)

\displaystyle\nabla\Phi_{R}(u)

\displaystyle=DG(u)^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}\bigl(G(u)-{w}^{\dagger}\big)-C_{0}^{-1}(m_{0}-u).

(238b)

Thus we obtain, from (
191
),

\frac{\mathrm{d}u}{\mathrm{d}t}=-\nabla\Phi_{R}(u)=DG(u)^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}\bigl({w}^{\dagger}-G(u)\big)+C_{0}^{-1}(m_{0}-u).

Similarly, the covariance preconditioned gradient
flow (
233
) becomes

\frac{\mathrm{d}u}{\mathrm{d}t}=-C\nabla\Phi_{R}(u)=CDG(u)^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}\bigl({w}^{\dagger}-G(u)\bigr)+CC_{0}^{-1}(m_{0}-u).

(239)

We now approximate this equation using statistical linearization.

From (
237
) we deduce the equivalent
(assuming
C
is invertible) approximation

CDG(u)^{\top}\approx C^{uG},\quad\text{for all}\,u\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}.

Combining this with (
238
) we obtain

C\nabla\Phi(u)\approx C^{uG}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}\bigl(G(u)-{w}^{\dagger}\bigr).

(240)

Making this approximation in (
239
)
gives the following ensemble Kalman
approximation of mean field gradient descent for
\Phi_{R}
:

\frac{\mathrm{d}u}{\mathrm{d}t}=C^{uG}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}\bigl({w}^{\dagger}-G(u)\bigr)+CC_{0}^{-1}(m_{0}-u).

(241)

Remark 5.17
.

It may be verified that the affine invariance of (
233
) is preserved under the statistical linearization ansatz. To see this,
note that if
G(\cdot)=L\cdot
, then the covariance matrix
C^{uG}=CL^{\top}
so that

C^{uG}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}\bigl({w}^{\dagger}-G(u)\bigr)=CL^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}({w}^{\dagger}-Lu).

From this it follows that, in this linear setting,

\displaystyle C^{uG}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}\bigl({w}^{\dagger}-G(u)\bigr)

\displaystyle=-C\nabla\Phi(u),

\displaystyle\Phi(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}|Lu-{w}^{\dagger}|_{\mathsf{\Gamma}}^{2}.

Hence, (
220
) is also
affine invariant. Similar arguments also show that
(
222
) and (
241
) are affine invariant. In fact, this property holds for all the ensemble Kalman approaches to inverse
problems developed in the previous (discrete time) and current (continuous time) section.
\blacksquare

Algebraic Convergence

We now observe that statistical linearization is exact for linear problems, and provide explicit calculations in this linear case. Recall
G_{R},\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}
defined by (
142
) and assume that
\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}\succ 0
.

Example 5.18
.

Statistical linearization is exact in the linear setting: if
G(u)=Lu
then

DG(u)=(C^{uG})^{\top}C^{-1}.

Thus, in the setting of Example
4.4
, the preconditioned gradient flow
(
233
) with
\Psi=\Phi_{R}
reduces to (
241
) with
G(\cdot)=L\cdot
:

\frac{\mathrm{d}u}{\mathrm{d}t}=CL^{T}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}\bigl({w}^{\dagger}-Lu\bigr)+CC_{0}^{-1}(m_{0}-u).

(242)

This equation leads to the following closed equations for evolution of the mean and covariance:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}m}{\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle=CL^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}({w}^{\dagger}-Lm)+CC_{0}^{-1}(m_{0}-m),

(243a)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}C}{\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle=-2CL^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}LC-CC_{0}^{-1}C=-2CL_{R}^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}^{-1}L_{R}C.

(243b)

It is readily verified that the precision satisfies equation

\frac{\mathrm{d}C^{-1}}{\mathrm{d}t}=2L_{R}^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}^{-1}L_{R}

(244)

and hence the precision grows linearly in time to infinity.
As a consequence the covariance decays to zero at algebraic rate
\mathcal{O}(1/t).

\blacksquare

Exponential Convergence

To obtain exponential convergence we must overcome covariance collapse;
to this end we study the idea of covariance
inflation, introduced in discrete time in (
170
),
in the continuous time setting.
Again recall
G_{R},\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}
defined by (
142
) and assume that

\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}\succ 0
. We then consider the continuous time limit of (
170
)
to obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\beta\Sigma}\mathrm{d}W,

(245a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=G_{R}(u)\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}}\mathrm{d}B,

(245b)

where
W
and
B
are independent unit Brownian motions on
\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}
and
R^{d_{w}}

respectively. To determine
\mathsf{\Sigma}
set
{Z}^{\dagger}(t)=\{{z}^{\dagger}(s)\}_{s\in[0,T]}
and
define
\mathsf{\Sigma}=C
, where
C
is the covariance of random variable
u(t)|{Z}^{\dagger}(t).

When we apply the SDE (
245
)
to solve the inverse problem we take
{z}^{\dagger}(s):=s{w}^{\dagger}_{R}
for all
s\geq 0
.

Remark 5.19
.

As for the discrete-time model (
170
), equation
(
245
) defines an unusual form of mean field
model through dependence on the filtering distribution. As a
consequence, the filtering distribution is determined by a non-standard variant of the Kushner-Stratonovich equation which takes the form

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho=\frac{1}{2}\nabla\cdot\Bigl(\nabla\cdot\bigl(\rho\mathcal{C}(\rho)\bigl)\Bigr)-\frac{1}{2}\left\{\left|G_{R}\right|^{2}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}}-\mathbb{E}\left|G_{R}\right|^{2}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}}\right\}\rho+\left\langle G_{R}-\mathbb{E}G_{R},{w}^{\dagger}_{R}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}}\rho.

This may be derived from (
109
) with
f\equiv 0
,

\mathsf{\Sigma}=\mathcal{C}(\rho)
(the covariance under
\rho
),
h=G_{R}

and
\mathrm{d}{z}^{\dagger}(t):={w}^{\dagger}_{R}\mathrm{d}t.

Note that appearance of the covariance matrix
\mathcal{C}(\rho)

adds a further nonlocal nonlinearity which is not present in the density
evolution (
207
) that arises without covariance inflation.

\blacksquare

We now derive continuous time limits of the Gaussian projected filter
(
171
) and the ensemble Kalman filters
(
173
) and (
174
), derived in Subsection
4.5

for solution of the inverse problem (
140
).
Starting with the Gaussian projected filter and taking the limit
\Delta t\to 0
we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}m}{\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle=C^{uG}_{R}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}^{-1}\bigl({w}^{\dagger}_{R}-\mathbb{E}G_{R}(u)\bigr),

(246a)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}C}{\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle=\beta C-C^{uG}_{R}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}^{-1}(C^{uG}_{R})^{\top},

(246b)

\displaystyle C^{uG}_{R}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\bigl({u}-\mathbb{E}{u}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(G_{R}({u})-\mathbb{E}G_{R}({u})\bigr)\Bigr),

(246c)

where all expectations are with respect to
{u}(t)\sim\mathsf{N}\bigl(m(t),C(t)\bigr)
.
Using the explicit form of
\Phi_{R}
, this set of equations may be shown to be equivalent to

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}m}{\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle=C^{uG}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}\bigl({w}^{\dagger}-\mathbb{E}G(u)\bigr)+CC_{0}^{-1}(m_{0}-m),

(247a)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}C}{\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle=\beta C-C^{uG}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}(C^{uG})^{\top}-CC_{0}^{-1}C.

(247b)

As in discrete time we are using the approximation of the
covariance of the filtering distribution implied by the Gaussian
projected filter; this is since we do not have access to the
exact covariance.

Example 5.20
.

To highlight links with preconditioned gradient descent we now investigate these equations in the linear Gaussian setting. With
G(\cdot)=L\cdot
, we obtain from (
247
) the following equations for the mean and covariance matrix evolution:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}m}{\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle=CL^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}\bigl({w}^{\dagger}-Lm\bigr)+CC_{0}^{-1}(m_{0}-m),

(248a)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}C}{\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle=\beta C-CL^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}^{-1}LC-CC_{0}^{-1}C=\beta C-CL_{R}^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}^{-1}L_{R}C.

(248b)

These evolution equations for mean and covariance are now compared to the corresponding equations derived in Example
5.18
, concerning
statistical linearization. Equations (
243
) in that example arise
from statistical linearization of preconditioned
gradient descent, and exactly recover preconditioned gradient descent
in the linear setting.
We note that the evolution equations (
243
a)
and (
248
a) for the mean agree, while the covariance
matrices
C
defined by (
243
b)
and (
248
b) undergo different evolutions.

\blacksquare

In the proposition which follows we now show that the different
evolution equation for the covariance
(
248
b) leads to exponential convergence; this should be
contrasted with the algebraic convergence resulting
from (
243
b).

Proposition 5.21
.

Assume that
u_{0}
is initialized at a Gaussian

\mathsf{N}(m_{0},C_{0})
and assume also that
C_{0},\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}\succ 0
. Consider the setting where

G_{R}(\cdot)=L_{R}\cdot
for matrix
L_{R}\in\mathbb{R}^{(d_{w}+d_{u})\times d_{u}}
.
Now consider the filtering distribution
u(t)|Z^{\dagger}(t)
defined by (
245
) for
\beta>0
, with data
Z^{\dagger}(t)
defined by
z^{\dagger}(s)=s{w}^{\dagger}_{R}
, where
{w}^{\dagger}_{R}
is defined in (
144
). Then the filtering distribution is Gaussian
\mathsf{N}(m(t),C_{(}t))
for all
t\geq 0
. The mean and covariance converge at an exponential rate
\exp(-\beta t)
, as
t\to\infty
, to the limits
m_{\infty}=m_{\rm post}
and

C_{\infty}=\beta C_{\rm post}
, where
(m_{\rm post},C_{\rm post})
are the posterior mean (
150
) and covariance (
149
).

\Diamond

Remark 5.22
.

It is a remarkable fact that the rate of convergence is independent of
the properties of the limiting Gaussian posterior distribution, and in
particular of the conditioning of the posterior covariance. This property is the continuous
time analog of what we observed in Remark
4.19
. The desirable
universal convergence rate property is a result of the affine invariance of the Gaussian projected filter and ensemble Kalman methods studied in this subsection.

\blacksquare

Proof 5.23

(Proof of Proposition
5.21
)
.

We first note that
\beta>0
implies that equation (
248
b) for the covariance has two equilibria: an unstable one at
C=0
and a stable one satisfying

C_{\infty}=\beta\left(L_{R}^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}^{-1}L_{R}\right)^{-1}=\beta C_{\rm post}.

(249)

The exponential convergence to
C_{\infty}
for
\beta>0
can be best seen by considering the evolution for the precision matrix

C^{-1}
:

\frac{\mathrm{d}C^{-1}}{\mathrm{d}t}=-\beta C^{-1}+L_{R}^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}^{-1}L_{R}.

(250)

Direct calculation of the time-derivative of
C^{-1}(t)m(t)
shows that

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}(C^{-1}m)=-\beta C^{-1}m+L_{R}^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}^{-1}{w}^{\dagger}_{R}.

Thus
C^{-1}(t)m(t)
converges exponentially fast to limit

\frac{1}{\beta}L_{R}^{\top}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}^{-1}{w}^{\dagger}_{R}.
Since
C(t)
itself
converges exponentially fast to
\beta C_{\rm post}
it follows that
m(t)
converges
exponentially fast to posterior mean
m_{\rm post}
given by (
150
).

Remark 5.24
.

For
\beta=0
, we find algebraic convergence which we also found in equation (
244
), from Example
5.18
, for the preconditioned gradient descent formulation. It should be noted that (
244
) contains a pre-factor of two which is not present in
(
250
) so, even when
\beta=0
, the evolution for mean
and covariance does not coincide with that arising from preconditioned gradient descent.

\blacksquare

We now turn to ensemble Kalman methods, starting with the stochastic version.
We take continuous time limits in (
173
) to obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\beta C}\mathrm{d}W+C^{uG}_{R}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}^{-1}\bigl({w}^{\dagger}_{R}\mathrm{d}t-\mathrm{d}\widehat{z}\bigr),

(251a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\widehat{z}

\displaystyle=G_{R}(u)\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}}\mathrm{d}B,

(251b)

where
C^{uG}_{R}
is computed using (
246
c), and
C
is
the regular covariance, both under the law of
u.

As in discrete time we are using the approximation of the
covariance of the filtering distribution implied by the Gaussian
projected filter; this is since we do not have access to the
exact covariance.
Here
W
and
B
are independent unit Brownian motions on
\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}
and
R^{d_{w}}

respectively. The corresponding deterministic transport formulation is found by taking the continuous time limit in (
174
) to obtain

\mathrm{d}u=\sqrt{\beta C}\mathrm{d}W+C^{uG}_{R}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}^{-1}\Bigl({w}^{\dagger}_{R}-\frac{1}{2}\bigl(G_{R}(u)-\mathbb{E}G_{R}(u)\bigr)\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t,

(252)

where
W
is again a unit Brownian motion on
\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}

and where
C^{uG}_{R}
is computed using (
246
c), and
C
is
the regular covariance, both under the law of
u.

Remark 5.25
.

We note that it is possible to replace (
245
) by the
filtering problem associated with the model

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\beta(u-\mathbb{E}u)\mathrm{d}t,

(253a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=G_{R}(u)\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}}\mathrm{d}B.

(253b)

This results in the same Gaussian projected filter and ensemble Kalman
methods as before, in the linear Gaussian setting.
We note that using the filtering problem associated with
(
253
) leads to analogous ensemble Kalman methods to (
252
) or (
251
); these can be obtained by directly replacing the term
\sqrt{\beta C}\mathrm{d}W
with
\frac{1}{2}\beta(u-\mathbb{E}u)\mathrm{d}t
in (
252
) and (
251
).

\blacksquare

5.5.2
Algorithms for Bayesian Formulation

In Subsection
4.5.3
we derived Gaussian projected filter
and ensemble Kalman methods that converge exponentially fast to
the exact posterior distribution of the inverse problem (
140
) in
the linear, Gaussian setting. This is achieved by choosing

\beta=(1-\Delta t)^{-1}
as stated in (
182
). A similar argument
in the continuous time setting thus leads to the choice
\beta=1.
We have the
following corollary of Proposition
5.21
:

Corollary 5.26
.

Assume that
u_{0}
is initialized at a Gaussian

\mathsf{N}(m_{0},C_{0})
and assume also that
C_{0},\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}\succ 0
. Consider the setting where

G_{R}(\cdot)=L_{R}\cdot
for matrix
L_{R}\in\mathbb{R}^{(d_{w}+d_{u})\times d_{u}}
.
Now consider the filtering distribution
u(t)|Z^{\dagger}(t)
defined by (
245
) for
\beta=1
, with data
Z^{\dagger}(t)
defined by
z^{\dagger}(s)=s{w}^{\dagger}_{R}
, where
{w}^{\dagger}_{R}
is defined in (
144
). Then the filtering distribution is Gaussian
\mathsf{N}(m(t),C(t))
for all
t\geq 0
. The mean and covariance converge at an exponential rate, as
t\to\infty
, to the limits
m_{\infty}=m_{\rm post}
and

C_{\infty}=C_{\rm post}
, where
(m_{\rm post},C_{\rm post})
are the posterior mean (
150
) and covariance (
149
).

\Diamond

Preconditioned Gradient Flows

While the above extension to Bayesian inference problems is straightforward and leads to an exact recovery of the posterior distribution in the linear Gaussian setting, the resulting methods deliver only approximations in the general nonlinear setting. This, of course, is a theme throughout this article.
In the remainder of this subsection we take a different approach to deriving algorithms for sampling which are exact in the linear Gaussian setting. We start by considering preconditioned gradient descent, and its Langevin analog; we then note that application of statistical linearization gives approximations of these evolutions which are exact in the linear Gaussian setting, and hence also exhibit desirable convergence properties, in the linear Gaussian
setting.

First recall the preconditioned gradient descent
(
233
) for
\Psi=\Phi_{R}
resulting in

\frac{\mathrm{d}u}{\mathrm{d}t}=-C\nabla\Phi_{R}(u).

(254)

This methodology for optimization may be extended to a sampling methodology
by considering the preconditioned mean field Langevin SDE defined by

\mathrm{d}u=-C\nabla\Phi_{R}(u)\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2C}\mathrm{d}W.

(255)

Remark 5.27
.

We note here that it may be verified that (
255
) is invariant under affine transformations of type (
228
).
Thus (
255
) provides an attractive generalization of standard Langevin dynamics (
201
) for sampling from the posterior distribution, because of the
properties of affine invariant algorithms highlighted in Remark
5.13
. Although the desired posterior distribution is approached only in the limit
t\to\infty
, the fact that the convergence is exponential, with universal rate across all linear
Gaussian inverse problems, makes the approach potentially competitive. Theory concerning this equation is discussed in the bibliography Subsection
5.6
.

Use of the statistical linearization approximation (
237
), which we discuss next, converts both (
254
) and (
255
) into mean field ensemble Kalman methods which, through particle approximations, lead to implementable derivative-free methods.

\blacksquare

Inexact Gradients

It is possible to apply statistical linearization
(
240
) to the preconditioned Langevin equation (
255
),
resulting in the evolution equation

\mathrm{d}u=C^{uG}_{R}\mathsf{\Gamma}_{R}^{-1}\bigl({w}^{\dagger}_{R}-G_{R}(u)\bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2C}\mathrm{d}W.

(256)

Here
C^{uG}_{R}
is computed using (
246
c), and
C
is
the regular covariance, both under the law of
u.
Like (
251
)
and (
252
) this equation converges exponentially fast to the posterior
distribution in the linear Gaussian case. However the form of the mean field
stochastic differential equation is fundamentally different: here the Brownian
noise arises in state space
\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}
, whereas in the ensemble Kalman methods
it appears in data space
\mathbb{R}^{d_{y}}.

The Kalman-Wasserstein gradient flow structure for the
Liouville and Fokker-Planck equations considered here
is not maintained under statistical linearization: they
are not of gradient descent type in
{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}
, except in the
case where
G(u)
is linear.

Exact Gradients

This paragraph concerns analysis of (
254
) and (
255
) when the exact gradients of
\Phi
, and hence
\Phi_{R}
, are available: we consider the geometric properties of (
254
). We assume
that
u(t)
has smooth probability density
\rho(u,t)
for
all
t\geq 0
and recall that we denote the manifold of all smooth probability
density functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}
by
{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}
. Then
\rho
satisfies the Liouville equation

\partial_{t}\rho=\nabla\cdot(\rho C\nabla\Phi_{R}).

(257)

Again the appearance of the covariance matrix
C=\mathcal{C}(\rho)
renders (
257
) a nonlinear and nonlocal partial differential equation on
{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}
.
We will show that the evolution of
\rho
on
{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}
has gradient flow structure of the form given in (
199
).

In order to see this gradient structure we need to identify
the energy functional which is being minimized and then introduce a metric structure in which (
257
) is a gradient flow. The relevant energy functional

\mathcal{E}(\rho)
on
{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}
is simply

\mathcal{E}(\rho)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}}\Phi_{R}(u)\rho(u)\mathrm{d}u,

namely the expected value of
\Psi(u).
Note that the Fréchet derivative
30
30

30

Identified by writing
\mathcal{E}(\rho+\sigma)-\mathcal{E}(\rho)
as a linear operator acting on
\sigma\in T_{\rho}{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}
plus higher order terms.
of
\mathcal{E}
is given by

\frac{\delta\mathcal{E}}{\delta\rho}=\Phi_{R}.

Hence we can rewrite (
257
) as

\partial_{t}\rho=\nabla\cdot\Bigl(\rho\mathcal{C}(\rho)\nabla\frac{\delta\mathcal{E}}{\delta\rho}\Bigr).

(258)

Recalling the tangent space
T_{\rho}{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}
to
{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}
, at
\rho\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}
,
the appropriate choice of Riemannian metric tensor

g_{\rho,\mathcal{C}}\,:\,T_{\rho}{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}\times T_{\rho}{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}

for (
257
) is then

g_{\rho,\mathcal{C}}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}}\left\langle\nabla\psi_{1}\,,\,\mathcal{C}(\rho)\nabla\psi_{2}\right\rangle\,\rho\,\mathrm{d}u,

where
\sigma_{i}=-\nabla\cdot\left(\rho\mathcal{C}(\rho)\nabla\psi_{i}\right)\in T_{\rho}\mathfrak{P}_{+}
for
i=1,2
.
31
31

31

A precise mathematical treatment requires further assumptions on the considered set
{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}
of probability functions ensuring that the required potentials
\psi
indeed exists for all
\sigma\in T_{\rho}{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}
. See the bibliography for references and discussion of open questions in this area.
We refer to the
Kalman-Wasserstein metric
as the metric induced by this metric tensor, generalizing the Wasserstein-2 metric introduced in Subsection
5.3
.

The equation (
258
) takes gradient flow structure in
(\mathfrak{P}_{+},g_{\rho,\mathcal{C}})
:

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathcal{E}(\rho)=-g_{\rho,\mathcal{C}}(\partial_{t}\rho,\partial_{t}\rho).

(259)

To establish (
259
) note that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathcal{E}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}}\frac{\delta\mathcal{E}}{\delta\rho}\partial_{t}\rho\,\mathrm{d}u\,

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}}\rho\,\Bigl|\mathcal{C}(\rho)^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla\frac{\delta\mathcal{E}}{\delta\rho}\Bigr|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}u\,

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}}\left\langle\nabla\frac{\delta\mathcal{E}}{\delta\rho}\,,\,\mathcal{C}(\rho)\nabla\frac{\delta\mathcal{E}}{\delta\rho}\right\rangle\,\rho\,\mathrm{d}u\,

\displaystyle=-g_{\rho,\mathcal{C}}(\sigma,\sigma)\,,

where

\sigma=-\nabla\cdot\left(\rho\mathcal{C}(\rho)\nabla\frac{\delta\mathcal{E}}{\delta\rho}\right)=-\partial_{t}\rho.

Thus the gradient flow identity (
259
)
is established. It is interesting to compare the
gradient structure (
259
)
on
{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}
to the gradient flow structure on
\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}
defined by
(
226
) with
B=\mathcal{C}(\rho).
The state space
gradient flow on
\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}
ensures decrease of
\Phi_{R}\bigl(u(t)\bigr)

along trajectories whilst the probability space gradient flow on

{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}
ensures decrease of the expected value of
\Phi_{R}\bigl(u(t)\bigr)

across a distribution of trajectories found from random initialization of the state space problem.

The preceding calculations demonstrates that
any
evolution equation of
type (
258
) with appropriate energy functional
\mathcal{E}
induces a gradient flow on
{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}.
In particular, using the energy functional

\mathcal{E}(\rho)=\int\left(\Phi_{R}+\ln\rho\right)\rho\,\mathrm{d}u

defined in (
203
), we observe that
the associated evolution equation (
258
) becomes

\partial_{t}\rho=\nabla\cdot(\rho\mathcal{C}(\rho)\nabla\Phi_{R})+\nabla\cdot(\mathcal{C}(\rho)\nabla\rho).

(260)

This nonlinear and
nonlocal Fokker-Planck equation governs evolution of the probability
density funtion for the mean field SDE (
255
).
Now recall Remark
5.2
in which we note that
\mathrm{KL}[\rho\|\pi]
and
\mathcal{E}(\rho)

differ by a constant, and where
\pi
is the posterior density associated with posterior measure
\mu
given by (
146
). Thus the global minimizer of the gradient flow associated with (
203
) is attained at
\rho=\pi
and hence solves the Bayesian inverse problem.

Finally, it is also useful to see the nonlinear and nonlocal
Fokker-Planck equation (
260
)
written in the abstract gradient form (
199
). In this case we
may choose

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\rho)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{E}(\rho)=\int\left(\Phi_{R}+\ln\rho\right)\rho\,\mathrm{d}u,

(261a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(\rho)^{-1}\psi

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\nabla\cdot(\rho\mathcal{C}(\rho)\nabla\psi)\in T_{\rho}{\mathfrak{P}}_{+}.

(261b)

5.6
Bibliographical Notes

The notion of gradient flow plays a central role in this
paper. The subject is enormous and we cannot do justice to it
here. We point the reader to the text
Hirsch et al. 2013
for the study of gradient flows
in Euclidean space and to the text
Ambrosio et al. 2008

for gradient flows in metric spaces, including spaces
of probability measures. The paper
Chen et al. 2023

contains an overview of gradient flows for probability measures,
focused on applications to Bayesian inversion.

Continuous time limits of ensemble Kalman filters were first derived in
Bergemann and Reich 2010a
and
Bergemann and Reich 2010b
and further explored in the context of continuous time transport in
Reich 2011
. A connection between
the non-stochastic Kalman transport equations (
222
) and preconditioned gradient descent was first identified in
Bergemann and Reich 2010b
for finite ensemble sizes and in
Reich and Cotter 2015
for the mean field limit. The paper
Pidstrigach and Reich 2023
adopts the time continuous setting.
See also
Yang et al. 2014
for related formulations based on the feedback particle filter approach to continuous time filtering.

The papers
Schillings and Stuart 2017
and

Schillings and Stuart 2018
studied the use of ensemble Kalman methods for optimization problems,
taking a continuous time limit, making a connection to preconditioned gradient descent and exploiting an invariant subspace property (see, for example,
Iglesias et al. 2013
[Theorem 2.1]) inherent in the basic form of the ensemble Kalman methodology.
This led to work on approximate sampling from the preconditioned Langevin equation in

Garbuno-Inigo et al. 2020a
,

Garbuno-Inigo et al. 2020b
,

Nüsken and Reich 2019
and

Liu et al. 2022
; in particular the papers
Nüsken and Reich 2019
,
Garbuno-Inigo et al. 2020b
demonstrated a finite ensemble size correction to the mean field limit introduced in
Garbuno-Inigo et al. 2020a
.
The paper
Garbuno-Inigo et al. 2020a
used the
non-standard Kalman-Wasserstein metric, first introduced in
Reich and Cotter 2015
, to provide a framework to analyze the preconditioned Langevin equation. It remains open to fully develop the mathematical foundations of gradient flows using this metric. These papers demonstrate the role that the ensemble plays in preconditioning the dynamics. This makes a link to the important paper
Goodman and Weare 2010
which introduced the concept of affine invariant ensemble samplers, an idea developed further in
Leimkuhler et al. 2018
.

The paper
Chada et al. 2021
overviews the optimization perspective and provides a unifying framework, going beyond gradient descent-based methods. In particular, framing ensemble Kalman based optimization methods in terms of statistical linearization, as we do in Subsection
5.5.1
, originates in that paper.
The papers
Reich and Weissmann 2021
and
Pavliotis et al. 2022
show how to construct a derivative free Langevin sampler using localized ensembles; the use of localized ensembles to
train neural networks may be found in
Haber et al. 2018
. An alternative interacting particle system approach to solving inverse problems and optimization tasks is the use of consensus based methods

(
Tsianos et al. 2012
,
Ha et al. 2021
,
Fornasier et al. 2021
,
Carrillo et al. 2018
,
Carrillo et al. 2022a
,
Chen et al. 2022a
,
Pinnau et al. 2017
,
Fornasier et al. 2020
)
.
The papers
Ding et al. 2021
,
Ding and Li 2021a
,
Ding and Li 2021b

undertake a systematic analysis of the link between interacting particle systems and mean field systems, mostly focused on the solution of inverse problems; however the methods developed are more widely applicable.

Recall that (
214
)
is a derivative-free approach to approximately solving the Bayesian inverse problem by application of the Gaussian projected filter.
In appendix Section
11
we show that these equations may
may also be derived from the Fisher-Rao gradient flow (
208
), deriving equations for the mean and covariance
evolution from it, and then by invoking a number of approximations.
In appendix Section
11
, as a step in this derivation, we
obtain the equations

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}m}{\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}\bigl(\Phi(u)(u-m)\bigr),

(262a)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}C}{\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}\bigl(\Phi(u)(u-m)(u-m)^{\top}\bigr)+C\mathbb{E}\bigl(\Phi(u)\bigr),

(262b)

which may be viewed
as a closed evolution when expectation is computed under the Gaussian defined by
(m,C).
These closed equations also define a
derivative-free approach to approximate the Bayesian inverse problem, different from (
214
). It is natural to ask which of (
262
) and (
214
) is preferable; computational
experiments underpinning the work in
Chen et al. 2023
indicate that (
214
) is more robust
in various settings.
Discussion of the Fisher-Rao gradient flow projected into the manifold of Gaussians is contained in
Chen et al. 2023
.

Theory showing that the Fokker-Planck equation (
202
) arises as a continuous time limit of the MCMC method (
152
) was initiated in
Gelman et al. 1997
, for the random walk Metropolis algorithm, and followed up for the Metropolis adjusted Langevin equation in
Roberts and Rosenthal 1998
. See
Roberts and Rosenthal 2001
for an overview of this field.
Most of the analysis is done at the level of weak convergence
of sample paths and hence works directly with (
201
),
rather than with its density, which is governed by (
202
).

6
Conclusions and Open Problems

This paper presents a unifying perspective on the
derivation, interpretation and analysis of ensemble
Kalman methods through use of the ideas of mean
field models, second order approximate transport
and particle approximation. Both state estimation
and parameter estimation (inverse problems)
are studied; similar ideas may be developed for joint parameter-state estimation problems but are not discussed in this paper. The ideas have been presented in discrete time and, through specific parametric scalings, continuous time limits have been identified. Our unifying approach constitutes a novel presentation of the subject, and creates a framework for the mathematical development
of the subject area.

Ensemble Kalman methods have been enormously impactful
in the geosciences, where they originated, and are starting
to be used in numerous other application domains.
However, if they are to realize their potential for
widespread adoption and application, many research
challenges remain. These challenges are both in
mathematical analysis and in the development of methodology.
One of the biggest challenges is the following: some theory,
and abundant numerical evidence, show that ensemble Kalman
methods perform well at state estimation and at parameter
estimation; however, there is very little theory, or empirical evidence, which identifies situations in which the statistical
information in the ensemble constitutes valid approximate Bayesian inference. The recent papers
Carrillo et al. 2022b
and
Calvello et al. 2024
make first steps
in this direction. In mathematical analysis a number of other substantial challenges
are presented by ensemble Kalman methods, which we list here.

•

For state estimation, determine conditions, related to small noise scenarios,
under which the true state is
well-approximated by the mean or sample path of mean field
models based on second order transport; find sharp error estimates.

•

For state estimation, determine conditions under which the filtering distribution is well-approximated by mean field models based on second order transport; find sharp error estimates and appropriate metrics for
the analysis; furthermore, identify which problems satisfy these conditions.

•

For inverse problems, determine conditions under which the optimizer of a (to-be-identified) loss function is well-approximated by the mean or sample path of mean field models based on second order transport, in both the transport and iterative approaches to inversion; find sharp error estimates.

•

For inverse problems, determine conditions under which the Bayesian posterior is well-approximated by mean field models based on second order transport, in both the transport and iterative approaches to inversion; find sharp error estimates and appropriate metrics for the analysis; furthermore, identify which inverse problems satisfy these conditions.

•

For all of the preceding four scenarios derive error bounds for
particle approximations of the mean field models; when low-rank
structure is present in covariances prove that ensemble Kalman methods
can correctly identify it, and exploit the low-rank structure in the
analysis of particle approximations.

•

In all of the particle methods arising above, compare the
cost/error trade-off with that arising for other methods,
to determine when ensemble Kalman methods are competitive.

•

All of the algorithms in this paper are studied in
idealized scenarios, in the absence of
widely employed techniques such as covariance inflation and localization;
developing analyses which account for
covariance inflation and localization will be highly desirable.

On the methodology side there are also a number of significant
challenges, which we also list here.

•

Given ability to compute an ensemble of evaluations of the combined state-observation dynamical system, determine the optimal (in terms of cost/error trade-off) way to combine the ensemble to either estimate the state given an observation sequence, or the filtering distribution.

•

Given ability to compute an ensemble of evaluations of the forward model, what is the optimal way to combine them to either
estimate the parameter given an observation, or the posterior distribution, for the corresponding inverse problem.

•

What role might be played by machine learning in addressing
the design of algorithms, and in particular in addressing the
preceding questions.

•

Develop an overarching interacting particle and mean field framework that subsumes ensemble Kalman and alternative particle filters as well as derivative-free and consensus based optimization methods and use the framework to create new methods.

•

Develop principles for the deployment of covariance inflation,
and generalizations of localization, so that the resulting
methodology is widely applicable and does not need application-specific
principles to be applied. Different ideas may be needed in the inverse problem setting.

•

Expand the preceding scenarios beyond the additive error models
discussed in the survey, to include classification and other machine
learning tasks.
